Compound Logics for Modification Problems , Thanks: The first two authors where supported by the Research Council of Norway via the project BWCA (314528). The last three authors where supported by the ANR projects DEMOGRAPH (ANR-16-CE40-0028), ESIGMA (ANR-17-CE23-0010), and the French-German Collaboration ANR/DFG Project UTMA (ANR-20-CE92-0027). The third author was also supported by the ANR project ELIT (ANR-20-CE48-0008-01). Thanks: Emails: fedor.fomin@uib.no , petr.golovach@uib.no , ignasi.sau@lirmm.fr , giannos.stamoulis@lirmm.fr , sedthilk@thilikos.info .
Fedor V. Fomin, Petr A. Golovach, Ignasi Sau, Giannos Stamoulis, Dimitrios M. Thilikos

Abstract
We introduce a novel model-theoretic framework inspired from graph modification and based on the interplay between model theory and algorithmic graph minors. The core of our framework is a new compound logic operating with two types of sentences, expressing graph modification: the modulator sentence , defining some property of the modified part of the graph, and the target sentence , defining some property of the resulting graph. In our framework, modulator sentences are in counting monadic second-order logic ( CMSOL ) and have models of bounded treewidth, while target sentences express first-order logic ( FOL ) properties along with minor-exclusion. Our logic captures problems that are not definable in first-order logic and, moreover, may have instances of unbounded treewidth. Also, it permits the modeling of wide families of problems involving vertex/edge removals, alternative modulator measures (such as elimination distance or 𝒢 \color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G} -treewidth), multistage modifications, and various cut problems. Our main result is that, for this compound logic, model-checking can be done in quadratic time. All derived algorithms are constructive and this, as a byproduct, extends the constructibility horizon of the algorithmic applications of the Graph Minors theorem of Robertson and Seymour. The proposed logic can be seen as a general framework to capitalize on the potential of the irrelevant vertex technique . It gives a way to deal with problem instances of unbounded treewidth, for which Courcelle’s theorem does not apply. The proof of our meta-theorem combines novel combinatorial results related to the Flat Wall theorem along with elements of the proof of Courcelle’s theorem and Gaifman’s theorem. We finally prove extensions where the target property is expressible in FOL​+DP , i.e., the enhancement of FOL with disjoint-paths predicates. Our algorithmic meta-theorems encompass, unify, and extend all known meta-algorithmic results on minor-closed graph classes.



Keywords:
​ Algorithmic meta-theorems,​ Graph modification problems,​ Model-checking,​ Graph minors,​ First-order logic,​ Monadic second-order logic,​ Flat Wall theorem,​ Irrelevant vertex technique.

1
Introduction

Our work is kindled by the current algorithmic advances in graph modification. The core of our approach is a novel model-theoretic framework that is based on the interplay between model theory and algorithmic graph minors.
Departing from this new perspective, we obtain an algorithmic meta-theorems that encompass, unify, and extend all
known meta-algorithmic results on minor-closed graph classes.

1.1
State of the art and our contribution

Modification problems.

A
graph modification problem
asks whether it is possible to apply a series of modifications to a graph in order to transform it to a graph with some desired target property.
Such problems have been the driving force of Parameterized Complexity where parameterization quantifies the concept of “distance from triviality”
[
82
]
and measures the amount of the applied modification. Classically, modification operations may be vertex or edge deletions, edge additions/contractions, or combinations of them like taking a minor. In their generality, such problems are
NP
-complete
[
108
,
138
]
and much research in Parameterized Complexity is on the design of algorithms in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f(k)\cdot n^{{\cal O}(1)},
where the parameter
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
is some measure of the modification operation
[
38
]
.
The target property may express desired structural properties that respond to certain algorithmic or combinatorial demands. A widely studied family of target properties are minor-closed graph classes such as edgeless graphs
[
29
]
, forests
[
28
,
97
]
, bounded treewidth
graphs
[
58
,
95
,
57
]
,
planar graphs
[
87
,
115
,
90
]
, bounded genus graphs
[
98
]
, or, most generally,
minor-excluding graphs
[
128
,
2
,
130
]
. However, other families of target properties have also been considered, such as those that exclude an odd cycle
[
51
]
, a topological minor
[
59
]
, an (induced) subgraph
[
26
,
127
,
40
]
, an immersion
[
69
]
, or an induced minor
[
71
]
.
A broad class of graph modification problems concerns cuts. In a typical cut problem, one wants to find a minimum-size set of edges or vertices
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
in a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
such that in the new graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X,
obtained by deleting
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
some terminal-connectivity conditions are satisfied. For example, the condition can be that a set of specific terminals becomes separated or that at least one connected component in the new graph is of a specific size.
The development of parameterized algorithms for cut problems is a popular trend in parameterized algorithms
[
114
,
22
,
94
,
39
,
96
,
112
,
83
]
.
More involved modification measures of vertex set removals, related to treewidth or treedepth, have been considered very recently
[
24
,
47
,
86
,
25
,
4
]
.

Algorithmic meta-theorems.

A vibrant line of research in Logic and Algorithms is the development of
algorithmic meta-theorems
. According to Grohe and Kreutzer
[
78
]
,
algorithmic meta-theorems state that certain families of algorithmic problems, typically defined by some logical and some combinatorial condition, can be solved “efficiently”, under some suitable definition of this term.
Algorithmic meta-theorems play an important role in the theory of algorithms as they reveal deep interplays between Algorithms, Logic, and Combinatorics. One of the most celebrated meta-theorems is Courcelle’s theorem
asserting that graph properties definable in
CMSOL
(counting monadic second-order logic) are decidable in linear time on graphs of bounded treewidth
[
31
,
33
,
32
]
; see also
[
20
,
6
]
. Another stream of research concerns identifying wide combinatorial structures where model-checking for
FOL
(first-order logic) can be done in polynomial time. This includes graph classes of bounded degree
[
133
]
, graph classes of bounded local treewidth
[
60
]
,
minor-closed graph classes

[
52
]
, graph classes locally excluding a minor
[
41
]
, and more powerful concepts of sparsity, such as having bounded expansion

[
46
,
116
,
121
,
119
,
120
]
, nowhere denseness
[
79
]
, or having bounded twin-width
[
18
]
. (See
[
100
,
77
]
for surveys. Also for results on the combinatorial horizon
of
FOL
and
CMSOL
(and its variants) see
[
79
,
18
,
17
]
and
[
14
,
13
]
respectively.)

Another line of research, already mentioned in
[
77
]
, is to prove algorithmic meta-theorems for extensions of
FOL
of greater expressibility. Two such extensions have been recently presented.
The first one consists in
enhancing
FOL
with predicates that can express
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
-connectivity for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k\geq 1
. This extension of
FOL
, was introduced independently
by Schirrmacher, Siebertz, and Vigny in
[
132
]
(under the name
FOL​+conn
) and
by Bojańczyk in
[
15
]
(under the name
separator logic
).
The second and more expressive extension, also introduced by Schirrmacher, Siebertz, and Vigny in
[
132
]
, is
FOL​+DP
, that enhances
FOL
with predicates expressing the existence of disjoint paths between certain pairs of vertices. For
FOL​+conn
, an algorithmic meta-theorem for model-checking on graphs excluding a topological minor has been very recently given by Pilipczuk, Schirrmacher, Siebertz, Torunczyk, and Vigny
[
124
]
. For the more expressive
FOL​+DP
, an algorithmic meta-theorem for model-checking on graphs excluding a minor has been very recently given by
Golovach, Stamoulis, and Thilikos in
[
73
]
(see
[
74
]
for the full version).

Research on the meta-algorithmics of
FOL
is quite active and has moved to several directions such
as the study of
FOL
-interpretability
[
16
,
123
,
118
,
117
,
122
,
65
]
or the enhancement of
FOL
with counting/numerical predicates
[
103
,
104
,
45
,
80
]
(see also
[
137
,
75
,
48
,
81
]
for other extensions).

In this paper, we initiate an alternative approach consisting in combining the expressive power of
FOL
and

CMSOL
. A typical family of problems where such an approach becomes relevant is the one of modification
problems.
Courcelle’s theorem implies that if the target property
corresponds to a class of bounded treewidth and the modification conditions are definable in

CMSOL
, then such modification problems are fixed-parameter tractable when parameterized by the length of
the sentence and the treewidth of the graph. However, when the target class graph is of unbounded treewidth,
none of the aforementioned algorithmic meta-theorems encompasses broad families of modification problems.
As an illustrative example, consider the
Planarization

problem, which consists in deciding whether at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
vertices can be removed from an input graph to make
it planar (or equivalently, minor-excluding
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{5}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{3,3}
). While this problem is definable in
CMSOL
,
Courcelle’s theorem cannot be applied as we cannot assume that
yes
-instances are of bounded treewidth. On
the other hand, we can easily assume that
yes
-instances minor-exclude
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{k+6}.
However, all known meta-
theorems whose combinatorial condition encompasses the minor-exclusion are about
FOL
, and
FOL
cannot
express the
Planarization
problem. On the positive side, an algorithm in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f(k)\cdot n^{2}
for
Planarization
is an algorithmic consequence of Robertson-Seymour’s theorem
[
126
]

(combined with
[
91
,
125
]
). This automatic implication follows directly
(albeit non-constructively) for a wide family of modification problems whose
yes
-instances are minor-closed.
There is a long line of research in parameterized algorithms towards providing constructive and reasonable
estimations of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f(k)

[
87
,
2
,
115
,
90
,
128
,
130
]
. Note
that Robertson-Seymour’s theorem, besides not being constructive in general, automatically offers results
only for problems whose
yes
-instances are minor-closed.

Our contribution.

We introduce a
compound logic
that models computational problems through the lens of the “modulator vs target” duality of graph modification problems.
Each sentence of this logic is a composition of two types of sentences. The first one, called the
modulator sentence
, models a modification operation, while the second one, called the
target sentence
, models a target property. Informally, our result, in its simplest form, asserts that if some appropriate version of the modulator sentence meets the meta-algorithmic assumptions of Courcelle’s theorem
[
31
]
(i.e.,

CMSOL
-definability and bounded treewidth) and the target sentence meets the meta-algorithmic assumptions of the theorem of Flum and Grohe
[
52
]

(i.e.,
FOL
-definability and minor-exclusion), then model-checking for the composed compound sentence can be done, constructively, in quadratic time.
Our main result (
Subsection 1.2
) can be seen as a “two-dimensional product” of the two aforementioned meta-algorithmic results, contains both of them as special cases, and automatically implies the tractability of wide families of problems that
neither
are
FOL
-definable
nor
have instances of bounded treewidth (see
Section 3
for the meta-algorithmic applications).

1.2
Our results

In this subsection we give formal statements of our results. We need first some definitions.

Preliminaries on graphs.

Most of our graph definitions are compatible with Diestel’s book
[
43
]
.
Given a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cc}(G)
the set of all connected components of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G.

For a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
and a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(G),
the
stellation
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
is the graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf stell}(G,X)
obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G

if, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C\in{\sf cc}(G\setminus X),
we contract all the edges of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
to a single vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{C}.

The
torso
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
is the graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf torso}(G,X)
obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf stell}(G,X)
if, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{C}
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C\in{\sf cc}(G\setminus X),
we add
all edges between neighbors of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{C}

and finally remove all
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{C}
’s from the resulting graph.

Given a family of graphs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal H},
we define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf excl}({\cal H})
as the class of all graphs
minor-excluding the graphs in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal H}
and note that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf excl}({\cal H})
is a minor-closed class
(see
Section 4
for the definition of minor relation, minor closeness, and minor-exclusion).
The
Hadwiger number
of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf hw}(G),
is the minimum
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\in{\sf excl}(\{K_{k}\})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{k}
is the complete graph on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
vertices.
We also use the well-known parameter of
treewidth
of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf tw}(G),
that is defined in
Section 4
.
Given a class of graphs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
we define

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf tw}({\cal G})=\max\{{\bf tw}(G)\mid G\in{\cal G}\}.
We define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf hw}({\cal G})
analogously. We use
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}_{\rm all}
for the set of all graphs.

Preliminaries on logic.

We use
CMSOL
(resp.
FOL
) for the set of sentences in counting monadic second-order logic (resp. first-order logic) – see
Subsection 4.3
for the definitions.
Given some vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
and a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau],

we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\rm Mod}(\textphi)
the set of
all finite
models of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi,
i.e., all structures that are models of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi.

In this introduction, in order to simplify our presentation, all structures that we consider are either graphs or annotated graphs, i.e., pairs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,X)
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
is a graph and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(G).
In the first case
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau=\{{\sf E}\},

and in the second
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau=\{{\sf E},{\sf X}\}.

Given a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\{{\sf E}\}],
we define the
connectivity extension

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi^{({\sf c})}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi
so that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\models\textphi^{({\sf c})}
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\forall C\in{\sf cc}(G),C\models\textphi.

Similarly, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal L}\subseteq\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\{{\sf E}\}],
we define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal L}^{({\sf c})}={\cal L}\cup\{\textphi^{({\sf c})}\mid\textphi\in{\cal L}\}.

Notice that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{\textphi\}^{({\sf c})}=\{\textphi,\textphi^{({\sf c})}\}
.
Also by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf PB}({\cal L})
we denote the set of all positive Boolean combinations (i.e., using only the Boolean connectives
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\lor
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\land
) of sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal L}.

We next define the following sets of sentences:

•

The set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\sf tw}[\{{\sf E},{\sf X}\}]
contains every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\{{\sf E},{\sf X}\}]
for which there exists some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c_{\textbeta}
such that the torsos of
all the models of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta
have treewidth at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c_{\textbeta}.
Formally,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\sf tw}[\{{\sf E},{\sf X}\}]=\{\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\{{\sf E},{\sf X}\}]\mid\exists c_{\textbeta}:{\bf tw}\{{\sf torso}(G,X)\mid(G,X)\models\textphi\}\leq c_{\textbeta}\}.

•

The set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf EM}[\{{\sf E}\}]
is the set of all sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\{{\sf E}\}]
that express the minor-exclusion of a non-empty set of graphs.
Formally,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf EM}[\{{\sf E}\}]=\{\textmu\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\{{\sf E}\}]\mid\exists{\cal H}\subseteq{\cal G}_{\rm all},{\cal H}\neq\emptyset:{\rm Mod}(\textmu)={\sf excl}({\cal H})\}.

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{0}[\{{\sf E}\}]
contains every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\wedge\textmu

where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}[\{{\sf E}\}]
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu\in{{\sf EM}}[\{{\sf E}\}].

For simplicity, we use
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\sf tw},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf EM},
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{0}
as shortcuts for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\sf tw}[\{{\sf E},{\sf X}\}],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf EM}[\{{\sf E}\}],
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{0}[\{{\sf E}\}],
respectively.

Algorithmic meta-theorems.

We are now in position to restate three major meta-algorithmic results that were mentioned in the previous subsection.

Proposition 1

(Courcelle’s
[
31
]
)
.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\sf tw},
there is an algorithm deciding
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\rm Mod}(\textbeta)
in linear time.

Proposition 2

(Robertson and Seymour
[
125
,
126
]
and Kawarabayashi, Kobayashi, and Reed
[
91
]
)
.

For every minor-closed graph class
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
deciding membership in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
can be done in quadratic time.

Proposition 3

(Flum and Grohe
[
52
]
)
.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma\in\textTheta_{0},
there is an algorithm deciding
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\rm Mod}(\textgamma)
in quadratic time.

Some comments are in order. The statements of
Subsection 1.2
and
Subsection 1.2

have been adapted so to
incorporate the combinatorial
demands in the logical condition. While they can both be stated for structures, we state
Subsection 1.2
for annotated graphs and
Subsection 1.2
for graphs in order to facilitate our presentation.
In the classic formulation of Courcelle’s theorem, we are given a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}
and a tree decomposition of bounded treewidth. As such a decomposition can be found in linear time, using e.g.,
[
10
,
11
,
99
]
, the linearity in the running time of Courcelle’s theorem is preserved when it is stated in the form of
Subsection 1.2
.
For the theorem of Flum and Grohe, the situation is different
as the combinatorial demand is minor-exclusion of a clique, which is not definable is
FOL
. For this reason we state
Subsection 1.2
using the logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{0}

that contains
compound
sentences of the form
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\wedge\textmu,
where

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu
expresses minor-exclusion.
For the running time of the algorithm of
Subsection 1.2
, we also need
to take into account
Subsection 1.2
.
As we already mentioned,
Subsection 1.2
and
Subsection 1.2
cannot deal, in general,
with modification problems to properties of unbounded treewidth. Moreover, recall that
Subsection 1.2
applies only to problems whose
yes
-instances are minor-closed.

We stress that

Subsection 1.2
,
Subsection 1.2
, and
Subsection 1.2

are non-constructive. In order to construct the algorithms promised by
Subsection 1.2
,
one should also know the bound
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c_{\textbeta}
on the treewidth of the models of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\sf tw}
.
Similarly, for
Subsection 1.2
(resp.
Subsection 1.2
), one should have an upper bound on the Hadwiger number of the graphs in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
(resp. the models of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma
).

A logic for modification problems.

As a key ingredient of our result, we define the following operation between sentences.
Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\{{\sf E},{\sf X}\}]
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\{{\sf E}\}].

We refer to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta
as the
modulator
sentence on annotated graphs and to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma

as the
target
sentence on graphs.
We define

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma
so that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\text{$\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\models\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma$ if there is $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(G)$ such that $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}({\sf stell}(G,X),X)\models\textbeta$
and $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X\models\textgamma$}.

) that are obtained if in the definition of

While presenting our results and techniques, for the sake of simplicity, we choose to focus on the statement and the proof of our meta-theorems for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
(

).

Constructibility.

While Robertson-Seymour’s theorem (
Subsection 1.2
)
implies the existence of an algorithm, its proof is not constructive and cannot be used to construct such an algorithm
[
49
]
.
An extra feature of the proof of
Subsection 1.2
(as well as of its corollary
Subsection 1.2
) is that it is
constructive
, in the sense that
the implied algorithms can be constructed if we are given some bound on the Hadwiger number of the models of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.
This considerably extends the constructibility horizon of
Subsection 1.2
for graph classes that are not necessarily minor-closed or even hereditary
(see
Section 12
for more on the constructibility of our results).

Techniques.

The algorithm and the proofs of
Subsection 1.2
use
as departure point core techniques

from the proofs of Propositions
1.2
,
1.2
, and
1.2
such as Courcelle’s theorem for dealing with
CMSOL
-sentences, the use of Gaifman’s theorem for dealing with
FOL
-sentences,

and an extended version of the
irrelevant vertex technique
,
introduced
by Robertson and Seymour in
[
125
]
, along with some suitable version of the Flat Wall theorem which appeared recently in
[
129
,
93
]
(see also
[
7
,
128
,
130
,
131
]
). The algorithm produces equivalent
and gradually “strictly simpler” instances of an annotated version of the problem. Each equivalent instance is produced in linear time and this simplification is repeated until the graph has bounded treewidth (here we may apply Courcelle’s theorem, that is
Subsection 1.2
).
This yields a (constructive) quadratic-time algorithm. We stress that our approach avoids
techniques that have been recently used for this type of problems such as
recursive understanding
(in
[
4
]
) or the use of
important separators
(in
[
86
]
) that give worst running times in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}n
. For a more detailed discussion on the results of
[
4
,
86
]
and their relation to our results, see the applications section (
Section 3
).

Organization of the paper.

In
Section 2
we provide an overview of our proof.
In
Section 3
, we discuss some applications of our results
in modification problems.
In
Section 4
we provide some basic definitions that
will be used throughout the paper and in
Section 5
we give the formal definition of our logic.
To describe the algorithm for
Subsection 1.2
,
we first introduce an annotated version of the problem; this is done in
Section 6
.
Then, in
Section 7
, we give some preliminary concepts and results
and, in
Section 8
, we present the general scheme of the algorithm for
Subsection 1.2
.
Sections
9
,
10
, and
11
are devoted to the gradual presentation of the main subroutine of the algorithm of
Subsection 1.2
and its correctness.
Next, in
Section 12
,
we discuss the constructibility of our results and present some consequences of our results concerning the constructibility of Robertson-Seymour’s theorem.
In
Section 13
, we explain how to modify our proofs so that they also work for the more expressive logics
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta^{\sf DP}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\textTheta}^{\sf DP}
.
We conclude the paper with
Section 14
by mentioning the limitations of our approach, possible extensions, and open research directions. In
Appendix A
we present the flat wall framework that we use in this paper, which was introduced in
[
129
]
.
Also, in
Appendix B
, we provide all the details of the omitted proofs of
Section 10
.

2
Overview of the proof

In this section we summarize some of the main ideas involved in the proof of
Subsection 1.2
(stated as
Subsection 5.4
in its full versatility on structures), while keeping the description at an intuitive level. We would like to stress that some of the informal definitions given in this section are deliberately
imprecise
, since providing the precise ones would result in a huge overload of technicalities that would hinder the flow of the proof.

Our algorithms consider as input a general structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}

(not necessarily a graph), and most of the arguments in the proofs concern its Gaifman graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}
(see
Subsection 4.3
for the definition). Dealing with general structures, besides making our results more versatile,
turns out to be useful in the proofs, in particular for using tools such as the
Backwards Translation Theorem

[
35
,
14
]
(see
Subsection 5.2
), or for extending our results to other modification operations beyond vertex removal (see
Subsection 3.2
in
Subsection 3.2
). Since the Gaifman graph of a graph is the graph itself, in this overview we will assume for simplicity that the input of our algorithms is a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
instead of a general structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}.

In
Subsection 2.1
we present the general scheme of the algorithm (see
Section 8
, in particular
Figure 7
, for a more detailed presentation), common to the distinct cases presented in
Section 9
,
Section 10
, and
Section 11
, corresponding to different fragments of our logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta.
In
Subsection 2.2
we present a simplified and illustrative setting, where the input sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
belongs to the fragment
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}_{1}

(see
Section 9
). This (very) particular case of
Subsection 1.2
is helpful to illustrate our main conceptual ideas, and after sketching its proof, in
Subsection 2.3
we discuss how to integrate new technical ingredients, step by step, from this particular case up to the general compound logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
considered in
Subsection 1.2
(see
Section 10
and
Section 11
for the details).

2.1
General scheme of the algorithm

We use the
irrelevant vertex technique
introduced by Robertson and Seymour
[
125
]
. Our overall strategy is the “typical” one when using this technique: if the treewidth of the input graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
is bounded by an appropriately chosen function, depending only on the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta,
then we use Courcelle’s theorem
[
31
,
32
,
33
]
and solve the problem in linear time, using the fact that our compound logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
is a fragment of counting monadic second-order logic (see
Subsection 5.4
). Otherwise, we identify an irrelevant vertex in linear time, that is, a vertex whose removal produces an equivalent instance. Naturally, the latter case concentrates all our efforts and, in what follows, we sketch the main ingredients that we use in order to identify such an irrelevant vertex. In a nutshell, our approach is based on introducing a robust combinatorial framework for finding irrelevant vertices. In fact,
what we find is
annotation-irrelevant flat territories
, building on our previous recent work
[
7
,
129
,
53
,
128
,
130
,
131
,
7
]
,
which is formulated with enough generality so as to allow for the application of powerful tools such as Gaifman’s locality theorem (see
Subsection 4.3
)
or a variant of Courcelle’s theorem on boundaried graphs (see
Subsection 7.1
).

Flat walls.

An essential tool of our approach is the notion of
flat wall
, originating in the work of Robertson and Seymour
[
125
]
.
Informally speaking, a flat wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
is a structure made up of (non-necessarily planar) pieces, called
flaps
, that are glued together in a bidimensional grid-like way defining the so-called
bricks
of the wall (see
Figure 25
).
While such a structure may not be planar, it enjoys topological properties similar to those of planar graphs, in the sense that two paths that are not routed entirely inside a flap cannot “cross”, except at a constant-sized vertex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A
whose vertices are called
apices
. Hence,
flat walls are only “locally non-planar”, and after removing apices we can apply useful locality arguments, in the sense that two vertices that are in “distant” flaps should also be “distant” in the whole graph without the apices.
One of the most celebrated results in the theory of Graph Minors by Robertson and Seymour
[
126
,
125
]
, known as the Flat Wall theorem (see
Subsection A.5
for a variant recently proved in
[
129
,
93
]
), informally states that graphs of large treewidth contain either a large clique minor or a large flat wall. In this article we use the framework recently introduced in
[
129
]
that provides a more accurate view of some previously defined notions concerning flat walls, particularly in
[
93
]
. We provide these precise definitions in

Subsection A.4
, including the concepts of
flatness pair
,
homogeneity
,
regularity
,
tilt
, and
influence
, and we stress that they are
not
critical in order to understand the main technical contributions of the current article (however, they are critical for their formal correctness).
In what follows, when considering a flat wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
with an apex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A
in a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
for simplicity we refer to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
by using indistinguishably the terms “wall” and “compass of a wall”, which can be roughly described as the component containing
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
in the graph obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
by removing
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A
and the “boundary” of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
(see
Subsection A.4
for the formal definition).

Working with an annotated version of the problem.

We start by defining a convenient equivalent version of the problem (see
Section 6
), by replacing our sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta
with an equivalent enhanced sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.
This is done in two steps, presented in
Subsection 6.1
and
Subsection 6.2
.

Assuming the existence of a flat wall and an apex set in our input graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
we first transform (see
Subsection 6.1
) the question
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
to a question on a structure obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
by “neutralizing” the apex set (
Subsection 6.1
).
The goal of this step is to ask the final
FOL
-sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma

of our sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
in a “flattened” structure, where apices can no longer “bring close” any distant parts of the wall. This transformation of the problem, which we call
apex-projection
, will allow for the application of the locality-based strategy discussed in the definition of the
in-signature
of a wall in
Subsection 2.2
.
To do this, we introduce some additional constant symbols
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
to our vocabulary that will be interpreted as the apex vertices.

The second step (
Subsection 6.2
) consists in defining an equivalent
annotated
version of the problem in order to deal with the
FOL
-sentences of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,
inspired by the approach of
[
53
]
. To do so, we introduce a vertex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R\subseteq V(G),
and require, for each
FOL
-sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,
that the vertices interpreting the variables of (the equivalent Gaifman sentence of)
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
belong to the annotated set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R.

We prove that the initial sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
and the obtained sentence, denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
and called an
enhanced sentence
,
are equivalent for
any
choice of the apex set interpreting
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
and when
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}
is interpreted as the whole vertex set of the graph (see
Subsection 6.2
and
Subsection 6.2
). This independence of the choice of the apex set is strongly used in the proofs since, as discussed below, we will consider a number of different flat walls, each of which associated with a different apex set.

Our algorithms will work with the enhanced sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.
Starting with the input graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(G)
as the annotated set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R,
we will create successive equivalent annotated instances, in which vertices from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
are removed and such that the annotated set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R
is only reduced.

Zooming inside a flat wall.

Our next step is to find, in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
a large flat wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{0}
to work with. The definition of our logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
implies (see
Subsection 5.4
) that models of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
exclude a fixed complete graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{c}
as a minor, where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
depends only on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.
Therefore,
we can apply
Subsection A.5
to the input graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
and, assuming that the treewidth of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
is large enough, we can find in linear time a flat wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{0}
and an apex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
such that the height of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{0}
is a sufficiently large function of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.
Moreover, another crucial property guaranteed by
Subsection A.5
is that the treewidth of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{0}
is bounded from above by a function of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.
This will be exploited in
Subsection 2.2
in order to compute the so-called
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
-characteristic
of a wall. We will now apply a series of “zooming” arguments to the wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{0},
which are illustrated in
Figure 2
(see the proof of
Subsection 8.1
for the precise constants).

Subsection 7.2
Subsection 7.2
Subsection A.6
Find_Equiv_FlatPairs
Subsection 8.1
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{0}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{1}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{2}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{3}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal W}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\star}

Figure 2
:
Sequence of walls considered in the general scheme of our algorithm, along with the results used to obtain them, where the first wall is obtained by applying
Subsection A.5
to the input graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G.

Starting from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{0}
and its associated apex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A,
we apply
Subsection A.6
and find, in linear time, a large (again, as a function of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
) subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{1}
that is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textlambda
-
homogeneous
, where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textlambda
depends only on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.
The definition of a homogenous flat wall can be found in
Subsection A.6
, and roughly means that each of its bricks can route the same set of partial minors of the graphs corresponding to the minor-exclusion part of the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.
We now apply
Subsection 7.2
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{1},
which is a core result of
[
130
]
(see also
[
128
]
), and obtain in linear time a large subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{2}
that is
irrelevant
with respect to the minor-exclusion part of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
after the removal of a vertex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(G)
of small enough
bidimensionality
(see
Subsection 2.2
). Intuitively, working “inside”
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{2}
allows us to “forget” the minor-exclusion part of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
in what follows. As our next step, we apply
Subsection 7.2
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{2},
and obtain in linear time a still large subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{3}
such that its associated apex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A_{3}
is “tightly tied” to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{3},
in the sense that the neighbors in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{3}
of every vertex in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A_{3}
are spread in a “bidimensional” way. This combinatorial technical condition is critically used in the proof of
Subsection 8.1
.

Finding an irrelevant subwall.
So far, we have found a large wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{3}
that satisfies the conditions listed in the statement of

Subsection 8.1
. Now, in order to identify an irrelevant vertex inside
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{3},
we proceed as follows (see the algorithm
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\tt Find\_Equiv\_FlatPairs}
discussed informally in
Subsection 8.3
and presented with all details in
Subsection 9.5
). The strategy of the proof is to find, inside the wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{3},
a collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal W}
of pairwise disjoint subwalls, and to associate each of these subwalls with an appropriately defined
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
-characteristic
that captures its behavior with respect to the partial satisfaction of the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.
Then the idea is that, if there are sufficiently many subwalls in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal W}
with the same
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
-characteristic (called
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
-equivalent
), then some subwall in the interior of one of them can be declared
annotation-irrelevant
and this implies some progress in simplifying the current problem instance.

The above strategy is formalized in
Subsection 8.1
, which allows to identify a subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\star}
inside
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal W}
such that its central part can be removed from the annotated set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R,
and such that a smaller central part can be removed from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
(the blue and grey subwalls in the rightmost wall of
Figure 2
, respectively). The proof of
Subsection 8.1
is based on
Subsection 8.1
, which is the main technical part of this paper, and whose full proof
is postponed to Sections
9
,
10
, and
11
for different fragments of the logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta.
The proof is based on the algorithm
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\tt Find\_Equiv\_FlatPairs}
mentioned above, which is in turn based on an appropriate definition of the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
-characteristic
of a wall. A brief explanation of the proof strategy of
Subsection 8.1
is given in
Subsection 8.3
(see
Figure 8
), and in what follows we sketch the main ingredients and key ideas.

2.2
A simplified and illustrative setting

In order to provide some intuition of the proof of
Subsection 8.1
, in this subsection we
focus on formulas
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta
of a particular form, i.e., belonging to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}_{1},
a set of formulas formally defined in
Section 9
which we proceed to define informally in a semantical level:
Given a general graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
as input, we seek for a vertex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(G),
called
modulator
, such that, using the notation defined in the introduction,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf stell}(G,X)
satisfies the so-called
modulator sentence

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta,
and either every connected component
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X,
or the whole graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X,
satisfies the so-called
target sentence

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma,
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma=\textsigma\wedge\textmu
with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
being an arbitrary
FOL
-sentence and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu
expressing the property of belonging to a proper minor-closed graph class.

Note that when
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\bar{\textTheta}_{1},
the target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma
needs to be satisfied either by each of the resulting connected components separately, or jointly by their union. We deal with this easily, by introducing a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\circ/\bullet
-flag into the corresponding sentences that distinguishes both cases. The latter case is simpler, but in
this description, in order to better illustrate our techniques, we assume the former.

Identifying the privileged component.

A very useful tool in our algorithms is to identify, for every given
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
, a
unique
connected component among those of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X,
which we call the
privileged component
, that contains “most” of the wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{3}.

Let us formalize a bit this idea. For a positive integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q,
a
pseudogrid

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q},
defined in
[
102
]
, is a collection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q
“vertical” and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q
“horizontal” paths that intersect in a “grid-like” way, as illustrated in
Figure 6
. Note that the considered wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{3}
naturally defines a (large, as a function of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
) pseudogrid. A connected component
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
is
privileged
with respect to a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(G)
and a pseudogrid
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
is a connected component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X
that contains entirely at least one vertical and one horizontal path of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}.
It is easy to see (
Subsection 7.3
) that such a privileged component, if it exists, is unique.

Moreover, when
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
is a modulator, the fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf torso}(G,X)
has bounded treewidth implies that every connected component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X
has a “small interface” to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
and thus the flat wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{0}
(and any large subwall of it) is not significantly “damaged” by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X,
which we formalize via the notion of having small
bidimensionality
(see
Subsection 7.2
). Intuitively (see
Subsection 7.2
for the definition), this means that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
intersects a small number of so-called “bags” of the wall. Informally, the
bags
of a wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
in a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
with apex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A
define a partition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus A
into connected sets, such that each bag, except the external one, contains the part of the wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
between two neighboring degree-3 vertices of the wall, as illustrated in
Figure 26
(see
Subsection A.7
for the definition).
This property is used extensively in the proofs and, in particular, it defines, assuming the existence of a large flat wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{0}
and a modulator
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X,
a
unique
privileged component
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X
(regardless of the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\circ/\bullet
-flag).
In our sentences, in order to identify such a component, we need to integrate the “recognition” of a pseudogrid
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}
and its associated privileged component with respect to a modulator
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
:
it is easy to see that these properties can be defined in
CMSOL
(see
Section 9
).

Splitting the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

The existence of a privileged component
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
allows us to see the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
as a conjunction of two subsentences: one that concerns the privileged component
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
(where we will find the irrelevant vertex) and another one concerning the modulator
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
and the other (non-privileged) components of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X.
Namely, in
Subsection 9.1

we define a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{{q}},
called the
split version
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}},
that allows us to “break”
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
into two questions: one denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\sf out}_{{q}}
that is the conjunction of the
modulator sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta
and the target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma
in the non-privileged components of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X
and another one that concerns the target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma
in the privileged component
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C.
This latter question is composed of two subsentences (see
Equation 6
), namely one about the satisfaction of the
FOL
-sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
and another one about the minor-exclusion given by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu.
Given this decomposition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
into three questions (one “external” and two “internal” ones), our “irrelevancy” arguments also decompose into three parts. Concerning the “irrelevancy” for minor-exclusion, as discussed above, the fact that the whole wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{2}
is irrelevant with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu
allows us to focus on the other two questions. For this, we need to define the
characteristic
of a wall with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,
denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\text{-}{\sf char}
(see
Equation 25
).
This characteristic is composed of two parts: the
out-signature
(see
Subsection 9.3
) corresponding to the satisfiability of the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\sf out}_{{q}},
and the
in-signature
(see
Subsection 9.4
) corresponding to the
FOL
-sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma.

Let us now explain how we define the out-signature and the in-signature, and sketch why we can eventually declare a subwall irrelevant.

Defining the out-signature of a wall.

Dealing with the irrelevancy with respect to the “external” sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\sf out}_{{q}}
turns out to be the most interesting part of the proof of
Subsection 8.1
, and we introduce several ideas which are, in our opinion, one of the main conceptual contributions of this article. The goal is, for each wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
in the collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal W},
to encode all the necessary information that concerns the satisfiability of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\sf out}_{{q}}
in the “non-privileged” part of the graph and the modulator
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X.
To do this, for each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W\in{\cal W}
with apex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A,
we define a set of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
-boundaried graphs
(i.e., graphs in which
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
“boundary” vertices are equipped with labels), constructed as we describe below, and where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
depends only on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

The boundary corresponds to where the sentence has been “split” and we need to “guess” how to complement this boundary by the part of the modulator that is not inside the wall. Note that, since
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\sf out}_{{q}}
is a
CMSOL
-sentence, by a variant of Courcelle’s theorem for boundaried graphs
[
31
,
32
,
33
]
(see
Subsection 7.1
), there exists a
finite
collection

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf rep}^{(\ell)}(\texttheta^{\sf out}_{{q}})
of sentences on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
-boundaried graphs that are “representatives” of the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\sf out}_{{q}}
and that can be effectively constructed. We next described how these
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
-boundaried graphs are constructed.

We observe that, by
Subsection 7.2
(which uses the bounded-treewidth property of the modulator sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta
), there exists a “buffer”
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W,
consisting of a set of consecutive layers of the wall, which is disjoint from a hypothetical modulator
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X.
We guess with an integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}d
where this “buffer”
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I
is placed in the wall and we denote its inner part by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I^{(d)}.
This naturally induces a partition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
into
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{\sf in}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{\sf out},
with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{\sf in}
being the part of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
that is inside
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I^{(d)}
(see
Figure 14
). We also guess which subset of the apex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A
will belong to the modulator
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
and we denote it by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V_{L}({\bf a}),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}L
is the set containing the indices of the corresponding apex vertices. Since parts of the “non-privileged” vertex set of the graph may lie outside the considered wall, we need to guess the part of the modulator (namely, its boundary towards the component) that lies outside the wall. More precisely, we need to guess as well
which subset
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}F^{\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{\sf out},
other than
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V_{L}({\bf a}),
will belong to the neighborhood of the privileged component. This is achieved by guessing all ways an (abstract) graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}F^{\prime}
with a bounded number of vertices can extend the boundary (see
Figure 9
). We let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}F
be the graph obtained from the union of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V_{L}({\bf a})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}F^{\prime}.
Finally, we also need to consider a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Z
that corresponds to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{\rm in}
together with the part inside
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I^{(d)}
that has been “chopped off”
by the modulator
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X,
that is, the part of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
inside
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I^{(d)}
that will not belong to the privileged component after the removal of the modulator
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X.

We denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\partial(Z)
the set of vertices in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Z
that have a neighbor in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I^{(d)}.

Altogether, these guesses result in the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
-boundaried graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K^{(d,Z,L,F)}
obtained from the graph induced by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I^{(d)}
and the set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}F,
whose boundary is the set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\partial(Z)\cup F
;
see
Figure 10
and
Figure 12
for an illustration of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K^{(d,Z,L,F)}
and of how this graph lies with respect to the privileged component
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C,
respectively.

With each such a guess
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(R,d,L,Z)
we associate the out-signature defined as follows and denoted by
out
-
sig
(see
Equation 7
). Its elements are pairs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}({\bf H},\bar{\texttheta}),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf H}
encodes how the set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V_{L}({\bf a})
in the boundary has been extended by the “abstract” graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}F^{\prime},
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\texttheta}\in{\sf rep}^{(\ell)}(\texttheta^{\sf out}_{{q}})
prescribes the equivalence class, within the set of Courcelle’s representatives mentioned above, of the considered
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
-boundaried graph. This concludes the description of the out-signature.

While this out-signature indeed encodes the behavior of the considered wall with respect to the “external” sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\sf out}_{{q}},
a crucial issue has been overlooked so far: in order to be able to identify an irrelevant subwall inside the collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal W}
within the claimed running time, we need to be able to
compute
the (in- and out-) signature of a wall in linear time. To do this using Courcelle’s theorem, we need to consider a graph that has treewidth bounded by a function of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

Recall that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\sf out}_{{q}}
is the conjunction of the modulator sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta
(which is evaluated in the graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf stell}(G,X)
) and the target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma
in the “non-privileged” components of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X.
By the condition guaranteed by
Subsection A.5
discussed in the paragraph above
Figure 2
, we have that the treewidth of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
is bounded by a function of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,

hence the treewidth of the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
-boundaried “subwall”
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K^{(d,Z,L,F)},
for which we want to compute the out-signature, is also bounded
by a function of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.
However, the graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K^{(d,Z,L,F)}\setminus V(F)
“lives” inside the
whole
privileged component
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C,
and we cannot guarantee that the treewidth of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
is bounded by a function of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.
We overcome this problem with the following trick, which is an important tool in the proof of
Claim 1
. We observe that the satisfaction of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\sf out}_{{q}}
is preserved if, instead of the whole privileged component
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C,
we consider the graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K^{(d,Z,L,F)},
which is obtained
by “shrinking”
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
to the subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I^{(d)},
and which has bounded treewidth as we need (compare the left part of
Figure 15
with
Figure 16
). Indeed, this modification does not change any of the non-privileged components in which the target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma
is evaluated and, by adding edges from the “guessed extended boundary”
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}F^{\prime}
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I^{(d)}
in order to preserve connectivity (see
Figure 10
), the resulting graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf stell}(G,X)
remains unchanged with this transformation, and therefore
the satisfaction of the modulator sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta
is also preserved.

Defining the in-signature of a wall.

To deal with the irrelevancy with respect to the
FOL
-sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma,
we use arguments strongly inspired by those of
[
53
]
. The core tool here is Gaifman’s locality theorem (see
Subsection 4.3
), which states that every
FOL
-sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
is a Boolean combination of basic local sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma_{1},\ldots,\textsigma_{p},
in the sense that the satisfaction of each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma_{i}
depends only on the satisfaction of a set of sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi_{1},\ldots,\textpsi_{\ell_{i}}
evaluated on single vertices that can be assumed to be pairwise far apart (see
Subsection 6.2
). As discussed before, taking care of the domain of these vertices is the main reason why we consider a annotated version of the problem, corresponding to the enhanced sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

Extending the approach of
[
53
]
(which does not deal with apices), the in-signature of a wall, denoted by
in
-
sig
, encodes all (partial) sets of variables, one set for each basic local sentence of the so-called Gaifman sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textsigma},
such that these variables lie inside an “inner part” of the wall, they are scattered in the “apex-projection” of this inner part, and they satisfy the local sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi_{i}
; see
Equation 8

for the formal definition.

Declaring a subwall irrelevant.

We now sketch the remaining of the proof of
Subsection 8.1
for sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}_{1},
presented in
Subsection 9.6
(see
Figure 8
).
As mentioned above, suppose that we have already found, inside the collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal W},
a large (as a function of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
) subcollection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal W}^{\prime}\subseteq{\cal W}
of walls all having the same
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
-characteristic. We pick one of these walls, say
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\star}\in{\cal W}^{\prime},
and we declare its central part irrelevant (see
Figure 2
). We need to prove that, if the input graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
satisfies
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,
then the graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}
obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
by removing the central part of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\star},
also satisfies
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.
That is, given a modulator
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
in the original instance
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
we need to construct another set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X^{\prime}\subseteq V(G)
that is disjoint from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\star}
and that is a modulator in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}.
For this, we proceed as follows.

The cardinality of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal W}^{\prime}
and the fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
intersects few bags of the wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{3}
(see
Subsection 7.2
) imply that there exists a large (again, as a function of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
) subcollection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal W}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{\cal W}^{\prime}
of walls that are disjoint from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X.
We take such a wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{W}\in{\cal W}^{\prime\prime}
and, using the fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\star}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{W}
have the same
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
-characteristic, we show that we can “replace” the part of the modulator
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
that intersects
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\star}
with another part in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{W}
(see
Figure 19
), together with an alternative assignment of variables that satisfies the corresponding sentences. This results in another set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X^{\prime}
that is a modulator in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime},
hence yielding the annotation irrelevancy of (the central part of)
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\star}.

Showing these facts is far from being easy and we need a number of technical details
that are structured into three parts, corresponding to
Claim 1
,
Claim 2
, and
Claim 3
. Each of these claims deals, respectively, with the irrelevancy with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\sf out}_{q}
(which incorporates
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma,
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu.
In particular, an important idea in the proof of
Claim 1
is that, changing from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X^{\prime},
we obtain a new boundaried graph, which is in fact the same graph but with a new boundary (see
Figure 19
). In the proof of
Claim 2
, the replacement arguments for the in-signature work because of the aforementioned distance-preservation property of the apex-projection.

2.3
How to deal with the general logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta

In this subsection we sketch how to generalize the ideas presented in
Subsection 2.2
to the general compound logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta.
We do this in two steps, corresponding to
Section 10
and
Section 11
, respectively.

A less particular case: allowing for recursion.

Once the fragment
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}_{1}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
is proved, the next step is to consider the fragment
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}
presented in
Section 10
. Namely, the problem is defined by a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\bar{\textTheta}
composed of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{1},\ldots,\texttheta_{r}
with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
modulator sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta_{1},\ldots,\textbeta_{r}
and one target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{0}=\textsigma\wedge\textmu,
which are defined recursively. That is, starting with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i=r,
the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i},
evaluated on a (dynamic) graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
asks for the existence of a vertex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{i}\subseteq V(G)
that satisfies the modulator sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta_{i}
and such that either each connected component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X,
or the whole graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X,
satisfies the “next” sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i-1}
(see
Equation 22
).

The strategy of the proof is essentially the same as in the previous case, and the main extra technical issue is to deal with what we call the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\circ/\bullet
-
scenarios
, which capture whether, in each level of the recursion defined by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i},
the next sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i-1}
needs to be satisfied by each connected component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X,
or by the whole graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X.
Such a scenario
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
of length
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
gives rise to, instead of a privileged component
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C,
to the notion of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
-privileged sequence

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{1},\ldots,C_{r+1}
with respect to a pseudogrid
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}
and a collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal X}=\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}\}
of pairwise disjoint subsets of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(G).
A
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
-privileged sequence contains, for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[r],
the privileged component
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{i}
(which is again unique) of the “current” graph after the removal of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{i}\cup\ldots\cup X_{r},
defined according to the scenario
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w.
Note that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq C_{r},
and we call
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{1}
the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
-privileged set
. See
Subsection 10.1
for the details and
Figure 20
for an illustration of a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\circ/\bullet
-scenario. It is worth observing that a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
-privileged set is a, possibly disconnected, graph (see
Figure 20
), and in the proof we always work in the privileged component with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal X}.

It is again easy to see that the notion of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
-privileged set can be defined in
CMSOL
(see
Subsection 10.1
), which allows to integrate this information in our sentences. We then split the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}

in a similar way as we did in the previous case (see
Equation 23
), separating the questions that concern the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
-privileged sequence, in which we aim to find an irrelevant vertex, namely in the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
-privileged set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{1}
that contains the large portion of the wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{3},
as illustrated in
Figure 20
.

The proof of
Subsection 8.1
for the case
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\bar{\textTheta}
follows closely the one for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\bar{\textTheta}_{1}
discussed in
Subsection 2.2
, and is summarized in
Subsection 8.3
and presented in detail in
Appendix B
. One of the main differences is that, when defining the out-signature of a wall (see
Subsection 10.3
), the boundaried graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K^{(d,{\cal Z},{\cal L},{\cal F})}
that we consider (see
Figure 21
) is defined with respect to
collections

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal Z},{\cal L},{\cal F},
in order to deal with the boundaries that are created recursively by the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.
The roles of
Claim 1
,
Claim 2
, and
Claim 3
is now replaced by
Claim 4
,
Claim 5
, and
Claim 6
, respectively (see
Figure 8
).

The general case: allowing for Boolean combinations.

Finally, we deal in
Section 11
with the general logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta,
corresponding to
Subsection 1.2
. The difference with respect to the previous case is that now, within each level of the recursion, we allow for Boolean combinations of sentences in a lower level (see
Subsection 5.4
). Since the strategy of the proof is again the same as in the previous cases, in
Section 11
we do not repeat it again, and we rather focus on the local changes that need to be done with respect to the proof presented so far, which mostly concern the definition of the in-signature and the out-signature of a wall, and provide a sketch of how to integrate these changes in the previous proof.

Our approach first considers a restricted version of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta,
the logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\textTheta}
defined in
Subsection 11.1
where the only positive Boolean combination of sentences that we allow in each recursive level is the
conjunction
of a finite number of sentences.
Then, in
Subsection 11.2
we show how to insert also disjunctions to our arsenal of
positive Boolean combinations, in order to achieve the generality of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta.

This approach is based on the fact (see
Section 11
) that every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi
that is a positive Boolean combination of some set of sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textPhi
has an equivalent sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi^{\prime}
that is a disjunction of conjunctions of sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textPhi.

In
Subsection 11.1
, the idea is to associate each sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\hat{\textTheta}
with a rooted tree expressing its recursive definition, where the root corresponds to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
and every conjunction to a bifurcation of the tree.
Under the presence of a large enough pseudogrid, we use this tree to define an equivalent version of the problem, where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
is “focused” towards the privileged connected component occurring each time in the leaves of the tree.
Under this scope, every root-to-leaf path of the tree corresponds to a sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}.

Performing this modification, we have to keep track of the bifurcations of the tree and ask the modulators that correspond to each such a bifurcation to be the same sets in all paths that contain this “bifurcated” node.
These equalities have to be respected when searching for an equivalent “solution-certificate” that comes with the application of the irrelevant vertex technique.
For this reason, we have to (further) modify the definition of signatures and characteristics given in
Subsection 10.3
so as to add one “extra dimension” to them (corresponding to the swift from the “path-like” structure of sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}
to the “tree-like” structure of sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\textTheta}
), while respecting the equalities obtained from above.
At the end of
Subsection 11.1
we sketch how to prove
Subsection 8.1
for a sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\textTheta}.

In
Subsection 11.2
, when disjunctions are allowed, we define a notion of a
conjunctive scenario

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal S}
of a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta
(expressed in terms of a tree representation as in
Subsection 11.1
)
and prove (see
Subsection 11.2
) that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
is satisfied by a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
if and only if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
satisfies at least one of the conjunctive scenarios of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.
This results in a redefinition of the characteristic of a wall with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,
as the product of the characteristics of all these possible scenarios (see
Equation 24
). Then, we follow the same arguments as sketched at the end of
Subsection 11.1
.
In the current case, when considering a collection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
-equivalent walls, following the definition of the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
-characteristic given by
Equation 24
, these walls are

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{S}
-equivalent for
every
conjunctive scenario
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S\in{\cal S}.

Therefore, when we find a part of a wall and declare it “irrelevant”, it is “irrelevant” for any possible conjunctive scenario of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,
and the proof is complete.

3
Applications

Before we proceed to a discussion on the consequences of
Subsection 1.2
(stated as
Subsection 5.4
for general structures)
Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf t}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})\in\mathbb{N}^{l}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\chi,\textpsi:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}.

We say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\chi(n)={\cal O}_{{\bf t}}(\textpsi(n))
if there exists a computable
function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi:\mathbb{N}^{l}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}

such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\chi(n)={\cal O}(\textphi({\bf t})\cdot\textpsi(n)).

Given a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
we define its
size

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|G|
as the number of its vertices and when we present running times of algorithms we always use
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}n=|G|.

Given a finite set of graphs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal H},
we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|{\cal H}|
the biggest size of a graph in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal H}.
Given a graph class
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}

we define its
minor-obstruction
set (or simply,
obstruction set
) as the set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\cal G})
of minor-minimal graphs not in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}.
According to Robertson-Seymour’s theorem,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\cal G})
is a finite set for every graph class
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
(see
Subsection 12.2
).

3.1
Vertex removal problems

In this subsection we consider several modification problems based on vertex removals.

Classic modification problems.

The first wide family of modification problems modeled by

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{1}
asks whether the
vertex removal distance
of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
is at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k,
that is,
whether there is set of at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
vertices in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
whose removal gives a graph in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}.

In many cases,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}={\sf excl}({\cal H})
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal H}
is some (finite) set of graphs
and/or satisfies some
FOL
-definable property. In the case where the
FOL
demand is void, this problem admits a time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{k,|{\cal H}|}(n^{2})
algorithm because of
Subsection 1.2
, as its
yes
-instances are minor-closed for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k.
Instantiations of this general problem
occupied a lot of research in the last years
[
87
,
130
,
2
,
115
,
90
]
.
However, for arbitrary
FOL
properties, the

yes
-instances of this problem are not minor-closed anymore and cannot be treated by any of Propositions
1.2
,
1.2
, or
1.2
. The first result
in this direction appeared in
[
53
]
and treats the case where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal H}=\{K_{5},K_{3,3}\},
i.e., when the target property
is being planar and satisfying some
FOL
property. Therefore,
Subsection 1.2
constitutes a far-reaching generalization of the results of
[
53
]
in the following sense: while it extends the tractability of modification problems to a much more general property than planarity, it also is applicable to a much broader class of modulators. We note that the very recent results of
[
73
,
74
]
can also treat certain modulators of bounded size on minor-closed graph classes.

Alternative measures of the modulator.

In a recent wave of results, alternative quality measures of the “modulator” to some graph class were considered, other than just its size
[
3
,
24
,
25
,
21
,
110
,
47
,
56
,
5
,
86
]
.
The first step in this direction was
done by Bulian and Dawar
[
24
,
25
]

who considered the
elimination distance
to some graph class
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}.
We assume that the target graph class is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}={\rm Mod}(\textgamma_{0}),
for some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma_{0}\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}.

Also, for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k\geq 1,
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma_{k}=\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma_{k-1},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta
demands than
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf torso}(G,X)

is edgeless.
Using our notation, a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
has
elimination distance
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
if

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\models\textgamma_{k}.

Alternatively, one may observe that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma_{k}=\textbeta_{k}\triangleright\textgamma_{0}
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta_{k}
expresses the fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf torso}(G,X)
has tree-depth
at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k.

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}={\sf excl}({\cal H}),
where

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal H}
is a finite set of graphs, Bulian and Dawar
[
25
]

proved that
the corresponding modification problem
can be solved by a (constructive) algorithm running in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{k,|{\cal H}|}(n^{2}).
Elimination distance to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
has been also studied in
[
56
]
for the case where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
is some
FOL
-definable graph class. We note that the result in
[
124
]
implies that deciding whether the elimination distance to some
FOL
-definable graph class is at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
can be done in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{k,|{\cal H}|}(n^{3})
on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal H}
-topological-minor-free graphs.

An alternative elimination distance measure was recently given by the parameter of
bridge-depth
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}.
Using our notation, a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
has
bridge-depth
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\in{\rm Mod}(\textgamma_{k})
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma_{k}
is
is defined as above with the difference that now
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta
demands that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf torso}^{+}(G,X)
is acyclic.
2
2

2

The graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf torso}^{+}(G,X)
is defined as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf torso}(G,X)
with the difference that now we do not remove the contracted vertices
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{C}
in the end.

Bridge-depth was introduced in
[
21
]
for the case where the target graph class
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
is edgeless and has been used on the the study of the existence of polynomial kernels for structural parameterizations.

Another modulator measure is the notion of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
-treewidth
, recently introduced by Eiben, Ganian, Hamm, and Kwon
[
47
]
. Having
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
-treewidth at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
is equivalent to asking that the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf torso}(G,X)
has treewidth at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
and the target property is containment in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}.
Jansen, de Kroon, and Włodarczyk proposed in
[
86
]
a time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{k,|{\cal H}|}(n^{{\cal O}(1)})
algorithm for this problem in the case when
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}={\sf excl}({\cal H}),
for some finite set of graphs

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal H}.
The algorithms in
[
86
]
are strongly based on the computation of important separators
[
113
]
and the contribution of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k,|{\cal H}|
on their running times is explicit.

Recently, Agrawal, Kanesh, Lokshtanov, Panolan, Ramanujan, Saurabh, and Zehavi proved in
[
4
]
that, under certain assumptions on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
-treewidth, elimination distance to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G},
and vertex deletion to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
are all
FPT
-equivalent. The techniques used in
[
4
]
are heavily based on the meta-algorithmic result of
[
111
]
(based on the recursive understanding technique) that is non-constructive and implies
FPT
-algorithms whose running time is worse than quadratic.

All problems above are
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
-definable even if, apart from asking containment in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf excl}({\cal H}),
we additionally impose some
FOL
-definable demand to the target property (or even a demand definable in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
itself).
To formalize this, in what follows we give a theoretical framework that comprises, by
Subsection 1.2
, all the aforementioned measures on the modulator.

A parametric variant of our results.

A
graph parameter
is a function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}:{\cal G}_{\rm all}\to\mathbb{N}.
We say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}
is
treewidth-bounded

if there is a function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
for each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\in{\cal G}_{\rm all},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}(G)\leq f({\sf tw}(G)).

We say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}
is
CMSOL
-definable

if for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k\in\mathbb{N}
there is a
CMSOL
-sentence (on graphs)
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta_{k}

such that the set of all models of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta_{k}
is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\rm Mod}(\textbeta_{k})=\{G\mid{\bf p}(G)\leq k\}.

Clearly, if
