\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}
is treewidth-bounded then we can also
assume that each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta_{k}
is a sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\sf tw}
and in this case we say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}
is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\sf tw}
-definable.
There are several known graph parameters that are
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\sf tw}
-definable, such as treewidth, pathwidth, tree-depth, bridge-depth, block tree-depth, vertex cover, feedback vertex set, branch-width, carving-width, or cutwidth.

For a graph parameter
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}
and a graph class
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G},

we define the new graph parameter
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}_{\cal G}:{\cal G}_{\rm all}\to\mathbb{N}

such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle{\bf p}_{\cal G}(G)

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\min\{k\mid\exists X\subseteq V(G)\mid{\bf p}({\sf torso}(G,X))\leq k\penalty\ \wedge\penalty\ G\setminus X\in{\cal G}\}.

(3)

Thus
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}_{\cal G}
measures by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}
the quality of a modulator
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
to property
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}.
For example, when
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}
is the size of the modulator, then this is just the
vertex deletion distance to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
, that is, the minimum number of vertices
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X\in{\cal G}.

When
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}
is the tree-depth of a graph, then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}_{\cal G}
is the elimination distance to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}.
Or when
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}
is the treewidth of a graph, then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}_{\cal G}
corresponds to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
-treewidth.
We consider the general setting where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\sf tw}
-definable graph parameter and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
-definable graph class, that is,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\rm Mod}(\texttheta)={\cal G}
for some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta.
By setting
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{k}=\textbeta_{k}\triangleright\texttheta\in\textTheta,
we have that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\rm Mod}(\texttheta_{k})=\{G\mid{\bf p}_{\cal G}(G)\leq k\}.
Then the following theorem is a direct consequence of
Subsection 1.2

and
Subsection 1.2
.

Theorem 7
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}
be a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\sf tw}
-definable graph parameter and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}={\rm Mod}(\texttheta)
for some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta.
Then there is an algorithm
that, with input a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k\in\mathbb{N},
checks whether
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}_{\cal G}(G)\leq k
in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{k,|\texttheta|}(n^{2}).

Moreover, if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}={\rm Mod}(\tilde{\texttheta})
for some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}\in\tilde{\textTheta},

then there is an algorithm
that, with the same input, checks whether
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf p}_{\cal G}(G)\leq k
in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{k,|\texttheta|,{\sf hw}(G)}(n^{2}).

All the results mentioned in this subsection, in what concerns minor-excluded graphs, are subsumed
by
Subsection 3.1
. Moreover, by allowing
FOL
-definability in the target sentence and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\sf tw}
-definability in the modulator sentence, we vastly extend
Subsection 1.2

to graph classes and parameters that are not necessarily minor-closed or hereditary. We stress that none of the results in
[
124
,
73
]
is able to deal with the problems captured by
Subsection 3.1
in their full generality.

3.2
Other variants of modifications problems

Subsection 1.2
deals with vertex modulators. On the other hand, there is a lot of literature on graph modification problems involving several types of local operations (see e.g.,
[
37
]
for a survey).

Subsection 1.2
in its full model-theoretic power is stated on structures (see
Subsection 5.4
in
Section 5
). This permits us to
encode way more entangled modification operations. For instance, one may consider annotated graphs and modification in several steps with different constraints, including colored graphs and demands on the distribution on the colors in the modulator and the target part.
The systematic study of the power of definability of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
goes beyond the scope of this paper. However, in what follows we give three indicative examples
of problems that can be expressed via reductions to structures.

A grammar for modification problems.

A compact way to extend the applicability of
Subsection 5.4
is by defining the context-free language
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal M}
for graph modifications.
We define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal M}
as the context-free language generated by specific rules. The alphabet of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal M}
consists of all sentences of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{0}[\{{\sf E}\}]
plus nine additional letters
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{{\sf M},{\sf F},{\sf(},{\sf)},\vee,\wedge,{\sf n},{\sf e},{\sf c}\}.

We use
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf n},
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf e}

to encode vertex removal, and edge removal, respectively. We also need
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}

to work with connected components.
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal M}
is generated by the following rules. The terminal strings of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal M}
are the sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{0}[\{{\sf E}\}],
thus

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf F}\rightarrow\textTheta_{0}[\{{\sf E}\}].
The rest of the production rules are:
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf M}\rightarrow({\sf F}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf M}\rightarrow({\sf n}{\sf M}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf M}\rightarrow({\sf e}{\sf M}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf M}\rightarrow({\sf c}{\sf M}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf M}\rightarrow({\sf M}\wedge{\sf M}),
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf M}\rightarrow({\sf M}\vee{\sf M}).

For each string
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf w}\in{\cal M},

we define the class of graphs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}_{\sf w}
as follows.

•

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|{\sf w}|=1
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}_{\sf w}={\rm Mod}({\sf w}).

•

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf w}=({\sf n}{\sf w}^{\prime}),
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}_{\sf w}=\{G\mid\exists v\in V(G),G\setminus v\in{\cal G}_{{\sf w}^{\prime}}\}.

•

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf w}=({\sf e}{\sf w}^{\prime}),
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}_{\sf w}=\{G\mid\exists e\in V(G),G\setminus e\in{\cal G}_{{\sf w}^{\prime}}\}.

•

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf w}=({\sf c}{\sf w}^{\prime}),
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}_{\sf w}=\{G\mid\forall C\in{\sf cc}(G),\ C\in{\cal G}_{{\sf w}^{\prime}}\}.

•

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf w}=({\sf w}^{\prime}\vee{\sf w}^{\prime\prime}),
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}_{\sf w}={\cal G}_{{\sf w}^{\prime}}\cup{\cal G}_{{\sf w}^{\prime\prime}}.

•

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf w}=({\sf w}^{\prime}\wedge{\sf w}^{\prime\prime}),
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}_{\sf w}={\cal G}_{{\sf w}^{\prime}}\cap{\cal G}_{{\sf w}^{\prime\prime}}.

Let us remind that we use
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus v
for vertex deletion and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus e
for edge deletion.

If in the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal M}
we demand that terminal strings of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal M}
are the sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL},
then we define the context-free language
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal M}.
That way, the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}_{\tilde{\sf w}}
can also be extended for every string
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\sf w}\in\tilde{\cal M}.

By making use of
Subsection 5.4
, we prove the following theorem about modifications defined by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal M}.

Theorem 8
.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf w}\in{\cal M},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}_{\sf w}
is decidable in quadratic time. Moreover, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\sf w}\in\tilde{\cal M},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}_{\tilde{\sf w}}
is decidable in quadratic time on graphs of bounded Hadwiger number.

Proof.

We start with the proof of the first part of the theorem.
Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
be the maximum Hadwiger number of the models of the target sentences of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf w}.

The proof is based on a transformation of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
to a doubly annotated graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G^{\prime},R,B)

and the construction of some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta[\{{\sf E},{\sf X},{\sf X}^{\prime}\}]

such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\in{\cal G}_{\sf w}
if and only if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G^{\prime},R,B)\models\texttheta.
This fact, together with
Subsection 5.4

and the fact that the transformation that we will give can be done in linear time, yield the correctness of the theorem.

The transformation of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G

creates a doubly annotated graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G^{\prime},R,B)
whose annotated vertices make possible to simulate the edge removals by vertex removals.
The new graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}
is constructed by subdividing once each edge
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}e
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
(we call
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{e}
the subdivision vertex) and for each vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,

we construct a clique
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{v}
on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c+1
vertices and identify one of its vertices with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v

We also define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R=\bigcup_{v\in V(G)}V(K_{v}\setminus\{v\})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}B=\{v_{e}\mid e\in E(G)\}.
We call the vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R
(resp.
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}B
)
red vertices
(resp.
blue vertices
) of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}
and we call the rest of the vertices
white vertices
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}.

We next proceed with the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta[\{{\sf E},{\sf X},{\sf X}^{\prime}\}].
Keep in mind that in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}
each blue vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{e}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}
corresponds to an edge
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}e\in E(G)
and each “red-white” clique
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{v}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}
corresponds to a vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in V(G).

To define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,
we use the parsing tree of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf w}
so that (i) each production rule
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf M}\rightarrow({\sf n}{\sf M})
corresponds to
a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\sf tw}[\{{\sf E},{\sf X},{\sf X}^{\prime}\}]
asking for a clique consisting of one white vertex and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
red vertices simulating the removal of a vertex in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
(ii) each production rule
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf M}\rightarrow({\sf e}{\sf M})
corresponds to
a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\sf tw}[\{{\sf E},{\sf X},{\sf X}^{\prime}\}]
asking for a blue vertex whose removal
corresponds to the removal of an edge in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,

(iii) each production rule
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf M}\rightarrow({\sf c}{\sf M})

is simulated by the application of the
c
operation (notice that the previous operations maintain the same components in
both
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}
), and (iv) each production rule
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf M}\rightarrow({\sf M}\wedge{\sf M})
or
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf M}\rightarrow({\sf M}\vee{\sf M})
is simulated by the
PB
operation. We also modify each target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma=\textsigma\wedge\textmu\in\textTheta_{0}[\{{\sf E}\}]

to a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma^{\prime}=\textsigma^{\prime}\wedge\textmu^{\prime}\in\textTheta_{0}[\{{\sf E},{\sf X}\}]
as follows: (i)
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu^{\prime}
is defined so that each obstruction
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H^{\prime}
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\rm Mod}(\textmu^{\prime}))

is created by an obstruction
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\rm Mod}(\textmu))
by identifying each of its vertices with a vertex of a clique of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c+1
and (ii)

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma^{\prime}
is defined using
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
so that the
all quantifications are restricted to white vertices
and the adjacency predicate between two vertices
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}x
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}y

is replaced by the existence of a blue vertex adjacent to both
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}x
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}y.

The definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma^{\prime}
guarantees
that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
is simulated on the “terminal” graphs resulting after the vertex removals and the edge removals. The definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu^{\prime}
implies that if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Q
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Q^{\prime}
are the terminal graphs corresponding to the evaluation of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\prime},
respectively,
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Q
contains some minor in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\rm Mod}(\textmu^{\prime}))
if and only if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Q^{\prime}
contains some minor
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\rm Mod}(\textmu)).

For the second part of the theorem, we have the promise that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf hw}(G)\leq c
for some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c\in\mathbb{N}.

Under this promise, if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\in{\cal G}_{\tilde{\sf w}},
then
the models of the target sentences occurring after the application of every modification scenario encoded in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\sf w}
also have Hadwiger number at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c.
We now consider
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf w}\in{\cal M}
where each target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\sf w}
is replaced by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\wedge\textmu
where

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\rm Mod}(\textmu)={\sf excl}(K_{c}).
Then, under the promise assumption that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf hw}(G)\leq c,
it holds that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\in{\cal G}_{\tilde{\sf w}}\iff G\in{\cal G}_{{\sf w}}

and the result follows as, by the first part of the theorem,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}_{{\sf w}}
can be decided in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{|{\sf w}|}(n^{2})={\cal O}_{|\tilde{\sf w}|,{\bf hw}(G)}(n^{2}).

∎

Subsection 3.2
is able to model
complex
hierarchical modifications
.
That is, it permits to ask for an
iterative removal of sets of edges/vertices
and, in each step, apply a different set of modifications to the resulting connected
components.
This multistage modification can be further enhanced by different modification scenarios using the disjunction/conjunction connectors.

Before we give some examples of results that become special cases of
Subsection 3.2
,
we need some notation. First of all, in the strings of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal M}

we may omit parenthesis by agreeing that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\wedge
has priority over
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\vee.

Also, for a finite set of graphs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal H},
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu_{\cal H}
be a
CMSOL
-sentence expressing
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal H}
-minor-exclusion,
and we use
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpi=\textmu_{\{K_{5},K_{3,3}\}}
for expressing planarity.
Then,
[
128
,
128
,
130
]
treat
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf w}={\sf n}^{k}\textmu_{\cal H},

[
76
,
92
]
treat
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf w}={\sf e}^{k}\textpi,

[
53
]
treats (among others)
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf w}={\sf n}^{k}(\textsigma\wedge\textpi)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf w}={\sf e}^{k}(\textsigma\wedge\textpi),

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL},

and
[
24
]
treats
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}({\sf cd})^{k}{\sf c}\textmu_{\cal H}.

Αs an example of the second part
of
Subsection 3.2
for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{M},

we mention the result of
[
110
]

computing the elimination distance to graphs of bounded degree
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{5}
-minor-free graphs. This problem corresponds to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\sf w}=({\sf cd})^{k}{\sf c}\textsigma_{d},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma_{d}
expresses degree bounded by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}d.

Finally, we wish to mention the result of Agrawal, Kanesh, Panolan, Ramanujan, and Saurabh in
[
5
]

where they consider the problem of checking whether the elimination distance of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\rm Mod}(\textsigma_{\cal F})
is at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k,
where

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma_{\cal F}
expresses minor-exclusion of some finite set of graphs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal F}
as induced subgraphs. According to
[
5
]
, this problem can be solved
in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{k}(n^{{\cal O}_{|\textsigma_{\cal F}|}(1)})
for general graphs.
This problem corresponds to the string
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\sf w}=({\sf cd})^{k}{\sf c}\textsigma_{\cal F}
and, because of the second part
of
Subsection 3.2
, it can be solved
in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{k,h,|\textsigma_{\cal F}|}(n^{2})
on
the special case of
graphs of Hadwiger number bounded by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h.

Variants of
Multiway Cut
and
Multicut
.

Consider first the
Multiway Cut to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
problem: the input is
an annotated graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,T),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T
is a set of terminals. We ask for a set of edges (or vertices) that, when removed from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
leaves each of the terminals in a separate component of the remaining graph, and each such a component belongs to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}.

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}={\rm Mod}(\textsigma)
for some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}\subseteq\tilde{\textTheta},

then, because of
Subsection 5.4
(that is
Subsection 1.2
stated on structures), this problem can be solved in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{k,|\textsigma|,h}(n^{2})
on

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{h}
-minor-free graphs.
The reduction is a simple version of the proof of
Subsection 3.2

with the only difference that we add an extra target
FOL
-sentence asking that after the removal of the edges the remaining graph should have only one terminal vertex.
Alternatively, we may move the minor-exclusion to the target property
and ask that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}={\rm Mod}(\textsigma)\cap{\sf excl}({\cal H})
for some (finite) set of graphs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal H}.
In this latter case, the problem can again be solved in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{k,\textsigma,|{\cal H}|}(n^{2})
without any promise assumption on its inputs, because of
Subsection 5.4
(that is
Subsection 1.2
stated on structures).
Another application is the following extension of the well-studied
Multicut
problem. We define
Multicut to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
as follows.
For an input graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
and a collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{(s_{i},t_{i}),i\in[r]\}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
pairs of terminals, the question is whether we may remove
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
edges (or vertices) from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
such that for each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[r],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}s_{i}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t_{i}
are in different components of the remaining graph, and each of these components belongs to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}.
When
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
is the class of all graphs, this is
Multicut
.
Because of
Subsection 5.4
and
Subsection 5.4
, the previous results about
Multiway Cut to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
translate to

Multicut to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
: when
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}={\rm Mod}(\textsigma)
for some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL},

Multiway Cut to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
can be solved in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{k,|\textsigma|,h,r}(n^{2})
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{h}
-minor-free graphs,
while, when
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}={\rm Mod}(\textsigma)\cap{\sf excl}({\cal H}),

Multiway Cut to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
can be solved in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{k,|\textsigma|,|{\cal H}|,r}(n^{2})
.

Removing edges of prescribed adjacency.

While above we introduced ways to express modifications
involving edge removals, we may further ask for prescribed adjacencies between them. Given a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
and an edge set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}F\subseteq E(G),
we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(F)
the set of all the endpoints of the edges in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}F,
i.e.,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(F)=\bigcup_{e\in F}e.
We also define the subgraph
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G

spanned
by the edge set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}F
as the graph

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(V(F),E).

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H
be a graph. We
say that the graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}
is an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H
-modification
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G

if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H
is a subgraph of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}
is obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
if we remove the edges corresponding to the edges of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H.

For example, if we want to remove a matching of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
edges in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
so that the resulting graph is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime},
then we ask for an

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H
-modification of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G

where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H=([2k],\{\{2i-1,2i\}\mid i\in[k]\}).

Given a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H
and a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta,

we define the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(H,\texttheta)
-
Modification
problem that, with
input a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
asks whether there is an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H
-modification of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
that is a model of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

Theorem 9
.

For every graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H
and every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta,

there is an algorithm solving the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(H,\texttheta)
-
Modification
problem
in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f(|H|,|\texttheta|)\cdot n^{2}
. Moreover, if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}\in\tilde{\textTheta}
the same problem can be solved in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{|H|,|\texttheta|,{\bf hw}(G)}(n^{2}).

Proof.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k=|E(H)|.

We provide a transformation of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
to a multi-annotated graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G^{\prime},R,B,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})
and the construction of some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta[\{{\sf E},{\sf X}_{1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{k+2}\}]

such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\in{\cal G}_{\sf w}
if and only if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G^{\prime},R,B,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})\models\texttheta.

The transformation is similar to the one of
Subsection 3.2
. However, instead of subdividing each edge
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}e=\{x,y\}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,

we replace it by the following gadget, called
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}D_{x,y}
:
take a path of length two between
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}x
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}y,
whose internal vertex is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}z,
increase the multiplicity of each of its two edges to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
and, in the resulting multigraph, subdivide each edge and, for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[k],
include the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i
-th subdivision vertex into
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y_{i}.
Also include
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}z
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}B

and identify each vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in V(G)
with
a clique
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{v}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf hw}({\rm Mod}(\texttheta))+k+1
whose
all vertices except from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v
are included in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R,
as in the construction of
Subsection 3.2
. We call the vertices in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R
(resp.
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}B
)
red
(resp.
blue
) vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}
and we also call the vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}

that correspond to the original vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G

white
. We set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y=Y_{1}\cup\ldots\cup Y_{k}
and we call
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y
the set of the
yellow
vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}.

We now consider a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{+}=\textbeta^{\prime}\triangleright\texttheta^{\prime}
defined as follows.
The modulator sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta^{\prime}
removes
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
blue vertices
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}
and, for each such
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}z_{i},i\in[k],
it also removes all
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}2(k-1)
adjacent yellow
vertices that do not belong to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y_{i}.
This pair of “surviving” yellow vertices will be used to encode the edges of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H.

Moreover,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\prime}
is defined so that (i) each modulator
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\sf tw}
-sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta
composing
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
is modified so to exclude from any quantification the red and the yellow vertices
and (ii) each target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma=\textsigma\wedge\textmu\in\textTheta_{0}[\{{\sf E}\}]

is modified to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma^{\prime}=\textsigma^{\prime}\wedge\textmu^{\prime}\in\textTheta_{0}[\{{\sf E}\}]
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu^{\prime}
is defined exactly as in the proof of
Subsection 3.2
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma^{\prime}=\textsigma_{1}\wedge\textsigma_{2},
defined as follows. Here
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma_{1}
is obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
if we
restrict any quantification to the white vertices and simulate
the adjacency predicate
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}x\sim y
by the existence of the gadget
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}D_{z,y}
between
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}x
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}y.
While, by the construction presented so far, we are able to simulate the edge removals by the removals of blue vertices, we also need to simulate the adjacencies of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H
and this is done by the
FOL
-sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma_{2},
that checks
the existence of an isomorphism between
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H
and the
graph whose vertices are the white vertices that are neighbours of degree-1 yellow vertices, and where two white vertices are adjacent if they have neighboring degree-1 yellow vertices in the same set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k}\}.

∎

4
Basic definitions

This section as well as Sections 5–11 are devoted to the formal statement and proof of our results.
We present here some basic definitions.

4.1
Integers, sets, and tuples

We denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathbb{N}
the set of non-negative integers.
Given two integers
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}p
and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q,
the set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}[p,q]
refers to the set of every integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}p\leq r\leq q.

For an integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}p\geq 1,
we set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}[p]=[1,p]
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathbb{N}_{\geq p}=\mathbb{N}\setminus[0,p-1].

Given a non-negative integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}x,

we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf odd}(x)
the minimum odd number that is not smaller than
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}x.

For a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S,
we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}2^{S}
the set of all subsets of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S
and, given an integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\in[|S|],

we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\binom{S}{r}
the set of all subsets of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r.

Given two sets
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A,B
and a function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f:A\to B,

for a subset
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq A
we use
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f(X)
to denote the set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{f(x)\mid x\in X\}.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal S}
be a collection of objects where the operations
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\cup
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\cap
are defined.
Given two tuples
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{l}),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}x_{i},y_{i}\in{\cal S},
we denote
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf x}\cup{\bf y}=(x_{1}\cup y_{1},\ldots,x_{l}\cup y_{l})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf x}\cap{\bf y}=(x_{1}\cap y_{1},\ldots,x_{l}\cap y_{l}).

Also, we denote
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}{\cal S}=\bigcup_{X\in{\cal S}}X.

4.2
Graphs

Basic concepts on graphs.

All graphs considered in this paper are undirected, finite, and without loops or multiple edges.
We use standard graph-theoretic notation and we refer the reader to

[
43
]
for any undefined terminology.
Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
be a graph. We say that a pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(L,R)\in 2^{V(G)}\times 2^{V(G)}
is a
separation
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G

if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}L\cup R=V(G)
and there is no edge in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
between a vertex in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}L\setminus R
and a vertex in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R\setminus L.

Given a vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in V(G),
we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}N_{G}(v)
the set of vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
that are adjacent to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G.

Also, given a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S\subseteq V(G),
we set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}N_{G}(S)=\bigcup_{v\in S}N_{G}(v).

For
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S\subseteq V(G),
we set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G[S]=(S,E\cap{S\choose 2})
and use the shortcut
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus S
to denote
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G[V(G)\setminus S].

Given a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
and a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(G),
we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\partial_{G}(X)
the set of vertices in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
that are adjacent to vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X.

A path
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{1}v_{r}
-path
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(P)=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}\}
for distinct
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}E(P)=\{\{v_{1},v_{2}\},\{v_{2},v_{3}\},\ldots,\{v_{r-1},v_{r}\}\}.
We denote this by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P=v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}.
Given two disjoint paths
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P=v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Q=u_{1},\ldots,u_{q}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{r}
is adjacent to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}u_{1},
we say that the path
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P\cdot Q=v_{1},\ldots,v_{r},u_{1},\ldots,u_{q}
is the
concatenation
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Q.

Given a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
and a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S\subseteq V(G),
we define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cc}(G,S)
to be the set of the vertex sets of the connected components of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus S.

Treewidth.

A
tree decomposition
of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G

is a pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,\chi)
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T
is a tree and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\chi:V(T)\to 2^{V(G)}

such that

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bigcup_{t\in V(T)}\chi(t)=V(G),

•

for every edge
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}e
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
there is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t\in V(T)
such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\chi(t)

contains both endpoints of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}e,
and

•

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in V(G),
the subgraph of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{T}

induced by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{t\in V(T)\mid{v\in\chi(t)}\}
is connected.

The
width
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,\chi)
is equal to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\max\big\{\left|\chi(t)\right|-1\;\big|\;t\in V(T)\big\}
and the
treewidth
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
is the minimum width over all tree decompositions of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G.

Contractions and minors.

The
contraction
of an edge
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}e=\{u,v\}
of a simple graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
results in a simple graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}

obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus\{u,v\}
by adding a new vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}uv
adjacent to all the vertices
in the set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}N_{G}(u)\cup N_{G}(v)\setminus\{u,v\}.

A graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}
is a
minor
of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}\preceq_{\sf m}G,

if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}
can be obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
by a sequence of vertex removals, edge removals, and edge contractions.
Given a finite collection of graphs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal F}
and a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
we use the notation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal F}\preceq_{\sf m}G
to denote that some graph in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal F}
is a minor of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G.

Given a set of graphs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal F},
we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf excl}({\cal F})
the set containing every graph that excludes all graphs in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal F}
as minors.
A graph class
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
is
minor-closed
if every minor of a graph in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
is also a member of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}.

4.3
First-order logic and monadic second-order logic

In this subsection, we present some basic notions on logical structures, we define first-order logic and counting monadic second-order logic on structures, and present Gaifman’s locality theorem.
We refer the reader to
[
35
]
for a broader discussion on logical structures and monadic second-order logic, from the viewpoint of graphs (see also
[
109
]
).

Structures.

A
vocabulary
is a finite set of relation and constant symbols (we do not use function symbols).
Every relation symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}
is associated with a positive integer that is called the
arity
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R},
which we denote
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ar}({\sf R}).

A
structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
of vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
, in short a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
, consists of a non-empty set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A}),
called the
universe
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},

an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-ary relation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}\subseteq{V(\mathfrak{A})}^{r}
for each relation symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 1,

and an element
3
3

3

We stress that we allow constant symbols to be interpreted as the element
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\varnothing
, where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\varnothing
is an element that is not in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})
.
Throughout this paper, we assume that the universe of every given structure is extended by adding the extra element
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\varnothing
, while all relation symbols are interpreted as tuples of elements of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})
, not containing
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\varnothing
.
Moreover, we assume that for every formula that we consider, quantified first order variables are interpreted as elements of the original universe of the structure (and not
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\varnothing
).

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}}\in\{\varnothing\}\cup V(\mathfrak{A})
for each constant symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}\in\texttau
.
We refer to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}
(resp.
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}}
) as the
interpretation of the symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}
(resp.
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}
) in the structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
.
A structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
is
finite
if its universe
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})
is a finite set.
We denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathbb{STR}[\texttau]
the set of all finite
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structures.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}
be
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structures (both containing
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\varnothing
to their universe).
We say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
is a
substructure
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B},

and we write
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\subseteq\mathfrak{B},
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})\subseteq V(\mathfrak{B}),

for every constant symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}\in\texttau,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{B}}={\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}}
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}}\in V(\mathfrak{B})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{B}}=\varnothing
otherwise,
and
for every relation symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 1
we have
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}\subseteq{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{B}}\cap{V(\mathfrak{A})}^{r}.

We also say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
is an
induced substructure
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B},
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\subseteq\mathfrak{B}
and
for every relation symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 1
we have
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}={\sf R}^{\mathfrak{B}}\cap{V(\mathfrak{A})}^{r}.

Given a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A}),
we use
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}[S]
to denote
the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure with universe
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S,
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}[S]}={\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}\cap S^{r}

for each relation symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 1
and for each constant symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}\in\textsigma,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}[S]}={\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}},
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}}\in S
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}}=\varnothing.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\subseteq\texttau
be a vocabulary.
The
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
-reduct
of a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
is the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\!\upharpoonright\!\textsigma
with universe
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}\upharpoonright\textsigma}={\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}
for each relation symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\textsigma
and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}\upharpoonright\textsigma}={\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}}
for each constant symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}\in\textsigma.

We say that a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
is
isomorphic
to a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}
if there is a bijection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})\cup\{\varnothing\}
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{B})\cup\{\varnothing\},
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpi(\varnothing)=\varnothing
and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k\geq 1,
every relation symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k,
and every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\in{V(\mathfrak{A})}^{k},
it holds that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\in{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}\iff(\textpi(a_{1}),\ldots,\textpi(a_{k}))\in{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{B}}
and for every constant symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}\in\texttau,
it holds that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpi({\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}})={\sf c}^{\mathfrak{B}}.

Given two
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structures
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}
,
where for every constant symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}\in\tau
either
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}}={\sf c}^{\mathfrak{B}}
or
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}}=\varnothing\lor{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{B}}=\varnothing
, we define the
disjoint union
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B},
and we denote it by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\dot{\cup}\mathfrak{B},
as
the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A}\dot{\cup}\mathfrak{B})
is the disjoint union of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})\setminus\{\varnothing\}
,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{B})\setminus\{\varnothing\}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{\varnothing\}
, for every relation symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}\dot{\cup}\mathfrak{B}}={\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}\cup{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{B}},

and
for every constant symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}\in\tau
, if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}}={\sf c}^{\mathfrak{B}}
, then

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}\dot{\cup}\mathfrak{B}}={\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}}={\sf c}^{\mathfrak{B}}
, and if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}}=\varnothing
(resp.
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{B}}=\varnothing
), then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}\dot{\cup}\mathfrak{B}}={\sf c}^{\mathfrak{B}}
(resp.
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}\dot{\cup}\mathfrak{B}}={\sf c}^{\mathfrak{A}}
).

An undirected graph without loops can be seen as an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{{\sf E}\}
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{G}=(V(\mathfrak{G}),{\sf E}^{\mathfrak{G}}),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf E}^{\mathfrak{G}}
is a binary relation that is symmetric and anti-reflexive.

First-order and monadic second-order logic.

We now define the syntax and the semantics of first-order logic and monadic second-order logic of a vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau.

We assume the existence of a countable infinite set of
first-order variables
,
usually denoted by lowercase symbols
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf x}_{1},{\sf x}_{2},\ldots,

and of a countable infinite set of
set variables
,
usually denoted by uppercase symbols
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}_{1},{\sf X}_{2},\ldots.

A
first-order term
is either a first-order variable or a constant symbol.
A
first-order logic formula
, in short
FOL
-formula
, of vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
is built from atomic formulas
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf x}={\sf y}

and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}({\sf x}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{r})\in{\sf R},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
and has arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 1,
on first-order terms
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf x},{\sf y},{\sf x}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{r},

by using the logical connectives
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\vee,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\wedge,
ig
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\neg
and the
quantifiers
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\forall,\exists
on first-order variables.
We denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}[\texttau]
the set of all
FOL
-formulas of vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau.

A
monadic second-order logic formula
, in short
MSOL
-formula
, of vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
is obtained by enhancing
the syntax of
FOL
-formulas by allowing the atomic formulas
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf x}\in{\sf X},

for some first-order term
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf x}
and some set variable
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X},
and allowing quantification both on first-order and set variables.
We denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf MSOL}[\texttau]
the set of all
MSOL
-formulas of vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau.
We make clear that what we call here
MSOL
is what is commonly referred in the literature as
MSO
1
, in which, for the vocabulary of graphs, first-order variables are interpreted as vertices and set variables are interpreted as sets of vertices. Our approach uses Courcelle’s theorem for bounded treewidth
structures (
Subsection 1.2
) as a black-box, which applies for a more general logic than
MSO
1
, that is
MSO
2
.
For the vocabulary of graphs,
MSO
2
extends
MSO
1
by also allowing quantification over edges and edge sets (see
[
35
, Subsection 9.2]
for formal definition of
MSO
2
for general relational vocabularies).
Using this fact, our results hold also in the case we define
MSOL
to be
MSO
2
.

A
counting monadic second-order logic formula
, in short
CMSOL
-formula
, of vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
is obtained by enhancing the syntax of
MSOL
-formulas by allowing predicates
of the form
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf Card}_{p}({\sf X}),
expressing that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|{\sf X}|
is a multiple of an integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}p>1.

We denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau]
the set of all
CMSOL
-formulas of vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau.

The formulas in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}[\texttau]
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau]
are evaluated on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structures by interpreting every
symbol in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
as its interpretation in the structure and every first-order (resp. set) variable as an element
(resp. set of elements) of the universe of the structure.
Given a formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi,
the
free variables
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi
are its variables
that are not in the scope of any quantifier.
We write
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi({\sf x}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{k})
to indicate that the free variables of the formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi
are
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf x}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{k}.

A
sentence
is a formula without free variables.

Given a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},
a formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi({\sf x}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{k})\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau],

and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A}),
we write
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\textphi(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})
to denote that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi({\sf x}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{k})
holds in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
if, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[k],
the variable
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf x}_{i}
is interpreted as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a_{i}.

Two formulas
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi({\sf x}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{k}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi({\sf x}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{k})\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau]
are
equivalent
if for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
and every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in V(\mathfrak{A}),
we have
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\textphi(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\iff\mathfrak{A}\models\textpsi(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}).

We call the set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{\mathfrak{A}\in\mathbb{STR}[\texttau]\mid\mathfrak{A}\models\textphi\}
the set of
models of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi
and we denote it by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\rm Mod}(\textphi).

Gaifman’s locality theorem.

We now aim to present one of the key tools of our proofs,
Gaifman’s locality theorem
.
For this, we first give some definitions.
The
Gaifman graph

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}
of a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
is the graph with vertex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})

and an edge between two distinct vertices
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a,b\in V(\mathfrak{A})

if there is an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}

and a tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r})\in{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}
such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a,b\in\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\}.

Notice that in the particular case of graphs (seen as structures), the original graph and its Gaifman graph are the same.

The
distance

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}d^{\mathfrak{A}}(a,b)
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
between
two elements
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a,b\in V(\mathfrak{A})
is the length of a shortest path
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}
connecting
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}b.

Given an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 1
and an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a\in V(\mathfrak{A}),

we define the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-neighborhood
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}

to be the set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}N^{\mathfrak{A}}_{r}(a)=\{b\in V(\mathfrak{A})\mid d^{\mathfrak{A}}(a,b)\leq r\}.

We use
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}d(a,b)
instead of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}d^{\mathfrak{A}}(a,b)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}N_{r}(a)
instead of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}N^{\mathfrak{A}}_{r}(a)

when
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
is clear from the context.
A first-order formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi({\sf x})
with one free variable
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf x}

is called
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-local
if its validity at an element
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a
in the universe of a structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
only depends
on the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-neighborhood of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},
that is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\textpsi(a)\iff\mathfrak{A}[N_{r}^{\mathfrak{A}}(a)]\models\textpsi(a).

Observe that, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\in\mathbb{N},
there is a first-order formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textdelta_{r}({\sf x},{\sf y})
such that
for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a,b\in V(\mathfrak{A})
we have
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\textdelta_{r}(a,b)

if and only if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}d^{\mathfrak{A}}(a,b)\leq r
(see
[
135
, Lemma 2.4.2]
for a proof).
In what follows, we write
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}d({\sf x},{\sf y})\leq r
instead of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textdelta_{r}({\sf x},{\sf y})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}d({\sf x},{\sf y})>r

instead of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\neg\textdelta_{r}({\sf x},{\sf y}).

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell,r\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}.

A
basic local sentence with parameters
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
is a first-order sentence of the form

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\exists{\sf x}_{1}\ldots\exists{\sf x}_{\ell}\Big(\bigwedge_{1\leq i<j\leq\ell}d({\sf x}_{i},{\sf x}_{j})>2r\wedge\bigwedge_{i=1}^{\ell}\textpsi({\sf x}_{i})\Big)

where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi
is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-local. A
Gaifman sentence
is a Boolean combination of basic local sentences.

Proposition 10

(Gaifman’s locality theorem
[
63
]
)
.

Every first-order sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
is equivalent to a Gaifman sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textsigma}.

Moreover,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textsigma}
can be computed effectively from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
.

For every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau],
we will always denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textsigma}
a Gaifman sentence that is equivalent to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma.

Tree decompositions of structures.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary.
A
tree decomposition
of a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
is a pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,\chi),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T
is a tree and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\chi:V(T)\to 2^{V(\mathfrak{A})}
such that

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bigcup_{t\in V(T)}\chi(t)=V(\mathfrak{A}),

•

for every relation symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 1
and every tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r})\in{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}},

there exists a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t\in V(T)
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\in\chi(t),
and

•

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a\in V(\mathfrak{A}),
the subgraph of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T
induced by the set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{t\in V(T)\mid a\in\chi(t)\}
is connected.

The
width
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,\chi)
is equal to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\max\{|\chi(t)|-1\mid t\in V(T)\}
and the
treewidth
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
is the minimum width over all tree decompositions of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}.

Since for every tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r})\in{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}},
for some relation symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 1,
the graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}[\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\}]
is a complete graph on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
vertices and in a tree decomposition of a graph, every clique is contained in some bag, we have that the treewidth of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
is the same as the treewidth of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}.

Therefore, a pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,\chi)
is a tree decomposition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
if and only if it is a tree decomposition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}.

5
Definition of our logic

In this section we aim to define our compound logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta.

This is a logic that is evaluated on structures and its definition is based on a series of modifications done in the initial structure and some questions on “parts” of the structure.
To be able to express these modifications, in
Subsection 5.1
, we define some operations on structures.
Then, in
Subsection 5.2
, we show that these operations can be expressed as transductions between structures.
This allows us to do the following: Given a transduction
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf f}
that relates a structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
with the structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf f}(\mathfrak{A}),

we can “back-translate” questions on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf f}(\mathfrak{A})
to questions on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
(
Subsection 5.2
).
This implies that we can express properties of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf f}(\mathfrak{A})
as properties of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}.

Then, in
Subsection 5.3
, we define some classes of formulas that will be used to define our logic.
Our compound logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
is finally defined in
Subsection 5.4
.
Our main result is that model-checking of sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
can be done in quadratic time (
Subsection 5.4
).

5.1
Operations on structures

In this subsection we define some operations on structures.
All operations defined below are applied on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structures, where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
is a vocabulary and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}
is a unary relation symbol not contained in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau.

Intuitively, given a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},
the interpretation of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}
is a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
of elements of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})
(a vertex set, in the case of graphs), which can be considered as elements of a specific “color”.
Having such a “colored” set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X,
we can define the induced substructure of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
with respect to this set, denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ind}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A},X),
and the structure obtained by removing
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
from the universe of the structure, denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf rm}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A},X).

Also, we define an operation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cl}_{\sf X}
(that stands for “cliquing with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}
”), where given a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
and an interpretation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X},
we relate (add an edge, in the case of graphs) every two elements of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})
that are related (adjacent, in the case of graphs) to a common element of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X.

Finally, we define the operation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf star}_{\sf X},
where given a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
and an interpretation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X},
we replace each vertex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
of the connected components of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus X
with a single vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{C}
and we “project” every relation of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
that contains an element of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
to a relation that contains
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{C}
(in the case of graphs, this corresponds to contracting each connected component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X
to a single vertex).

Let us now give a formal definition of the above.
Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary
and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}\notin\texttau
be a unary relation symbol.

Induced structures.

We define the function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ind}_{\sf X}:\mathbb{STR}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]\to\mathbb{STR}[\texttau]
that maps
every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
to the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}[{\sf X}^{\mathfrak{A}}]\upharpoonright{\texttau}.

Substructures.

Also, we define the function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf rm}_{\sf X}:\mathbb{STR}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]\to\mathbb{STR}[\texttau]
that maps
every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
to the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}[V(\mathfrak{A})\setminus{\sf X}^{\mathfrak{A}}]\upharpoonright{\texttau}.

The functions
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cl}_{\sf X}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf star}_{\sf X}.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf E}\notin\texttau
be a binary relation symbol.
We define the function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cl}_{\sf X}:\mathbb{STR}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]\to\mathbb{STR}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf E},{\sf X}\}]

that maps every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
to the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf E},{\sf X}\})
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B},
where

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{B})=V(\mathfrak{A}),

•

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{B}}={\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}},

and

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf E}^{\mathfrak{B}}=\{(x,y)\in V(\mathfrak{B})\mid\exists z\in{\sf X}^{\mathfrak{A}}\ \exists{\sf R}\in\texttau\ \exists{\sf Q}\in\texttau\ \exists{\bf a}\in{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}\ \exists{\bf b}\in{\sf Q}^{\mathfrak{A}}\big(\{x,z\}\subseteq{\bf a}\wedge\{y,z\}\subseteq{\bf b}\big)\},

where by “
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\exists{\bf a}\in{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}
” we mean “
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\exists a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\ (a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\sf ar}({\sf R})})\in{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}
” and by “
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{x,z\}\subseteq{\bf a}
” we mean “
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{x,z\}\subseteq\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{{\sf ar}({\sf R})}\}
”.

For example, in the particular case of graphs (i.e., of structures
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{G}=(V(\mathfrak{G}),{\sf E}^{\mathfrak{G}})
),
for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(\mathfrak{G}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cl}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{G},X)
is the graph obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{G}
after
transforming the neighborhood of every vertex in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
to a clique (see
Figure 3
for an example).

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\color[rgb]{1,0.549,0}X}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\color[rgb]{1,0.549,0}X}

Figure 3
:
Left: The Gaifman graph of a structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{G},X),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}^{\mathfrak{A}}=X.

Right: The Gaifman graph of the structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cl}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{G},X).

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary without constant symbols
and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}\notin\texttau
be a unary relation symbol.
We also define the function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf star}_{\sf X}:\mathbb{STR}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]\to\mathbb{STR}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]

that maps every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
to the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B},
where

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{B})={\sf X}^{\mathfrak{A}}\cup{\sf cc}(G_{\mathfrak{A}},{\sf X}^{\mathfrak{A}}),

•

for every relation symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}m\geq 1,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{B}}=\bigcup_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})\in{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}}\{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\in V(\mathfrak{B})^{m}\mid\forall i\in[m],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle z_{i}=a_{i},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\mbox{ if }a_{i}\in{\sf X}^{\mathfrak{A}},\mbox{\penalty\ while\penalty\ }

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle z_{i}=Y,

if there is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y\in{\sf cc}(G_{\mathfrak{A}},{\sf X}^{\mathfrak{A}})

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\mbox{\ such that $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a_{i}\in Y$}\},\text{ and}

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}^{\mathfrak{B}}={\sf cc}(G_{\mathfrak{A}},{\sf X}^{\mathfrak{A}}).

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}X}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}X}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{C_{1}}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{C_{2}}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{C_{3}}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{2}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{3}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{1}

Figure 4
:
Left: The structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{G},X),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}^{\mathfrak{A}}=X
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{1},C_{2},
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{3}
are the vertex sets of the connected components of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{G}\setminus X.

Right: The structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{G}^{\prime},Y)={\sf star}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{G},X),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y=\{v_{C_{1}},v_{C_{2}},v_{C_{3}}\}.

For example, in the particular case of graphs, given a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{G}

and a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(\mathfrak{G}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf star}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{G},X)
is
the pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{G}^{\prime},Y),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
is the graph obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{G}
after contracting each
connected component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{G}\setminus X
it to a single vertex and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y
is the set of vertices
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
corresponding to the connected components of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{G}\setminus X
(see
Figure 4
for an example).
We avoid to define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf star}_{\sf X}
when
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
contains constant symbols, since we will always apply it to structures without constants.

5.2
Transductions

In this subsection we define (a particular type of) transductions between structures.
The definitions presented here are taken from
[
14
]
(see also
[
35
]
).

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
be two vocabularies without constant symbols
4
4

4

In this paper, we define transductions between structures without constants. We can extend this definition to transductions between structures with constants with the additional “promise” that these transductions do not change the constants.
.
We define a
transduction
with input vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
and output vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
to be
a set of pairs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
-structure.
Given a transduction
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal I}
with input vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
and output vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
and
a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},
we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal I}(\mathfrak{A})
the set
of all
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
-structures
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B})\in{\cal I}.

Notice that a transduction is a binary relation between structures that is not necessarily a function.
All the transductions that we will use in our algorithms,
are
deterministic
, in the sense that
they are partial functions (up to isomorphism).

CMSOL
-transductions.

We now define
CMSOL
-transductions, which are a special case of transductions that can be defined using
CMSOL
.
We begin by defining three types of transductions:

•

Copying.
Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
be a positive integer.
We define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
-copying
to be the transduction with input vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
and output vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma=\texttau\cup\{{\sf copy},{\sf layer}_{1},\ldots,{\sf layer}_{k}\},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf copy}
is a binary relation symbol,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf layer}_{i},i\in[k]
is a unary relation symbol,
and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},
outputs a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B},
where

–

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{B})
is the disjoint union of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
copies of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A}),

–

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
or arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 1,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{B}}
is the set of all
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-tuples over
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{B})
such that all the elements of the tuple are in the same copy of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})
and the original elements of the copies are in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}},

–

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf copy}^{\mathfrak{B}}
is the set of all pairs of elements in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{B})
that are copies of the same element of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A}),
and

–

for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[k],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf layer}_{i}^{\mathfrak{B}}
is the set of all elements that belong to the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i
-th copy of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A}).

•

Coloring.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf C}\notin\texttau
be a unary relation symbol.
We define
coloring
to be the transduction with input vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
and output vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma=\texttau\cup\{{\sf C}\}
that, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
and every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A}),
outputs the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}_{S},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{B})=V(\mathfrak{A}),
for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{B}}={\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}},
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf C}^{\mathfrak{B}}=S.

•

Interpreting.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
be two vocabularies.
We define
interpretation
to be the transduction with input vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
and output vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
as follows:
We consider a family of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau]
formulas

\{\textphi_{\sf dom},\textphi_{\sf univ}\}\cup\{\textphi_{\sf R}\}_{{\sf R}\in\textsigma},

where the formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf dom}
is a sentence (i.e., it has no free variables), the formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf univ}
has one free variable, and each formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf R}
has as many free variables as the arity of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}.

The free variables in the above formulas are first-order variables.
Given a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\textphi_{\sf dom},
the output of the interpretation is the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B},
where

–

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{B})=\{a\in V(\mathfrak{A})\mid\mathfrak{A}\models\textphi_{\sf univ}(a)\}
and

–

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\textsigma
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 1,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{B}}=\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r})\in{V(\mathfrak{B})}^{r}\mid\mathfrak{A}\models\textphi_{\sf R}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r})\}.

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\not\models\textphi_{\sf dom},
then the output of the interpretation is not defined.
Intuitively, the formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf dom}
specifies the
domain
of the interpretation, by “filtering out” all structures that do not satisfy it.
Also, the formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf univ}
defines the
universe
of the structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B},
while the formulas
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf R}
allow us to “interpret” the relation symbols in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma.

A relation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal I}
between
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structures and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
-structures is called an
CMSOL
-transduction with input vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
and output vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
if there exists a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal I}={\cal R}_{k}\circ\ldots\circ{\cal R}_{1},
where, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[k],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal R}_{i}
is a copying/coloring/interpreting between
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau_{i}
-structures and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma_{i}
-structures,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau_{1}=\texttau,
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma_{k}=\textsigma.

The reason why we call the above relations
CMSOL
-transductions is based on the fact that the formulas
we use in the definition of interpretation are formulas in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau].

We can define
FOL
-transductions
analogously,
by demanding that these formulas are
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}
-formulas.
Notice that since every
FOL
-formula is also an
CMSOL
-formula, an
FOL
-transduction is also an
CMSOL
-transduction.

Expressing operations as transductions.

We now prove that all operations defined in
Subsection 5.1
are
CMSOL
-transductions.

Lemma 11
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}\notin\texttau
be a unary relation symbol, and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf E}\notin\texttau
be a binary relation symbol.

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ind}_{\sf X}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf rm}_{\sf X}
are
CMSOL
-transductions from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structures to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structures.

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cl}_{\sf X}
is a
CMSOL
-transduction from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structures to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf E},{\sf X}\})
-structures.

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf star}_{\sf X}
is a
CMSOL
-transduction from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structures to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structures.

•

The function that maps a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure to its Gaifman graph is a
CMSOL
-transduction from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structures to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{{\sf E}\}
-structures.

Proof.

To see why
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ind}_{\sf X}
is a
CMSOL
-transduction
from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structures to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structures, observe that, for every

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},
using the interpretation where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf dom}
is always true,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf univ}({\sf x})={\sf x}\in{\sf X},

and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 1,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf R}({\sf x}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{r})=(({\sf x}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{r})\in{\sf R}),

we get the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}[{\sf X}^{\mathfrak{A}}]\upharpoonright{\texttau}.

We next argue why
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cl}_{\sf X}
is a
CMSOL
-transduction
from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structures to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf E},{\sf X}\})
-structures.
Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
be a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structure. To obtain

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}={\sf cl}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A}),
we use interpretation, where

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf dom},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf univ}({\sf x})
are always true,

•

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 1,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf R}({\sf x}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{r})=(({\sf x}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{r})\in{\sf R}),

and

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf E}({\sf x},{\sf y})=\exists{\sf z}\in{\sf X}\ \bigvee_{{\sf R},{\sf Q}\in\texttau}\exists{\sf a}_{1},\ldots,{\sf a}_{{\sf ar}({\sf R})}\ \exists{\sf b}_{1},\ldots,{\sf b}_{{\sf ar}(Q)}\Big(\big(({\sf a}_{1},\ldots,{\sf a}_{{\sf ar}({\sf R})})\in{\sf R}\ \wedge({\sf b}_{1},\ldots,{\sf b}_{{\sf ar}(Q)})\in Q\big)\to\big(\{{\sf x},{\sf z}\}\subseteq\{{\sf a}_{1},\ldots,{\sf a}_{{\sf ar}({\sf R})}\}\wedge\{{\sf y},{\sf z}\}\subseteq\{{\sf b}_{1},\ldots,{\sf b}_{{\sf ar}(Q)}\}\big)\Big).

We will prove that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf star}_{\sf X}
is a
CMSOL
-transduction from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structures to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structures.
Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
be a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structure. To obtain
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}={\sf star}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A}),

we first use coloring and add a new unary predicate
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf U}
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
and guess an interpretation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}U
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf U}
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A}),
which corresponds to a choice of representatives, one for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C\in{\sf cc}(G_{\mathfrak{A}},{\sf X}^{\mathfrak{A}}).

We call
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
this new
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X},{\sf U}\})
-structure.
Then, we use interpretation to transform
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B},
by setting
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf dom}
to be always true,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf univ}({\sf x})=({\sf x}\in{\sf X}\vee{\sf x}\in{\sf U}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{{\sf X}}({\sf x})=({\sf x}\in{\sf U}),
and, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 1,

\textphi_{{\sf R}}({\sf x}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{r})=\exists{\sf a}_{1},\ldots,{\sf a}_{r}\Big(({\sf a}_{1},\ldots,{\sf a}_{r})\in{\sf R}\ \wedge\bigwedge_{i\in[r]}\big(({\sf x}_{i}={\sf a}_{i}\wedge{\sf a}_{i}\in{\sf X})\vee({\sf x}_{i}={\sf U}\wedge{\sf a}_{i}\in{\sf U})\big)\Big).

To see how to obtain the Gaifman graph of a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure through a
CMSOL
-transduction, consider the interpretation where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf dom}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf univ}({\sf x})
are always true and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf E}(x,y)
is the formula that checks whether there is an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}x,y
belong simultaneously to some tuple in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}.

∎

The following result allows us to translate a question in one structure to an “equivalent” question in another structure through
CMSOL
-transductions. It is known as the
Backwards Translation Theorem

[
35
, Theorem 1.40]
(see also
[
14
, Lemma B.1]
).
We state it for sentences, i.e., formulas without free variables.

Proposition 12
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal L}
be either
CMSOL
or
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
be vocabularies without constant symbols.
Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal I}
be an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal L}
-transduction with input vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
and output vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma.

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi
is a sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal L}[\textsigma],

then there is a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi\in{\cal L}[\texttau]
such that for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B},
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}\in{\cal I}(\mathfrak{A})
for some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
