, it holds that

\mathfrak{A}\models\textpsi\iff\mathfrak{B}\models\textphi.

We now state the following result. Intuitively, it says that in the case of structures whose Gaifman graphs have bounded Hadwiger number, one can transduce the original structure from its Gaifman graph. This was proved in a more general setting in
[
19
, Lemma 3.1]
for the case where the Gaifman graphs have bounded star chromatic number, a property satisfied in classes of bounded expansion such as classes of bounded Hadwiger number.

Proposition 13
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary without constant symbols, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf E}\notin\texttau
be a binary relation symbol, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal C}\subseteq\mathbb{STR}[\texttau]
.
There is an
FOL
-transduction
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal I}
from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf E}
-structures to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structures
such that if all graphs in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{G_{\mathfrak{A}}\mid\mathfrak{A}\in{\cal C}\}
have Hadwiger number at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
, then, if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G=G_{\mathfrak{A}}
for some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\in{\cal C}
, it holds that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal I}(G)=\mathfrak{A}
.

At this point, we should comment that, in
Subsection 5.4
, we prove that the Gaifman graph of every structure that is a model of a formula in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
has bounded Hadwiger number (
Subsection 5.4
).
Therefore, due to
Subsection 5.2
, we can transduce every structure that is a model of a formula in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
from its Gaifman graph.
This, in turn, together with
Subsection 5.2
, the fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta\subseteq\mbox{\sf CMSOL},
and the observation that any
FOL
-transduction is also an
CMSOL
-transduction, indicates that
the problem of model-checking for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
in general structures is essentially not more general than in graphs.

Combining
Subsection 5.2
and
Subsection 5.2
, we get the following result.

Corollary 14
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary without constant symbols,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}\notin\texttau
be a unary relation
symbol, and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf E}\notin\texttau
be a binary relation symbol.

•

For every sentence

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau],

there is a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi|_{{\sf ind}_{\sf X}}\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]

such that for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},
it holds that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\textpsi|_{{\sf ind}_{\sf X}}\iff{\sf ind}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A})\models\textpsi,

•

for every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau],

there is a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi|_{{\sf rm}_{\sf X}}\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]

such that for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},
it holds that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\textpsi|_{{\sf rm}_{\sf X}}\iff{\sf rm}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A})\models\textpsi,

•

for every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf E},{\sf X}\}],

there is a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi|_{{\sf cl}_{\sf X}}\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]

such that for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},
it holds that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\textpsi|_{{\sf cl}_{\sf X}}\iff{\sf cl}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A})\models\textpsi,

•

for every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau],

there is a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi|_{{\sf star}_{\sf X}}\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]

such that for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},
it holds that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\textpsi|_{{\sf star}_{\sf X}}\iff{\sf star}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A})\models\textpsi,
and

•

for every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\{{\sf E}\}],

there is a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi|_{\sf gf}\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau]

such that for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},
it holds that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\textpsi|_{\sf gf}\iff G_{\mathfrak{A}}\models\textpsi.

Later in the paper, we will apply the transduction
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ind}_{\sf X}
to structures with constants (i.e., structures whose vocabulary contains constant symbols). In this case,
Subsection 5.2
cannot be applied to obtain a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi|_{{\sf ind}_{\sf X}}\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]
from a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau].
However, we can directly set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi|_{{\sf ind}_{\sf X}}
to be the sentence obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi
after replacing, for each first-order variable, every occurrence of “
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\exists/\forall\ {\sf x}
” with “
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\exists/\forall\ {\sf x}\in{\sf X}^{\mathfrak{A}}
” and, for each set variable
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y,
every occurrence of “
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\exists/\forall\ {\sf Y}
” with “
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\exists/\forall\ {\sf Y}\subseteq{\sf X}^{\mathfrak{A}}
”.

We define the operation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\triangleright
as follows.
Given
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau],
we define

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma:=\exists{\sf X}\ \textbeta|_{{\sf star}_{\sf X}}\wedge\textgamma|_{{\sf rm}_{\sf X}}.

(4)

As a byproduct of
Subsection 5.2
, we get the following.

Corollary 15
.

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau],
then

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau].

5.3
Classes of formulas

In this subsection we define several classes of formulas that will be used in the definition of our logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
in
Subsection 5.4
.

The class
EM
.

We say that a class
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal C}
of structures is
minor-closed
if the graph class
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{G_{\mathfrak{A}}\mid\mathfrak{A}\in{\cal C}\}
is minor-closed.

The following lemma asserts that minor-exclusion can be expressed in
CMSOL
.
Its proof is a direct implication of
[
35
, Corollary 1.14]
(see also
[
95
, Appendix D]
for an explicit
CMSOL
-formula for the case of topological minors).

Lemma 16
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
be a graph and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal F}
be a finite family of graphs.
There is a
CMSOL
-sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu_{\cal F}
that is evaluated on graphs such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\models\textmu_{\cal F}\iff G\in{\sf excl}({\cal F}).

Combining
Subsection 5.2
and
Subsection 5.3
, we get the following:

Corollary 17
.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
and every finite family
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal F}
of graphs, there is a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu_{\cal F}|_{\sf gf}\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau]
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\textmu_{\cal F}|_{\sf gf}\iff G_{\mathfrak{A}}\in{\sf excl}({\cal F}).

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau].
We say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\rm Mod}(\textphi)
is
trivial
if it contains all
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structures.
We denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf EM}[\texttau]
the class of all sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau]
such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\rm Mod}(\textmu)
is proper minor-closed.
Notice that for every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu\in{\sf EM}[\texttau],
because of Robertson-Seymour’s theorem
[
126
]
and based on
Subsection 5.3
, there is some positive integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c_{\textmu}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{G_{\mathfrak{A}}\mid\mathfrak{A}\in{\rm Mod}(\textmu)\}\subseteq{\sf excl}(K_{c_{\textmu}}).

CMSOL
-sentence of bounded treewidth.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary without constant symbols and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}\notin\texttau
be a unary relation symbol.
We say that a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]
has

bounded treewidth
if there is a constant
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c_{\textbeta}
such that
all
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\})
-structures in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\rm Mod}(\textbeta_{\sf cl}),
that is, in the set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{{\sf cl}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A},{\sf X})\mid(\mathfrak{A},{\sf X})\in{\rm Mod}(\textbeta)\},

have treewidth at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c_{\textbeta}.
We denote by
CMSOL

{}^{\bf tw}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]
the set of all
sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]
that have bounded treewidth.

Connected component closure of a formula.

We first observe that, given a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
and a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A}),

the fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
is the vertex set of a connected component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}
can be expressed in
CMSOL
.

Observation 18
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}\notin\texttau
be a unary relation symbol.
There is a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textkappa_{\sf X}\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]
such that for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},C)\in\mathbb{STR}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]
(where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}
is interpreted as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
),
it holds that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},C)\models\textkappa_{\sf X}\iff C
is the vertex set of a connected component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}.

We consider the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textkappa_{\sf X}\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]
as in
Subsection 5.3
.
For every formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau],
we define the formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi^{({\sf c})}
as

\textphi^{({\sf c})}=\forall{\sf X}\ \textkappa_{\sf X}\to\textphi|_{{\sf ind}_{\sf X}}.

Recall that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi|_{{\sf ind}_{\sf X}}
is a formula in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]
and note that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi^{({\sf c})}\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau].

Alternatively, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},

\mathfrak{A}\models\textphi^{({\sf c})}\iff\mbox{for every $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C\in{\sf cc}(G_{\mathfrak{A}},\emptyset)$ it holds that\penalty\ }\mathfrak{A}[C]\models\textphi.

Given a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal L}\subseteq\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau],
we define

{\cal L}^{({\sf c})}={\cal L}\cup\{\textphi^{({\sf c})}\mid\textphi\in{\cal L}\}.

Boolean combination of formulas.

Given a set of formulas
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal L}\subseteq\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau],
for some vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau,
we define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf PB}({\cal L})
as the set of all positive Boolean combinations of formulas in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal L},
i.e., all formulas constructed from formulas in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal L}
using the Boolean connectives
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\vee
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\wedge.

5.4
Our compound logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta

We are now in position to define our compound logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
for general structures. First, we define the intermediate logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}.

The intermediate logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary.
Recall that, given a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]
and a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma
is defined as the sentence

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\exists{\sf X}\ \textbeta|_{{\sf star}_{\sf X}}\wedge\textgamma|_{{\sf rm}_{\sf X}}
(see
Equation 4
).
We now recursively define, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in\mathbb{N},
the subclass
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}_{i}[\texttau]
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau]
so that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\bar{\textTheta}_{0}[\texttau]

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\{\textsigma\wedge\textmu\mid\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau]\mbox{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\textmu\in{\sf EM}[\texttau]\}\mbox{\penalty\ and}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\mbox{for $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\geq 1,$ }\bar{\textTheta}_{i}[\texttau]

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\{\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma\mid\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\bf tw}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]\mbox{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\textgamma\in{\bar{\textTheta}_{i-1}[\texttau]}^{({\sf c})}\}

On a semantical level, a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau]
belongs to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}_{i}[\texttau],i>0,
if and only if
there exists a

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\bf tw}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]
and a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma\in{\bar{\textTheta}_{i-1}[\texttau]}^{({\sf c})}

such that, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},

\mbox{there exists some set }X\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A})\mbox{\penalty\ such that\penalty\ }{\sf star}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A},X)\models\textbeta\mbox{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\mathfrak{A}\setminus X\models\textgamma.

We stress that, since
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\bf tw}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}],
the fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf star}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A},X)\models\textbeta
implies that there is a constant
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c_{\textbeta}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},
such that the structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cl}_{\sf X}({\sf star}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A},X))
has treewidth at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c_{\textbeta}.

See
Figure 5
for the Gaifman graph of a structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},X)
and the Gaifman graph of the structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cl}_{\sf X}({\sf star}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A},X)).

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}X}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}X}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{C_{1}}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{C_{2}}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{C_{3}}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{1}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{2}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{3}

Figure 5
:
Left: The Gaifman graph of a structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},X),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{1},C_{2},
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{3}
are the vertex sets of the connected components of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus X.

Right: The Gaifman graph of the structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cl}_{\sf X}({\sf star}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A},X)).

Notice that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}_{0}[\texttau]\subseteq\bar{\textTheta}_{1}[\texttau]\subseteq\bar{\textTheta}_{2}[\texttau]\subseteq\cdots.

We also set

\bar{\textTheta}[\texttau]=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\bar{\textTheta}_{i}[\texttau].

The compound logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\textTheta}.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary.
We now recursively define, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in\mathbb{N},
the subclass
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{i}[\texttau]
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau]
so that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\textTheta_{0}[\texttau]

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\{\textsigma\wedge\textmu\mid\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau]\mbox{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\textmu\in{\sf EM}[\texttau]\}\mbox{\penalty\ and}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\mbox{for $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\geq 1,$ }\textTheta_{i}[\texttau]

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\{\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma\mid\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\bf tw}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]\mbox{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\textgamma\in{\bf PB}(\textTheta_{i-1}[\texttau]^{({\sf c})})\}.

Notice that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{0}\subseteq\textTheta_{1}\subseteq\textTheta_{2}\subseteq\cdots.

We also set

\textTheta=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\textTheta_{i}.

We stress that the difference between
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
is that in the former we ask that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma\in{\bar{\textTheta}_{i-1}[\texttau]}^{({\sf c})},
while in the latter we ask that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma\in{\bf PB}(\textTheta_{i-1}[\texttau]^{({\sf c})}).

Observe that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}\subseteq\textTheta.

For every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in{\textTheta}[\texttau],
we call every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau]
used in the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
a
FOL
-target sentence
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
and every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu\in{\sf EM}[\texttau]
used the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
an
EM
-target sentence
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

Also, every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\bf tw}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]
used in the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
is called a
modulator sentence
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

We are now in position to state our main result using structures instead of graphs.
Recall that in the introduction we mentioned the version of this theorem on graphs, that is when
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau=\{{\sf E}\}
(
Subsection 1.2
).

Theorem 19
.

For every vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
and every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta[\texttau],

there exists an algorithm that, given a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\in\mathbb{STR}[\texttau]
, outputs whether
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\texttheta

in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{|\texttheta|}(n^{2})
.

As we mentioned in the introduction, we may also define the extension
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\textTheta}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
, by setting

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\textTheta}_{0}[\texttau]={\sf FOL}[\texttau]
as the base case. The following is an easy corollary of
Subsection 5.4
, that is the version of
Subsection 1.2
stated on structures.

Theorem 20
.

For every vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
and every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}\in\tilde{\textTheta}[\texttau],

there exists an algorithm that, given a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\in\mathbb{STR}[\texttau]
, outputs whether
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\tilde{\texttheta}

in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{|\texttheta|,{\bf hw}(G_{\mathfrak{A}})}(n^{2})
.

To see this how
Subsection 5.4
follows from
Subsection 5.4
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\{{\sf E}\}]

express minor exclusion of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{{\bf hw}(G)}
and enhance all target
FOL
-sentences of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}\in\tilde{\textTheta}

by adding on them the conjunction with the
EM
-target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu
. As this creates some new sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta
where

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\tilde{\texttheta}\iff\mathfrak{A}\models{\texttheta}
,
Subsection 5.4
follows as a consequence of
Subsection 5.4
.

Quantifying the sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
.

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta,
then we define the
height
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,

denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf height}(\texttheta),
as the minimum
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta_{i}.

We define the
treewidth
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,
denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf tw}(\texttheta),

as the maximum
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c_{\textbeta}
over all modulator sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

Also we define the
Hadwiger number
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,
denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf hw}(\texttheta),

as the maximum
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c_{\textmu}
over all
EM
-target sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

We prove that for every model of a sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta,
its Gaifman graph excludes some “big enough” clique as a minor.

Lemma 21
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary.
For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta[\texttau]
and every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\in{\rm Mod}(\texttheta),
it holds that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}\in{\sf excl}(\{K_{c}\}),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c={\bf hw}(\texttheta)+({\bf tw}(\texttheta)+1)\cdot{\sf height}(\texttheta).

Proof.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta[\texttau]
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
be a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\in{\rm Mod}(\texttheta).

We will prove that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}\in{\sf excl}(\{K_{c}\}),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c={\bf hw}(\texttheta)+({\bf tw}(\texttheta)+1)\cdot h,

by induction on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h.

In the case where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h=0,
by definition there exist
a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau]
and a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu\in{\sf EM}[\texttau]
such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta=\textsigma\wedge\textmu.

The fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\in{\rm Mod}(\texttheta)

implies that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\textsigma\wedge\textmu
and therefore, by
Subsection 5.3
,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}\in{\sf excl}(\{K_{c_{\textmu}}\}).

Also, by the assumption that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta_{0},
we have that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf hw}(\texttheta)=c_{\textmu}.

Suppose now that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h\geq 1
and assume that the lemma holds for all sentences of smaller height.
In this case, there is a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\bf tw}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}],
an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},
some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k_{1},\ldots,k_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},
and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[\ell]
and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j\in[k_{i}]
there is a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i,j}\in\textTheta_{h-1}[\texttau]^{({\sf c})}
such that

\texttheta=\exists{\sf X}\ \textbeta|_{{\sf star}_{\sf X}}\wedge\Big((\texttheta_{1,1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\texttheta_{1,k_{1}})\vee\ldots\vee(\texttheta_{\ell,1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\texttheta_{\ell,k_{\ell}})\Big)|_{{\sf rm}_{\sf X}}.

Suppose now that, given that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\texttheta
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c={\bf hw}(\texttheta)+({\bf tw}(\texttheta)+1)\cdot h,
we have that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{c}\preceq_{\sf m}G_{\mathfrak{A}}.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}M
be a minor-model of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{c}
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}},
i.e., a subgraph of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}
that can be transformed to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{c}
after a series of edge contractions.
We call
bag
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}M
the vertex set of every connected subgraph of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}M
whose edges are contracted in order to obtain a vertex of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{c}.

The fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\texttheta
implies that
there is a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A})

such that

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{{\sf star}_{\sf X}}(\mathfrak{A},X)\models\textbeta
and

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{{\sf rm}_{\sf X}}(\mathfrak{A},X)\models(\texttheta_{1,1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\texttheta_{1,k_{1}})\vee\ldots\vee(\texttheta_{\ell,1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\texttheta_{\ell,k_{\ell}}).

Since
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{{\sf star}_{\sf X}}(\mathfrak{A},X)\models\textbeta,
we know that the treewidth of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cl}_{\sf X}({{\sf star}_{\sf X}}(\mathfrak{A},X))
is at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c_{\textbeta}.

Observe that this implies that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S
intersects at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c_{\textbeta}+1
bags of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}M.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}M^{\star}
to be the graph induced by the union of all bags in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}M
that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
does not intersect.
Note that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}M^{\star}
is a minor-model of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{c-(c_{\textbeta}+1)}.

We now set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}:={\sf rm}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A},X).

The fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}\models(\texttheta_{1,1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\texttheta_{1,k_{1}})\vee\ldots\vee(\texttheta_{\ell,1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\texttheta_{\ell,k_{\ell}})
implies that
there is an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[\ell]
such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}\models\texttheta_{i,1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\texttheta_{i,k_{i}}.

Observe also that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}M^{\star}
is a subgraph of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{B}}
and recall that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}M^{\star}
is a minor-model of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{c-(c_{\textbeta}+1)}.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t:=\max\{{\bf tw}(\texttheta_{i,1}),\ldots,{\bf tw}(\texttheta_{i,k_{i}})\},
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j\in[k_{i}]
such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf tw}(\texttheta_{i,j})=t,
and let

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}m:=\max\{{\bf hw}(\texttheta_{i,1}),\ldots,{\bf hw}(\texttheta_{i,k_{i}})\}.

Since
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf tw}(\texttheta)\geq\max\{t,c_{\textbeta}\}
and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf hw}(\texttheta)\geq m,
it holds that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c-(c_{\textbeta}+1)={\bf hw}(\texttheta)+({\bf tw}(\texttheta)+1)\cdot h-(c_{\textbeta}+1)\geq m+({\bf tw}(\texttheta_{i,j})+1)\cdot(h-1)\geq{\bf hw}(\texttheta_{i,j})+({\bf tw}(\texttheta_{i,j})+1)\cdot(h-1).

We set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q:={\bf hw}(\texttheta_{i,j})+({\bf tw}(\texttheta_{i,j})+1)\cdot(h-1)
and observe that since
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{c-(c_{\textbeta}+1)}\preceq_{\sf m}G_{\mathfrak{B}}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c-(c_{\textbeta}+1)\geq q,
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{q}\preceq_{\sf m}G_{\mathfrak{B}}.

But
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i,j}\in\textTheta_{h-1}^{({\sf c})}
and by induction hypothesis, we have that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{B}}\in{\sf excl}(\{K_{q}\}),
a contradiction.
∎

6
An annotated version of the problem

In this section we aim to define an enhanced version of every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta
.
This is done in two steps.

In
Subsection 6.1
, we focus on “neutralizing” a tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
of elements of a structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
and transforming a question on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
to a question on the structure obtained after “neutralizing”
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
(
Subsection 6.1
).
We will apply this tool under the existence of an apex set and a flat wall in the Gaifman graph of our structure, in order to “neutralize” the apex set and
ask the final
FOL
-question of our sentence in a “flattened” structure, where apices can no longer “bring close” any distant parts of the wall. This transformation of the problem will allow the application of the “locality-based” strategy that uses Gaifman’s locality theorem.

In
Subsection 6.2
we define an enhanced version of the problem, by replacing, in a given
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta[\texttau],

each
FOL
-target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
with the sentence obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
after
(i) “projecting” it with respect to a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
of constant symbols
(using the definitions in
Subsection 6.1
),
(ii) taking a Gaifman equivalent sentence of the obtained sentence,
and (iii) requiring that the “scattered” variables of the basic local sentences of the Gaifman sentence belong to an annotated set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R.

We prove that the initial sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
and the obtained sentence, denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}},

are “equivalent” for any choice of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
interpreting
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
and when
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}
is interpreted as the whole universe of the given structure (
Subsection 6.2
).
Our algorithms will work with the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

6.1
Dealing with apices

In this subsection
we introduce all necessary tools to handle the (possible) apices in the Gaifman graph of the input structure.
As we mentioned in the overview (see
Section 2
),
apices are an obstacle to the locality arguments needed for the part of the proof that concerns
FOL
.
To be able to work in a “flat” graph, without the presence of the apices that possibly connect “distant” parts of the graph, we introduce an
apex-projection
of our structure and the corresponding
apex-projection
of a given
FOL
-sentence.
This construction is an extension to general structures of a trick in
[
52
]
(that deals with graphs)
and gives an equivalent sentence (see
Subsection 6.1
).
Finally, we express this transformation in terms of
FOL
-transductions (see
Subsection 6.1
) and by
Subsection 5.2
,
we obtain a “backwards translation” of the latter question to a questions in structures of the form
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})
(see
Subsection 6.1
).

Apex-tuples of structures.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
be a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure, and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l\in\mathbb{N}.

A tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l})
where each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a_{i}
is either an element of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})
or
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\varnothing
,
is called an
apex-tuple
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l.

We use
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V({\bf a})
for the set containing the non-
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\varnothing

elements in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}.

Also, if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A}),
we define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}\cap S=(a_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,a_{l}^{\prime})

so that if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a_{i}\in S,
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a_{i}^{\prime}=a_{i},
and otherwise
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a_{i}^{\prime}=\varnothing.

We also define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}\setminus S={\bf a}\cap(V(\mathfrak{A})\setminus S).

Given an apex-tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l,
we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}^{\star}
the tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(a_{1}^{\star},\ldots,a_{|V({\bf a})|}^{\star}),
where for each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[|V({\bf a})|],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a_{i}^{\star}
is the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i
-th non-
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\varnothing
element of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
(intuitively,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}^{\star}
can be seen as the substring of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
after removing every occurrence of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\varnothing
).

Constant-projections of vocabularies.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l\in\mathbb{N},
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}=\{{\sf c}_{1},\ldots,{\sf c}_{l}\}
be a collection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
constant symbols that are not contained in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau.

We define the
constant-projection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup{\bf c})
to be the vocabulary obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup{\bf c})
as follows:
For each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 2,
we consider a collection

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal R}_{\sf R}=\{{\sf R}_{r},{\sf R}_{r-1},\ldots,{\sf R}_{1}\}
of relation symbols,
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}_{i}
has arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i,

a collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal R}_{\sf R}^{({\sf ap})}=\{{\sf R}_{r}^{({\sf ap})},{\sf R}_{r-1}^{({\sf ap})},\ldots,{\sf R}_{1}^{({\sf ap})}\}
of relation symbols,
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}_{i}^{({\sf ap})}
has arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i,
and a collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal Y}_{\sf R}=\{Y_{\sf R}^{(1)},\ldots,Y_{\sf R}^{(l)}\}
of unary relation symbols.
We set

\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}:={\bf c}\cup\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\{{\cal R}_{\sf R}\cup{\cal R}^{({\sf ap})}_{\sf R}\cup{\cal Y}_{\sf R}\mid{\sf R}\in\texttau\text{ and has arity at least two}\}\cup\{{\sf R}\mid{\sf R}\in\texttau\text{ and has arity one}\}.

Projecting a structure with respect to an apex-tuple.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l\in\mathbb{N},
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}=\{{\sf c}_{1},\ldots,{\sf c}_{l}\}
be a collection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
constant symbols that are not contained in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau.

Let also
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}
be the constant-projection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup{\bf c}).

Given a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup{\bf c})
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a}),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l})
is an apex-tuple of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
and, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[l]
,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}_{i}^{(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})}=a_{i},

we define the structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})
to be the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}
-structure obtained as follows:

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V({\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a}))=V(\mathfrak{A}),

•

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[l]

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}_{i}^{{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})}=a_{i},

•

every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity one is interpreted in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})}
as in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\mathfrak{A}},

•

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 2
and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j\in[r],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}_{j}
is interpreted in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})
as the set

{\{{\bf x}\in{V({\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a}))}^{j}\mid\exists{\bf y}\in{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}\mbox{ such that }({\bf y}\setminus V({\bf a}))^{\star}={\bf x}\},}

•

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 2
and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j\in[r],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}_{j}^{({\sf ap})}
is interpreted in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})
as the set

\{{\bf x}\in{V({\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a}))}^{j}\mid\exists{\bf y}\in{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}\mbox{ such that }({\bf y}\cap V({\bf a}))^{\star}={\bf x}\},\text{ and}

•

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 2
and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[l],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y_{\sf R}^{(i)}
is interpreted in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})
as the set

\{v\in V(\mathfrak{A})\mid v\notin V({\bf a})\mbox{ and }\exists{\bf x}\in{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}\mbox{ such that }a_{i},v\in V({\bf x})\}.

Notice that if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a_{i}=\varnothing,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y_{\sf R}^{(i)}
is interpreted in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})
as the empty set.

Intuitively, given a structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})
is obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})
after “coloring” with the color
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y_{\sf R}^{(i)}
every element of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})\setminus V({\bf a})
that is related (in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}
) to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a_{i}
(or, adjacent to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a_{i},
in the case of graphs) and after “restricting” every relation on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
to the set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})\setminus V({\bf a}).
For the latter, we correspond each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 2
to a collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}_{1},\ldots,{\sf R}_{r}
of relation symbols that are interpreted as the “restricted” tuples in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}.

In other words, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[r],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}_{i}
is interpreted as the set of all
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i
-tuples of elements of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})
that are obtained from an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-tuple in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}
after removing from it all elements in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V({\bf a})
(that are eventually
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(l-i)
-many). Similarly, we correspond each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 2
to a collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}_{1}^{({\sf ap})},\ldots,{\sf R}_{r}^{({\sf ap})}
of relation symbols that are interpreted as the restriction of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}^{\mathfrak{A}}
to the set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V({\bf a}).

It is crucial to observe that the Gaifman graph of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})
is a subgraph of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}.

In fact,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})}
is obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}
after removing every edge that is between a vertex in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V({\bf a})
and a vertex in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})\setminus V({\bf a}).
This removal permits us
to deal with “flat structures” that are amenable to the application of Gaifman’s Theorem.

Apex-projected sentences.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf x}
be a tuple of variables of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j\in[r],
we define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal A}_{j}({\bf x})
to be the set of all possible partitions of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf x}
to two subtuples
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf z},{\bf w}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r-j,
respectively.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l\in\mathbb{N},
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}=\{{\sf c}_{1},\ldots,{\sf c}_{l}\}
be a collection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
constant symbols that are not contained in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau.

For every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau],
we define its
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
-apex-projected sentence

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma^{l}
to be the sentence obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
by replacing, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 2,
every term
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf x}\in{\sf R},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf x}
is a tuple of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r,
by

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\bigvee_{j\in[r]}\bigvee_{({\bf z},{\bf w})\in{\cal A}_{j}({\bf x})}\Bigg({\bf z}\in{\sf R}_{j}\wedge{\bf w}\in{\sf R}_{r-j}^{({\sf ap})}\wedge\bigvee_{{\bf t}\in[l]^{(r-j)}}\Big(\bigwedge_{i\in[r-j]}\big({\sf w}_{i}={\sf c}_{t_{i}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\sf z}\in{\bf z}}{\sf z}\in Y_{\sf R}^{(t_{i})}\big)\Big)\Bigg).

To get some intuition of the meaning of the above sentence,
notice that the part “
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bigvee_{j\in[r]}\bigvee_{({\bf z},{\bf w})\in{\cal A}_{j}({\bf x})}
”
corresponds to some “guessing” of a subtuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf z}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf x}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j.

The tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf z}
corresponds to the tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}({\bf x}\setminus V({\bf a}))^{\star},

while the tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf w}
corresponds to the tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}({\bf x}\cap V({\bf a}))^{\star}.

Therefore, in “
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf z}\in{\sf R}_{j}
”, we ask that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf z}
belongs to the “projection” of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}
away from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a},

while in “
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf w}\in{\sf R}_{r-j}^{({\sf ap})}
” we ask that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf w}
belongs to the “projection” of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}
inside
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}.

We then guess a tuple of apices in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V({\bf a})
(this corresponds to “
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bigvee_{{\bf t}\in[l]^{(r-j)}}
”).
Having these guessed apices,
for each one of them (“
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bigwedge_{i\in[r-j]}
”) we ask that the order of the elements in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf w}
coincides with the order of the guessed apices in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
(“
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf w}_{i}={\sf c}_{t_{i}}
”)
and
in “
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bigwedge_{{\sf z}\in{\bf z}}{\sf z}\in Y_{\sf R}^{(t_{i})}
” we ask that all the elements in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf z}
are colored with the color corresponding to each guessed apex.

The definition of the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
-apex-projected sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma^{l}
together with the above discussion imply the following lemma, which can be seen as a generalization to general structures
of
[
52
, Lemma 26]
that deals with graphs.

Lemma 22
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l\in\mathbb{N},
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
be a collection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
constant symbols.
For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau],
every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},
and every apex-tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l,
it holds that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\textsigma\iff{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})\models\textsigma^{l}
(where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
is interpreted as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
).

Backwards translating an apex-projected sentence.

We now aim to prove that
given a vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau,
an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l\in\mathbb{N},
a collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
constant symbols, and a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau],

we can find a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma^{\prime}\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau\cup{\bf c}]
such that
for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
and every apex-tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})\models\textsigma^{\prime}\iff{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})\models\textsigma^{l}.

For this reason, we first prove that the function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ap}_{\bf c}
is an
FOL
-transduction
and we then use
Subsection 5.2
to obtain the desired sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma^{\prime}
(see
Subsection 6.1
).
We stress that, in
Subsection 5.2
, we avoided to define transductions as relations between structures of vocabularies with constant symbols, for the sake of simplicity.
In our current case, we slightly abuse the definition of transductions and allow constant symbols, since
the function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ap}_{\bf c}
leaves the interpretation of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
intact and therfore we can safely extend the definition of transduction and the statement of
Subsection 5.2
to capture this case.
We refer the reader to
[
35
, Section 7.1.2]
for a discussion on transductions between structures with constants.

Lemma 23
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l\in\mathbb{N},
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
be a collection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
constant symbols, and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}
be the constant-projection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau\cup{\bf c}.

The function that maps every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup{\bf c})
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})
to the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})
is an
FOL
-transduction.
Moreover, there is an
FOL
-transduction from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau\cup{\bf c}
that maps
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})
, if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}
has bounded Hadwiger number.

Proof.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})
be a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup{\bf c})
-structure.
We will describe an interpretation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal I}
with input vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau\cup{\bf c}
and output vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle},
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal I}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})={\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a}).

To define this interpretation, we have to provide the formulas

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{\textphi_{\sf dom},\textphi_{\sf univ}\}\cup\{\textphi_{\sf R}\}_{{\sf R}\in\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}}.

The formulas that we give are formulas of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}[\texttau\cup{\bf c}],
and therefore
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal I}
is an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}
-transduction.

First, we define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf dom}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf univ}({\sf x})
to be two formulas in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}[\texttau\cup{\bf c}]
that are always true.
Then, it remains to define, for each relation symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle},

a formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf R}
(the interpretation of the constants remains unchanged).

Recall that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}={\bf c}\cup\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\{{\cal R}_{\sf R}\cup{\cal R}^{({\sf ap})}_{\sf R}\cup{\cal Y}_{\sf R}\mid{\sf R}\in\texttau\text{ and has arity $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 2$}\}\cup\{{\sf R}\mid{\sf R}\in\texttau\text{ and has arity one}\}
and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 2,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal R}_{\sf R}=\{{\sf R}_{r},{\sf R}_{r-1},\ldots,{\sf R}_{1}\}
is a collection of relation symbols,
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}_{i}
has arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal R}_{\sf R}^{({\sf ap})}=\{{\sf R}_{r}^{({\sf ap})},{\sf R}_{r-1}^{({\sf ap})},\ldots,{\sf R}_{1}^{({\sf ap})}\}
is a collection of relation symbols,
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}_{i}^{({\sf ap})}
has arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i,
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal Y}_{\sf R}=\{Y_{\sf R}^{(1)},\ldots,Y_{\sf R}^{(l)}\}
is a collection of unary relation symbols.

First, for each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity one, we set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{\sf R}({\sf x}):=({\sf x}\in{\sf R}).

Let now
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\in\texttau
of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 2.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j\in[r],
we define

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{{\sf R}_{j}}({\sf x}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{j})
to be the formula that checks whether there exist
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf y}_{1},\ldots,{\sf y}_{r}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}({\sf y}_{1},\ldots,{\sf y}_{r})\in{\sf R}
and whether there is a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I\subseteq[r]
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j
such that
for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\notin I,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf y}_{i}
is equal to some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf c}\in{\bf c}
and if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}\}
then for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k\in[j],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf x}_{k}={\sf y}_{i_{k}}.

More formally,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\textphi_{{\sf R}_{j}}({\sf x}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{j})

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle:=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\exists{\sf y}_{1},\ldots,{\sf y}_{r}(({\sf y}_{1},\ldots,{\sf y}_{r})\in{\sf R})\wedge

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{all subsets}\\
I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}\}\\
\text{of $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}[r]$ of size $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j$}\end{subarray}}\Bigg(\Big(i\notin I\to(\exists{\sf c}\in{\bf c}\ {\sf y}_{i}={\sf c})\Big)\wedge\bigwedge_{k\in[j]}{\sf x}_{i}={\sf y}_{i_{k}}\Bigg).

Also, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j\in[r],
we define

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{{\sf R}_{j}^{({\sf ap})}}({\sf x}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{j})
to be the formula that
checks whether there exist
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf y}_{1},\ldots,{\sf y}_{r}

and whether there is a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I\subseteq[r]
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j
such that for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\notin I,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf y}_{i}\notin{\bf c}
and
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}\}
then for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k\in[j],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf x}_{k}={\sf y}_{i_{k}}.

More formally,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\textphi_{{\sf R}_{j}^{({\sf ap})}}({\sf x}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{j})

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle:=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\exists{\sf y}_{1},\ldots,{\sf y}_{r}(({\sf y}_{1},\ldots,{\sf y}_{r})\in{\sf R})\wedge

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{all subsets}\\
I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}\}\\
\text{of $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}[r]$ of size $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j$}\end{subarray}}\Bigg(\Big(i\notin I\to\neg(\exists{\sf c}\in{\bf c}\ {\sf y}_{i}={\sf c})\Big)\wedge\bigwedge_{k\in[j]}({\sf y}_{i_{k}}\in{\bf c}\wedge{\sf x}_{i}={\sf y}_{i_{k}})\Bigg).

Finally, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[l],
we define

\textphi_{Y_{\sf R}^{(i)}}({\sf x})=(\forall{\sf c}\in{\bf c}\ {\sf x}\neq{\sf c})\wedge\exists{\sf y}_{1},\ldots,{\sf y}_{r}\Big(({\sf y}_{1},\ldots,{\sf y}_{r})\in{\sf R}\wedge\bigvee_{j,k\in[r],j\neq k}({\sf c}_{i}={\sf y}_{i}\wedge{\sf x}={\sf y}_{k})\Big).

It is easy to see that all above formulas are in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}[\texttau\cup{\bf c}]
and that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal I}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})={\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a}).

The existence of a
FOL
-transduction from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau\cup{\bf c}
that maps
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})
, if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}
has bounded Hadwiger number, follows from
Subsection 5.2
.
∎

Combining
Subsection 5.2
and
Subsection 6.1
, we get the following:

Corollary 24
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l\in\mathbb{N},
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
be a collection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
constant symbols, and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}
be the constant-projection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau\cup{\bf c}.

For every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}],
there exists a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi|_{{\sf ap}_{\bf c}}\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau\cup{\bf c}]
such that for every

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}
, if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}={\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})
for some

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\texttau\cup{\bf c})
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a}),
it holds that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})\models\textphi|_{{\sf ap}_{\bf c}}\iff{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a})\models\textphi.

Concluding this subsection, we present
Table 1
that summarizes the notations introduced above for the different kinds of formulas that we consider.

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf f},

6.2
Introducing an annotation

In this subsection we present a way to “slightly modify” our sentences in order to construct an enhanced version of every sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta.

Based on the results of
Subsection 6.1
, we first consider for each
FOL
-target sentence of our given sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta
its
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
-apex-projected sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma^{l}.

For each one of them, we take an equivalent Gaifman sentence of it.
Finally, we add an additional unary relation symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}
to our vocabulary and we ask that the interpretation of the “scattered” variables of each Gaifman sentence are made “inside” the interpretation of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}
in our structure.
This idea is borrowed from
[
53
]
but here, on the top of it, we also incorporate the “apex-projection” in order to be able to apply locality arguments inside a “flat” graph.

Restricting the domain of variables.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l\in\mathbb{N},
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
be a collection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
constant symbols,
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\notin\texttau
be a unary relation symbol.
We now describe how to define an
enhanced version

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
of a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta[\texttau].

For every
FOL
-target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
we do the following:
we consider the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
-apex-projected sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma^{l}\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}]
and we denote it by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textzeta.

By
Subsection 4.3
, there is a Gaifman sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}]
that is equivalent to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textzeta.

Since
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}
is a Gaifman sentence,
there exist
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}p\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r_{1},\ldots,r_{p},\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{p}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},
and a collection of sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textzeta_{1},\ldots,\textzeta_{p}\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}]
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}
is a Boolean combination of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textzeta_{1},\ldots,\textzeta_{p}
and, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h\in[p],
every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textzeta_{h}
is a basic local sentence with parameters
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell_{h}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r_{h},
i.e.,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\textzeta_{h}=\exists{\sf x}_{1}\ldots\exists{\sf x}_{\ell_{h}}\big(\bigwedge_{1\leq i<j\leq\ell_{h}}d({\sf x}_{i},{\sf x}_{j})>2r_{h}\wedge\bigwedge_{i\in[\ell_{h}]}\textpsi_{h}({\sf x}_{i})\big),

where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi_{h}
is an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r_{h}
-local formula in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}[\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}]
with one free variable.

Given a Gaifman sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}]
as above that is a Boolean combination of sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textzeta_{1},\ldots,\textzeta_{p}\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau],
we define the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}_{\sf R}
to be the sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}[\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}\cup\{{\sf R}\}]
that is the same Boolean combination of sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\textzeta}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\textzeta}_{p}\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}\cup\{{\sf R}\}]
such that, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h\in[p],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\tilde{\textzeta}_{h}=\exists{\sf x}_{1}\ldots\exists{\sf x}_{\ell_{h}}\big(\bigwedge_{i\in[\ell_{h}]}{\sf x}_{i}\in{\sf R}\wedge\bigwedge_{1\leq i<j\leq\ell_{h}}d({\sf x}_{i},{\sf x}_{j})>2r_{h}\wedge\bigwedge_{i\in[\ell_{h}]}\textpsi_{h}({\sf x}_{i})\big).

We define an
enhanced version

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
to be a sentence obtained
from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
after replacing each
FOL
-target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}_{\sf R}|_{{\sf ap}_{\bf c}},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textzeta=\textsigma^{l}.

Note that since
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}_{\sf R}\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}\cup\{{\sf R}\}],
it holds that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}_{\sf R}|_{{\sf ap}_{\bf c}}\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf R}\}\cup{\bf c}],
which in turn implies that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf R}\}\cup{\bf c}].

We also stress that, because of Gaifman’s theorem (
Subsection 4.3
),
for every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textzeta,
there may exist
many
different Gaifman sentences that are equivalent to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textzeta.

Due to this fact, a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta[\texttau]
can have
many
enhanced versions.
However, all the enhanced versions of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
are equivalent.
On the other hand, the proof of Gaifman’s theorem implies that there is
one effectively computable Gaifman sentence that is equivalent to the given sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textzeta
.

We now prove the equivalence between
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
and an enhanced version
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

Lemma 25
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\notin\texttau
be a unary relation symbol, and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
be a collection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
constant symbols, where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}.

Also, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta[\texttau]
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
be an enhanced version of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
and for every apex-tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
, it holds that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\texttheta\iff(\mathfrak{A},V(\mathfrak{A}),{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}
is interpreted as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
is interpreted as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}.

Proof.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
be an
FOL
-target sentence of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textzeta=\textsigma^{l}.

By
Subsection 6.1
,
for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}
and every apex-tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
, it holds that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}\models\textsigma\iff{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{B},{\bf a})\models\textzeta,
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
is interpreted as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}.

Also, observe that
since
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}
is a unary relation symbol and by the definition of the function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ap}_{\bf c},

the structures
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}({\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{B},{\bf a}),V({\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{B},{\bf a})))
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{B},V(\mathfrak{B}),{\bf a})
are the same.
This implies that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{B},{\bf a})\models\textzeta\iff{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{B},V(\mathfrak{B}),{\bf a})\models\breve{\textzeta}_{\sf R},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}
is interpreted as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{B}).

Thus, by
Subsection 6.1
,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}\models\textsigma\iff(\mathfrak{B},V(\mathfrak{B}),{\bf a})\models\breve{\textzeta}_{\sf R}|_{{\sf ap}_{\bf c}}.

∎

Observe that, for every
FOL
-target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,

by
Subsection 6.1
,
for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}

and for every two apex-tuples
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}_{1},{\bf a}_{2}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
,
it holds that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a}_{1})\models\textsigma^{l}\iff{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a}_{2})\models\textsigma^{l}.

Therefore, we can prove the following:

Lemma 26
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\notin\texttau
be a unary relation symbol, and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
be a collection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
constant symbols, where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}.

Also, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta[\texttau]
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
be an enhanced version of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},
for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A}),
and for every two apex-tuples
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}_{1},{\bf a}_{2}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
, it holds that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},R,{\bf a}_{1})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\iff(\mathfrak{A},R,{\bf a}_{2})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

In
Table 2
, we present all formulas needed to define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

Formulas

Meaning

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma

a

-target sentence of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textzeta

the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
-apex-projected sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma^{l}
of

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textzeta

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-local formulas of the basic local sentences of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}_{\sf R}

the Gaifman sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}
after adding
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}
(whose model is of the form
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf ap}_{\bf c}(\mathfrak{A},{\sf R})
)

-target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}_{\sf R}|_{{\sf ap}_{\bf c}}

:
