\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta.

7
Preliminary tools

In this section we present a series of preliminary results required for our algorithm and its proof of correctness.

Our first tool, presented in
Subsection 7.1
, deals with boundaried structures (a generalization of boundaried graphs).
Given a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau],
we define an equivalence relation on boundaried structures with respect to the (partial) satisfaction of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi.
A variant of Courcelle’s theorem (
Subsection 7.1
) indicates that there is a finite set of sentences that are evaluated on boundaried structures and are “representatives” of the equivalence classes defined by the above equivalence relation.
These “representatives” will help us to “finitize” the way a sentence is partially satisfied (or not) in a boundaried part of our structure.

In
Subsection 7.2
we define the notion of
bidimensionality
of a vertex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
with respect to a flatness pair (by flatness pair, here, we mean a flat wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
together with a tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
that certifies its flatness, as defined in
[
129
]
; see
Subsection A.4
for a formal definition).
This concept is a measure of the “dispersion” of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
inside the “bidimensional territories” of a flatness pair. We present two results on these notions, namely
Subsection 7.2
and
Subsection 7.2
. These two results will be crucial for our algorithm and its correctness.

In
Subsection 7.3
we define the notion of privileged component, that will express the part of our input structure that, under the presence of a “big enough” flatness pair, contains the “bulk” of the wall of this flatness pair.

7.1
A variant of Courcelle’s theorem

In this subsection we aim to present a variant of Courcelle’s theorem (
Subsection 7.1
).
We start with some definitions on
boundaried structures
.

Boundaried structures.

Given a vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
and a non-negative integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell,
an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
-boundaried
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
is a tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell}),
also denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf x}),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}x_{i}\in V(\mathfrak{A}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[\ell].

A
boundaried
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
is an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
-boundaried
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure, for some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell\in\mathbb{N}.

Given a vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau,
we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal B}_{\texttau}
the set of all boundaried
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structures
and, given an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell\in\mathbb{N},
we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal B}_{\texttau}^{(\ell)}
the set of all
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
-boundaried
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structures.
We treat
CMSOL
-sentences evaluated on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
-boundaried
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structures, as sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf b}_{1},\ldots,{\sf b}_{\ell}\}],

where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf b}_{1},\ldots,{\sf b}_{\ell}
are constant symbols not contained in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell\in\mathbb{N}.

We say that two
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
-boundaried
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structures
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf x}),(\mathfrak{B},{\bf y})\in{\cal B}_{\texttau}^{(\ell)}

are
compatible
if there is a function that maps
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}x_{i}
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}y_{i},
for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[\ell],
that is an isomorphism from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}[V({\bf x})]
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}[V({\bf y})].

Given two compatible
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
-boundaried
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structures
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf x})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{B},{\bf y}),

we define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf x})\oplus(\mathfrak{B},{\bf y})
as the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure obtained
if we take the disjoint union of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{B}

and, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[\ell],
we identify the elements
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}x_{i}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}y_{i}.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau].

We say that two
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
-boundaried
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structures
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf x}),(\mathfrak{B},{\bf y})\in{\cal B}_{\texttau}^{(\ell)}

are
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\textphi,\ell)
-equivalent
, and we denote it by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf x})\equiv_{\textphi,\ell}(\mathfrak{B},{\bf y}),
if they are compatible and

\forall\ (\mathfrak{C},{\bf z})\in{\cal B}_{\texttau}^{(\ell)},\ (\mathfrak{C},{\bf z})\oplus(\mathfrak{A},{\bf x})\models\textphi\iff(\mathfrak{C},{\bf z})\oplus(\mathfrak{B},{\bf y})\models\textphi.

Note that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\equiv_{\textphi,\ell}
is an equivalence relation on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal B}_{\texttau}^{(\ell)}.

The following result is a variant of Courcelle’s theorem
[
31
,
32
,
33
]
. It essentially says that the dynamic programming tables constructed by the proof of Courcelle’s theorem are
also definable in
CMSOL
. This fact is implicit in the proof of Courcelle’s theorem. For instance, it can easily be derived from the proof of
[
12
, Lemma 3.2]
.

Proposition 27

(Courcelle)
.

There is a function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f:\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau,
every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau],
and every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell\in\mathbb{N},
it holds that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|{\cal B}_{\texttau}^{(\ell)}/_{\equiv_{\textphi,\ell}}|\leq f(|\textphi|,\ell,|\texttau|).

An alternative way to see
Subsection 7.1
is to say that, for every vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau,
every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau],
and every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell\in\mathbb{N},
there is a collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf rep}_{\texttau}^{(\ell)}(\textphi)=\{\textphi_{1},\ldots,\textphi_{m}\}
of sentences on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
-boundaried
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structures (i.e., sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf b}_{1},\ldots,{\sf b}_{\ell}\}]
)
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}m\leq f(|\textphi|,\ell,|\texttau|)
and such that

•

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf x})\in{\cal B}_{\texttau}^{(\ell)}
there exists
exactly one

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m]
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf x})\models\textphi_{i}
and

•

for every compatible
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf x}),(\mathfrak{B},{\bf y})\in{\cal B}_{\texttau}^{(\ell)}
and every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf x})\models\textphi_{i}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{B},{\bf y})\models\textphi_{i},
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf x})\equiv_{\textphi,\ell}(\mathfrak{B},{\bf y}).

The elements of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf rep}_{\texttau}^{(\ell)}(\textphi)
are called
types
and can be seen
as an
CMSOL
-definable encoding of the tables of the dynamic programming generated by Courcelle’s theorem.
This representation of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi,
in what concerns boundary structures, provides
an abstract representation that does not depend on the “internal part” of a boundary graph and will be used as a key ingredient of the encodings in Sections
9
,
10
, and
11
.

7.2
Bidimensionality of sets in flatness pairs

Bidimensionality of a vertex set with respect to a flatness pair.

See
Subsection A.7
for the definition of a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
-canonical partition of a graph, for some flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R}).

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
be a graph and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
be a flatness pair of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G.

For every set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(G)
and every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
-canonical partition
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal Q}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,

we define the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal Q}
-bidimensionality of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X

to be the number of internal bags of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal Q}
that
contain a vertex of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X.

For every set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(G),
we define the
bidimensionality of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
, and we denote it by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf bid}_{(W,\mathfrak{R})}(X),

to be
the maximum
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal Q}
-bidimensionality of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
over all
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
-canonical partitions
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal Q}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G.

At this point we wish to notice, even we do not use it in this paper, that the choice of the canonical partition does not affect substantially the bidimensionality of a set.

Observation 28
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k\in\mathbb{N},
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
be a graph, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
be a flatness pair of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
and let

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal Q}
be a

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
-canonical partition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G.

If the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal Q}
-bidimensionality of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k,
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf bid}_{(W,\mathfrak{R})}(X)\leq 6k.

Brambles.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
be a graph. Two sets
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V_{1},V_{2}\subseteq V(G)
are said to
touch
if they have a vertex in common or there is an edge
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{v_{1},v_{2}\}\in E(G)
with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{1}\in V_{1}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v_{2}\in V_{2}.

A set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal B}
of pairwise touching vertex sets of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(G)
that induce connected subgraphs of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
is called a
bramble
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G.

The
order
of a bramble
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal B}
is the minimum size of a vertex set that intersects every element of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal B}.

The following relation between treewidth and a maximum order bramble is proved in
[
134
]
(see also
[
9
, Theorem 5]
).

Proposition 29
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
be a non-negative integer, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
be a graph.
The treewidth of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
is at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
if and only if every bramble of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
has order at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k+1.

We will prove the following key result:

Lemma 30
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l,q\in\mathbb{N},

let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
be a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l})
be an apex-tuple of

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}},
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
be a
flatness pair of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus V({\bf a}).

For every set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A}),
if

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cl}_{\sf X}({\sf star}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A},X))
has treewidth at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q,
then

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf bid}_{(W,\mathfrak{R})}(X\setminus V({\bf a}))\leq(q+1)^{2}.

Proof.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A})
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cl}_{\sf X}({\sf star}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A},X))
has treewidth at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q.

Also, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal Q}
be a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
-canonical partition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus V({\bf a}).

We will show that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
intersects at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(q+1)^{2}
internal bags of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal Q}.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h
be the height of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R}).

Also, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H
be the Gaifman graph of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cl}_{\sf X}({\sf star}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A},S))
and keep in mind that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf tw}(H)\leq q.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[h],
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{i}
be the union of the vertex sets of all internal bags of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal Q}
that intersect the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i
-th horizontal path of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W,
i.e.,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{i}:=\bigcup_{j\in[2,h-1]}V(Q^{(i,j)}).

Also, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}L_{i}
be the union of the vertex sets of all internal bags of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal Q}
that intersect the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i
-th vertical path of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W,
i.e.,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}L_{i}:=\bigcup_{j\in[2,h-1]}V(Q^{(j,i)}).

We also define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T^{(i,j)}:=P_{i}\cup L_{j},i,j\in[h].

We let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T_{X}^{(i,j)}:=T^{(i,j)}\cap X.

We now consider the collection

{\cal D}=\{H[T_{X}^{(i,j)}]\mid i,j\in[h]\mbox{ and }T_{X}^{(i,j)}\neq\emptyset\}.

We will prove that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal D}
is a bramble of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H.

For this, we have to prove that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal D}
consists of pairwise touching connected subgraphs of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H.

Recall that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
is connected
and notice that
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v,u\in X
and there is a path
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
connecting
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}u
such that no internal vertex of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P
is in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X,
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{v,u\}\in E(H).

This implies that every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}D\in{\cal D}
is connected and every two
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}D_{1},D_{2}\in{\cal D}
are touching, thus
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal D}
is a bramble.

By
Subsection 7.2
, we have that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf tw}(H)\leq q
implies that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal D}
has order at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q+1.

This, in turn, implies that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
intersects at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(q+1)^{2}

internal bags of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal Q}.

∎

The next result intuitively states that given a flat wall and some apices, we can find another flat wall inside the first one such that the set of apices that are adjacent to the compass of the new flat wall has “big enough” bidimensionality to the flat wall, i.e., is adjacent to “many enough” internal bags of every canonical partition of the graph defined by the latter flat wall.
We refer the reader to
Subsection A.4
for the definition of the tilt of a wall inside a flatness pair.

Lemma 31
.

There is a function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f_{1}:\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}

and an algorithm that receives as an input two integers
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l,d\in\mathbb{N},
an odd integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 3,
a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A\subseteq V(G)
of size at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l,

and a flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus A
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@lacedaemonians]{f_{\ref*{@lacedaemonians}}}(r,l,d),
and outputs, in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{r,l,d}(n),
an set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A^{\prime}\subseteq A
and a flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}})
of

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus A^{\prime}

of height at least
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
that is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-tilt of some subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
and
for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a\in A^{\prime},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf bid}_{(\tilde{W},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}})}(N_{G}(a)\setminus A^{\prime})\geq d.

Proof.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l,d\in\mathbb{N}
and let an odd integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 3.

We define the function

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@lacedaemonians]{f_{\ref*{@lacedaemonians}}}:\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}
so that,
for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}x,z\in\mathbb{N},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@lacedaemonians]{f_{\ref*{@lacedaemonians}}}(x,0,z):=x,
while, for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}y\geq 1,
we set

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@lacedaemonians]{f_{\ref*{@lacedaemonians}}}(x,y,z):={\sf odd}(\lceil\sqrt{z+1}\rceil)\cdot(\hyperref@@ii[@lacedaemonians]{f_{\ref*{@lacedaemonians}}}(x,y-1,z)+2).

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
be a graph, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A\subseteq V(G)
of size at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l,
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
be a flatness pair of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus A
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@lacedaemonians]{f_{\ref*{@lacedaemonians}}}(r,l,d).

We will prove the lemma by induction on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l.

In the case that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l=0,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
has height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@lacedaemonians]{f_{\ref*{@lacedaemonians}}}(r,0,d)=r
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A=\emptyset,
so the lemma holds trivially for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R}).

Suppose now that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l\geq 1
and that the lemma holds for smaller values of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l.

We set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q:=\hyperref@@ii[@lacedaemonians]{f_{\ref*{@lacedaemonians}}}(x,y-1,z).

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal Q}
be a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
-canonical partition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus A.

If every vertex in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A
is adjacent in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
to at least
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}d
internal bags of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{Q},

then the algorithm outputs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R}).

Otherwise, there is a vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a\in A
that is adjacent, in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
to less than
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}d
internal bags of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal Q}.

In this case, we consider a collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal W}=\{W_{1},\ldots,W_{d+1}\}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}d+1
subwalls of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q
such that, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i,j\in[d+1],i\neq j,
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}_{i},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{i})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}_{j},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{j})
are some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{i}
-tilt and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{j}
-tilt of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
respectively, then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}{\sf influence}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{i}}(\tilde{W}_{i}))
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}{\sf influence}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{j}}(\tilde{W}_{j}))
are disjoint. The existence of this collection is guaranteed by the fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@lacedaemonians]{f_{\ref*{@lacedaemonians}}}(r,l,d)\geq\lceil\sqrt{d+1}\rceil\cdot(q+2)
and it can be found in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{r,l,d}(n).

Now notice that since
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a
is adjacent, in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
to less than
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}d
internal bags of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal Q},
then there is a wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{i},i\in[d+1]
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal W}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a
is adjacent, in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
to no internal bag of any
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}_{i},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{i})
-canonical partition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus A.

From the induction hypothesis, we have that we can compute, in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{r,l,d}(n),
a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime\prime}
-tilt
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\breve{W},\breve{\mathfrak{R}})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R}),
for some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime\prime}
that is a subwall of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{i}
(and therefore of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
), that has height at least
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
and set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A^{\prime}\subseteq A\setminus\{a\}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l^{\prime}<l
such that every vertex in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A^{\prime}
is adjacent, in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
to at least
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}d
internal bags of every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\breve{W},\breve{\mathfrak{R}})
-canonical partition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus A^{\prime}.

∎

The following result is the core result of
[
130
]
(which, in turn, is based on the results of
[
7
]
and
[
72
]
).
We refer the reader to
Subsection A.4
for a definition of a flatness pair.
The framework of flatness pairs was recently introduced in
[
129
]
to deal with some technical issues in the proof of the Flat Wall theorem in
[
93
]
(see
Subsection A.4
and
Subsection A.6
for the definitions of a regular and a homogeneous flatness pair, respectively).
We use
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c_{\cal F}
to denote
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|{\cal F}|,
i.e, the biggest size of a graph in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal F}.

Proposition 32
.

There exist two functions
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f_{2}:\mathbb{N}^{4}\to\mathbb{N}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f_{3}:\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every finite collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal F}
of graphs,
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r,l,k\in\mathbb{N},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
is a graph,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A
is a subset of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(G)
of size at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
is a regular flatness pair of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus A
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@denominations]{f_{\ref*{@denominations}}}(c_{\cal F},k,r,l)

that is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@surreptitiously]{f_{\ref*{@surreptitiously}}}(c_{\cal F},l)
-homogeneous with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}2^{A},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V_{K}

is the vertex set of the compass of the
central
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-subwall of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W,

then for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(G)
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf bid}_{(W,\mathfrak{R})}(X\setminus A)\leq k
it holds that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X\in{\sf excl}({\cal F})\iff G\setminus(X\setminus V_{K})\in{\sf excl}({\cal F}).

7.3
Privileged components

In this subsection we define pseudogrids and we introduce the notion of a
privileged connected component

of a graph with respect to a pseudogrid.
A pseudogrid is a collection of equally many “vertical” and “horizontal” paths that intersect in a “grid-like” way.
The size of a pseudogrid is the number of vertical/horizontal paths it contains.
Intuitively, a vertex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
of a graph is privileged with respect to a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
and a pseudogrid
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
is the vertex set of a connected component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X
that contains at least one vertical and one horizontal path (see formal definitions below).
We notice that, given a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
and a pseudogrid
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}
that is “big enough”,
there is at most one vertex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
that is privileged with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}
(see
Subsection 7.3
).
After this intuitive introduction, we present the formal definitions of the notions presented above.

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{1}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{2}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{3}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{4}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Q_{1}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Q_{2}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Q_{3}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Q_{4}

Figure 6
:
An example of a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}4
-pseudogrid
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{4}=(P_{1},\ldots,P_{4},Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{4}).

Respecting a collection of vertex sets.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
be a graph, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{q}\in\mathbb{N},
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{{q}}=(P_{1},\ldots,P_{{q}},Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{{q}})\in(2^{V(G)})^{2q}.
We say that a vertex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C\subseteq V(G)

respects

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{{q}}
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
contains at least one element of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{{q}}\}
and at least one element of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{{q}}\}.

Given an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(G),
we define

{\sf pr}(G,{\bf W}_{{q}},X)=\{C\in{\sf cc}(G,X)\mid\mbox{\penalty\ $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C$ respects $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{{q}}$}\}.

We call every set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C\in{\sf pr}(G,{\bf W}_{{q}},X)
a
privileged component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
.

Pseudogrids.

We now give the definition of a pseudogrid, as given in
[
102
, Definition 6]
.
Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
be a graph, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{q}\in\mathbb{N},
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{{q}}=(P_{1},\ldots,P_{{q}},Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{{q}})\in(2^{V(G)})^{2q}.

We say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{{q}}
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q
-pseudogrid
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal P}=(P_{1},\ldots,P_{q})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal Q}=(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{q})
are two sequences of vertex-disjoint paths and, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[q],

the path
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{i}
is a concatenation of paths
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{i,0},P_{i,1}^{Q},P_{i,1},P_{i,2}^{Q},P_{i,2},\ldots,P_{i,q}^{Q},P_{i,q}
in this order such that, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j\in[q],
each path
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{i,j}^{Q}
is a non-empty subpath of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Q_{j}
(possibly consisting of a single vertex) and, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j\in[0,q],
every path
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{i,j}
does not contain any edge nor internal vertex of any path
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Q_{j}
(
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{i,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{i,q}
are allowed to be paths of length 0), and the symmetric conditions hold with the roles of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal P}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal Q}
swapped (see
Figure 6
).
We refer to the paths in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal P}
(resp.
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal Q}
) as the
horizontal paths
(resp.
vertical paths
) of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}.

The next observation indicates that for every graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q
-pseudogrid
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q},
and every set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(G),
there is at most one set in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cc}(G,X)
that respects
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}.

Observation 33
.

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{q}
-pseudogrid of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
is a subset of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(G),
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf pr}(G,{\bf W}_{{q}},X)
is either a singleton or the empty set.

To see why
Subsection 7.3
holds, first notice that a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q
-pseudogrid of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
induces
a connected subgraph of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G.

Also notice that, if for a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}{\bf W}_{q}
it holds that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|{\sf pr}(G,{\bf W}_{{q}},X)|\geq 2,
then there are at least two distinct sets
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{1},C_{2}\in{\sf cc}(G,X)
that respect
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}.

This implies that there is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{i}
(resp.
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{j}
) and a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Q_{i}
(resp.
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Q_{j}
) in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q},
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{i},Q_{i}\subseteq C_{1}
(resp.
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{j},Q_{j}\subseteq C_{2}
).
But
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{i}\cup P_{j}\cup Q_{i}\cup Q_{j}
induces a connected subgraph of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X,
a contradiction to the fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{1}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C_{2}
are distinct elements of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cc}(G,X).

8
The algorithm

In this section we aim to present the general scheme of our algorithm for
Subsection 5.4
.
In
Subsection 8.1
,
we present the main subroutine of our algorithm that
reduces the annotated set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R
and the universe of the structure
under the presence of a flatness pair of “big enough” height in our structure, which is a certificate
that the treewidth of the structure is “big enough” (
Subsection 8.1
). The proof of
Subsection 8.1
is strongly based on
Subsection 8.1
, whose proof is the main technical part of this paper and
is postponed to Sections
9
,
10
, and
11
.
A brief explanation of the proof idea is given in
Subsection 8.3
.
Assuming the claimed algorithm of
Subsection 8.1
, in
Subsection 8.2
we show how to use this subroutine in order to design an algorithm for
Subsection 5.4
and we provide the proof of the latter.

8.1
Reducing the instance

As we mention in the overview of the proof presented in
Section 2
, we use the
irrelevant vertex technique
to reduce the problem to instances of bounded treewidth.
This idea is materialized in the next lemma that provides an algorithm that, given an instance
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},R,{\bf a}),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
is an apex-tuple of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},
and a regular flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus V({\bf a})
of “big enough” height, such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
has bounded treewidth, outputs an instance
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A}^{\prime},R^{\prime},{\bf a})
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A}^{\prime})\subsetneq V(\mathfrak{A}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R^{\prime}\subsetneq R,
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},R,{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\iff(\mathfrak{A}^{\prime},R^{\prime},{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

Lemma 34
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\notin\texttau
be a unary relation symbol, and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
be a collection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
constant symbols, where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}.

There is a function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f_{4}:\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm that receives as an input

•

an enhanced version
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
of a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta[\texttau],

•

a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t\in\mathbb{N}
and an odd integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}g\geq 3,

•

a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},
a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A}),
and an apex-tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l,
and

•

a regular flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus V({\bf a})
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@ausgeschlossen]{f_{\ref*{@ausgeschlossen}}}(|\texttheta|,l,g)

such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
has treewidth at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t,

and
outputs,
in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{|\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}|,l,g,t}(n),
a
set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A})\setminus V({\bf a})

and a flatness
pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus V({\bf a})
that is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-tilt of some subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}g
such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V({\sf compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime}))\subseteq Y

and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},R,{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\iff(\mathfrak{A}\setminus V({\sf compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime})),R\setminus Y,{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

To prove
Subsection 8.1
, we aim to reduce the annotated set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R
and to characterize some non-annotated vertices as ‘‘irrelevant’’ to the existence of a solution to the problem, which allows us to reduce our problem to ‘‘simpler’’ equivalent instances.
Since our problem has three basic elements
5
5

5

Throughout the reminder of the article, we use consistently this color coding using
blue
/
green
/
red
to easily identify the three parts of our problem.

1)

the satisfaction of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta
in the modulator sets,

2)

the satisfaction of a
FOL
-sentence in the remaining “terminal part” of the structure, and

3)

the exclusion of a minor from the Gaifman graph of the remaining “terminal part” of the structure,

our “irrelevancy” arguments also decompose into three parts.

Concerning the “irrelevancy” for minor-exclusion (Item
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3)}
), we use

Subsection 7.2
in order to
obtain a flatness pair whose compass is “irrelevant” with respect to any set of “small enough” bidimensionality.
After finding this flatness pair, our attention is focused on items
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1)}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0}2)}.

Lemma 35
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\notin\texttau
be a unary relation symbol, and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
be a collection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
constant symbols, where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}.

There are three functions
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f_{5}:\mathbb{N}^{5}\to\mathbb{N},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f_{6}:\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N},
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f_{7}:\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N},

and an algorithm that receives as an input

1.

a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta
and an enhanced version
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,

2.

two integers
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l,z\in\mathbb{N},
an odd integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}g\geq 3,

3.

a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},
a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A}),
and a
tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V({\bf a})\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A}),

4.

a flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus V({\bf a})
of height at least
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@occidentales]{f_{\ref*{@occidentales}}}({\bf hw}(\texttheta),{\bf tw}(\texttheta),c,l,g),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
is the maximum size of a
FOL
-target sentence of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,
such that

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
has treewidth at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}z
and

•

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a\in V({\bf a}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf bid}_{(W,\mathfrak{R})}(N_{G_{\mathfrak{A}}}(a)\setminus V({\bf a}))\geq\hyperref@@ii[@riguardavano]{f_{\ref*{@riguardavano}}}(|\texttheta|,{\bf tw}(\texttheta),g),
and

5.

a vertex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}D\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A})
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V({\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W))\subseteq D
and for every

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(G)
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf bid}_{(W,\mathfrak{R})}(X\setminus V({\bf a}))\leq\hyperref@@ii[@carthaginoise]{f_{\ref*{@carthaginoise}}}(|\texttheta|,{\bf tw}(\texttheta))
it holds that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus X\in{\sf excl}(\{K_{{\bf hw}(\texttheta)}\})\iff G_{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus(X\setminus D)\in{\sf excl}(\{K_{{\bf hw}(\texttheta)}\}),

and outputs, in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{|\texttheta|,l,z,j^{\prime}}(n),

a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A})\setminus V({\bf a})

and a flatness
pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus V({\bf a})
that is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-tilt of some subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}g
such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V({\sf compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime}))\subseteq Y

and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},R,{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\iff(\mathfrak{A}\setminus V({\sf compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime})),R\setminus Y,{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

The proof of
Subsection 8.1
is based on the algorithm
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\tt Find\_Equiv\_FlatPairs},

presented in
Subsection 9.5
(also informally sketched in
Subsection 8.3
).
First, in
Subsection 9.6
, we prove the correctness of this algorithm for sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}_{1}.

Then, in the end of
Subsection 10.3
we provide a sketch of the proof for sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}.

The full proof for sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}
can be found in
Appendix B
.
Finally, in
Section 11
, we explain how to extend the proof in
Appendix B
so to capture the full generality of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta.

We now provide the proof of
Subsection 8.1
, assuming the correctness of
Subsection 8.1
. See the down-right green rectangle of
Figure 7
for a summary of the main ideas and supporting results of the proof of
Subsection 8.1
.

Proof of
Subsection 8.1
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
be the maximum size of a
FOL
-target sentence of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

We set

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\textlambda:=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\hyperref@@ii[@surreptitiously]{f_{\ref*{@surreptitiously}}}({\bf hw}(\texttheta),l),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle d:=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\hyperref@@ii[@carthaginoise]{f_{\ref*{@carthaginoise}}}(|\texttheta|,{\bf tw}(\texttheta)),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle m:=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\hyperref@@ii[@riguardavano]{f_{\ref*{@riguardavano}}}(|\texttheta|,{\bf tw}(\texttheta)),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle p:=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\hyperref@@ii[@occidentales]{f_{\ref*{@occidentales}}}({\bf hw}(\texttheta),{\bf tw}(\texttheta),c,l,j),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle z:=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\hyperref@@ii[@lacedaemonians]{f_{\ref*{@lacedaemonians}}}(p,l,m),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle r:=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\hyperref@@ii[@denominations]{f_{\ref*{@denominations}}}({\bf hw}(\texttheta),d,z,l),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle w:=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\hyperref@@ii[@poderosamente]{f_{\ref*{@poderosamente}}}(r,l,\ell),\text{\penalty\ and}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\hyperref@@ii[@ausgeschlossen]{f_{\ref*{@ausgeschlossen}}}(|\texttheta|,j):=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle w.

We first run the algorithm of
Subsection A.6
with input
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w,l,\textlambda,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a},
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R}).

It outputs, in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{|\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}|}(n),
a flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\breve{W},\breve{{\mathfrak{R}}})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus V({\bf a})
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
that is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textlambda
-homogeneous with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}2^{V({\bf a})}
and
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{W}
-tilt of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,{\mathfrak{R}})
for some subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{W}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W.

The wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{W}
virtually corresponds to the second wall of
Figure 2
.
We set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}D
to be the vertex set of the compass of the central
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}z
-subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\overline{W}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{W}.

The wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\overline{W}
corresponds to the inner part of the third wall of
Figure 2
(that is also the fourth wall of the same figure).
Notice that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\overline{W}
is also a subwall of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W.

By
Subsection 7.2
, for every

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(G)
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf bid}_{(\breve{W},\breve{\mathfrak{R}})}(X\setminus V({\bf a}))\leq d
it holds that

G_{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus X\in{\sf excl}(\{K_{{\bf hw}(\texttheta)}\})\iff G_{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus(X\setminus D)\in{\sf excl}(\{K_{{\bf hw}(\texttheta)}\}).

Then, by applying the algorithm of
Subsection 7.2
for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l,m,r,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V({\bf a}),
and a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\overline{W}
-tilt of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R}),
we can find, in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{|\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}|}(n),
an apex-tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}^{\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l^{\prime}\leq l
and a
flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W^{\bullet},\mathfrak{R}^{\bullet})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus V({\bf a}^{\prime})

of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
that is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\star}
-tilt of a subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\star}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\overline{W}

such that for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a\in V({\bf a}^{\prime}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf bid}_{(W^{\bullet},\mathfrak{R}^{\bullet})}(N_{G_{\mathfrak{A}}}(a)\setminus V({\bf a}^{\prime}))\geq m.

Note that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W^{\bullet},\mathfrak{R}^{\bullet})
is also a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{*}
-tilt of a subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{*}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W.

The wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\bullet}
corresponds to the selected wall inside the fourth wall of
Figure 2
.
By applying
Subsection 8.1
, we can find, in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{|\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}|}(n),

a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A})\setminus V({\bf a}^{\prime})

and a flatness
pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus V({\bf a}^{\prime})
that is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-tilt of some subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j
such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V({\sf compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime}))\subseteq Y

and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},R,{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\iff(\mathfrak{A}\setminus V({\sf compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime})),R\setminus Y,{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

∎

Notice that in the case of a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta[\texttau]
whose
FOL
-target sentences are always true, the analogue of
Subsection 6.2
is the following:

Lemma 36
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}\notin\texttau
be a unary relation symbol, and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
be a collection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
constant symbols, where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}.

Also, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta[\texttau]
whose
FOL
-target sentences are always true and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
be an enhanced version of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},
for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A}),
and for every apex-tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
, it holds that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\texttheta\iff(\mathfrak{A},R,{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
is interpreted as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}.

Therefore, combining
Subsection 8.1
and
Subsection 8.1
,
we get the following corollary:

Corollary 37
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary.
There is a function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f_{8}:\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm that receives as an input a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t,l\in\mathbb{N},
a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta[\texttau]
whose
FOL
-target sentences are always true, a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},

an apex-tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l,
and
a regular flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus V({\bf a})
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@ausausgeschlossen]{f_{\ref*{@ausausgeschlossen}}}(|\texttheta|,l,g)

such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
has treewidth at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t,

and
outputs,
in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{|\texttheta|,l,g,t}(n),
a
flatness
pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus V({\bf a})
that is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-tilt of some subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}g
such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\texttheta\iff\mathfrak{A}\setminus V({\sf compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime}))\models\texttheta.

Subsection 8.1
has some special consequences on the constructibility of Robertson-Seymour’s theorem that will be discussed in
Subsection 12.2
.

8.2
The algorithm of
Subsection 5.4

We are now ready to present the proof of
Subsection 5.4
(assuming the correctness of
Subsection 8.1
and therefore of
Subsection 8.1
as well).

Proof of
Subsection 5.4
.

Given a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta[\texttau],
we set

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle c

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle:=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle{\bf hw}(\texttheta)+({\bf tw}(\texttheta)+1)\cdot{\sf height}(\texttheta),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle l

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle:=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\hyperref@@ii[@carlovingios]{f_{\ref*{@carlovingios}}}(c)\mbox{ where $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@carlovingios]{f_{\ref*{@carlovingios}}}$ is the function of\penalty\ \autoref{label_proletarians}},and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle r

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle:=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\hyperref@@ii[@ausgeschlossen]{f_{\ref*{@ausgeschlossen}}}(|\texttheta|,l,3).

Our algorithm consists of four steps,
which are summarized in
Figure 7
, along with the supporting results:

Step 1
:
Consider an enhanced version
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

Consider an arbitrary apex-tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}_{0}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
.
By
Subsection 6.2
, we have that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\models\texttheta\iff(\mathfrak{A},V(\mathfrak{A}),{\bf a}_{0})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R}
is interpreted as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
is interpreted as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}_{0}.

We set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R_{0}:=V(\mathfrak{A})
and we proceed to Step 2.

Step 2
:
Run the algorithm of
Subsection A.5
for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r,
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c.

This algorithm outputs, in linear time, either
a
report that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{c}\preceq_{\sf m}G_{\mathfrak{A}},

or a tree decomposition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}
of width at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@interference]{f_{\ref*{@interference}}}(c)\cdot r,
or
a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A\subseteq V(G),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|A|\leq l,
a regular flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus A
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r,

and a tree decomposition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
of width at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@interference]{f_{\ref*{@interference}}}(c)\cdot r.

In the first possible output,
by
Subsection 5.4
, we can safely report that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\notin{\rm Mod}(\texttheta).

In the second possible output,
i.e., a tree decomposition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}
of width at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@interference]{f_{\ref*{@interference}}}(c)\cdot r,

proceed to Step 4.
In the third possible output,
proceed to Step 3.

Step 3
:
We first consider an ordering
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}
of the vertices in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A,
and set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}).

By
Subsection 6.2
, we have that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},R_{0},{\bf a}_{0})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\iff(\mathfrak{A},R_{0},{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

We run the algorithm of
Subsection 8.1
for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R_{0},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a},
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R}),
and we obtain, in linear time, a
set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A})\setminus V({\bf a})

and a flatness
pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus V({\bf a})
that is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-tilt of some subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}3
such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V({\sf compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime}))\subseteq X

and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},R_{0},{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\iff(\mathfrak{A}\setminus V({\sf compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime})),R_{0}\setminus X,{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

Then, we set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}:=\mathfrak{A}\setminus V({\sf compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime})),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}_{0}:={\bf a},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R_{0}:=R_{0}\setminus X,
and we run again Step 2.

Step 4
:
Given a tree decomposition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}
of width at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@interference]{f_{\ref*{@interference}}}(c)\cdot r,
and since

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf R}\}\cup{\bf c}],
we decide whether
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},R_{0},{\bf a}_{0})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
in linear time by using Courcelle’s theorem.

Observe that the second and the third step of the algorithm are executed in linear time and they can be repeated no more than a linear number of times.
Therefore, the overall algorithm runs in quadratic time, as claimed.
∎

8.3
Sketch of proof of
Subsection 8.1

In the next three sections,
we aim to provide a proof
for
Subsection 8.1
. In this subsection we give a brief description of the main ideas of this proof.

Dealing with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}_{1}.

In
Section 9
we prove
Subsection 8.1
for sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}_{1}[\texttau].

A sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\bar{\textTheta}_{1}[\texttau]
can be written as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma,
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\bf tw}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma=\textsigma\wedge\textmu
or
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma=(\textsigma\wedge\textmu)^{({\sf c})},
for some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau]
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu\in{\sf EM}[\texttau].

We associate each sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\bar{\textTheta}_{1}[\texttau]
with a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\circ/\bullet
-flag, that is the symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\circ
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma=(\textsigma\wedge\textmu)^{({\sf c})}
, and the symbol
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bullet
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma=\textsigma\wedge\textmu.

From this viewpoint,
depending on the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\circ/\bullet
-flag of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w,

the target sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu
are asked either in the substructure of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
induced by the vertex set of each connected component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus X,
or in the whole structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}\setminus X.

Assume the existence of a “big enough” flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}

and a “big enough” pseudogrid
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}
defined by some vertical and horizontal paths of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W.

Note that since
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\bf tw}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf cl}_{\sf X}({\sf star}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A},X))
has bounded treewidth
and therefore, by
Subsection 7.2
,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
has “small” bidimensionality with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R}).

Therefore, the removal of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}
leaves a “big bulk” of the wall in some connected component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus X,
i.e., there is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{C}\in{\sf cc}(G_{\mathfrak{A}},X)
that is privileged with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X.

Depending on the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\circ/\bullet
-flag of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,

we define the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
-privileged set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}
with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
to be either
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{C}
or the whole set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\mathfrak{A})\setminus X.

The above allows us to define a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}
obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
after splitting it into three parts (see
Subsection 9.1
).
The first part of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}
is a new sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\sf out}_{q}
that contains the modulator sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta
and the target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}_{\sf R}|_{{\sf ap}_{\bf c}}\wedge\textmu
asked in every connected component that is not in the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
-privileged set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C.

Note that the latter question appears only when
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C=\breve{C}.

The other two parts of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}
are
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}_{\sf R}|_{{\sf ap}_{\bf c}}

and the
EM
-target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu,
asked in the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
-privileged set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C.

The obtained sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}
is called the
split version
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

Under the presence of a “big enough” pseudogrid,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
are proven to be equivalent (
Subsection 9.1
).

Therefore, to reduce a structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},R,{\bf a})
to an equivalent one with respect to the satisfaction of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}},

assuming the existence of a “big enough” wall in the input structure,
we will use the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}
that “separates” the questions to the non-privileged and the privileged part of the structure.

The irrelevancy for the “minor-exclusion” part of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}
is guaranteed by assumption 5 of
Subsection 8.1
.
To deal with the sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\sf out}_{q}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}_{\sf R}|_{{\sf ap}_{\bf c}},
we define the out-signature (
Subsection 9.3
) and the in-signature (
Subsection 9.4
) of a flatness pair, respectively, and the combination of these two constitutes the characteristic of a flatness pair.
This characteristic is an “encoding” of the partial satisfaction of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\sf out}_{q}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}_{\sf R}|_{{\sf ap}_{\bf c}}
inside the flatness pair, and it is worth noting that it is
CMSOL
-definable.
After defining this characteristic, we use the following algorithm, that is formally presented in
Subsection 9.5
.

The algorithm
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\tt Find\_Equiv\_FlatPairs}.

•

Compute a packing of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}z
subwalls
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{1},\ldots,W_{z}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W,
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}z
is some “big enough” integer depending on the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,
such that the compasses of all
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{i}
-tilts of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
are pairwise disjoint (this packing of walls virtually corresponds to the packing of walls inside the fifth wall of
Figure 2
).

•

Compute a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{i}
-tilt of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
for each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[z].
These define a collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal W}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}z
flatness pairs.

•

For each of the flatness pairs in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal W},
compute its characteristic.

•

Output a collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal W}^{\prime}
of at least
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}m
flatness pairs that have all the same characteristic and,
for some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W_{0},\mathfrak{R}_{0})\in\tilde{\cal W}^{\prime}
(that virtually corresponds to the sixth wall in
Figure 2
),
the set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y:=V({\sf compass}_{\breve{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}}(\breve{W}^{\prime})),
where

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\breve{W}^{\prime},\breve{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{W}
-tilt
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{W}
is the central
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j^{\prime}
-subwall of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{W}_{0},

and
a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\bullet}
-tilt

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})

of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R}),

where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\bullet}
is be the central
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}g
-subwall of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{W}_{0}

(in the sixth wall of
Figure 2
,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y
corresponds to the light blue area and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{W}^{\prime}
to the innermost part of the wall).

After detecting
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}),
what remains is to prove that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},R,{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\iff(\mathfrak{A}\setminus V({\sf compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime})),R\setminus Y,{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

The proof of the above is presented in
Subsection 9.6
and is split into three parts, corresponding to
Claim 1
,
Claim 2
, and
Claim 3
(see
Figure 8
).

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\theta}_{q}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\theta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mu
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\zeta}_{\sf R}|_{{\sf ap}_{\bf c}}
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\theta_{q}^{\sf out}
out-sig
in-sig

, while Claims

.

Dealing with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}.

In
Section 10
we extend the above idea to sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}.

We associate each sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\bar{\textTheta}
to a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\circ/\bullet
-scenario
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w,
that is a string of alphabet
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{\circ,\bullet\}
that encodes whether each recursive question in the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
is asked in the whole remaining structure or in each of its connected components (see
Subsection 10.1
).
In this sense,
the notion of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
-privileged set defined for sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}_{1}
is generalized to the notion of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
-privileged
sequence
, that is the sequence of recursively obtained
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
-privileged sets as dictated by the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\circ/\bullet
-scenario
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w.

Also, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\bar{\textTheta},
in
Subsection 10.2
we define analogously the split version
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}

and prove that under the presence of
a “big enough” pseudogrid,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}

are equivalent (
Subsection 10.2
).

Also, in the case where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\bar{\textTheta}[\texttau],
the out-signature and the in-signature of the extended compass of a flatness pair are defined analogously: we encode the partial satisfaction of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\sf out}_{q}
for each recursive level of the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
and the satisfaction of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}_{\sf R}|_{{\sf ap}_{\bf c}}
in the privileged connected component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}
with respect to a “big enough” pseudogrid
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}
and
the union of all modulators
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{i}
(see
Subsection 10.3
).

In order to prove
Subsection 8.1
for sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}[\texttau],
we use the algorithm
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\tt Find\_Equiv\_FlatPairs}
for the “more general” characteristic mentioned above.
The proof of correctness of the algorithm
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\tt Find\_Equiv\_FlatPairs}

for sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}[\texttau]
is
again split into three main claims, namely

Claim 4
,
Claim 5
, and
Claim 6
(see
Figure 8
).
Since the proof is very similar to the one for sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}_{1}[\texttau]
presented in
Subsection 9.6
,
we provide a sketch of proof in the end of
Subsection 10.3
, and a complete proof in
Appendix B
.

Dealing with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta.

In
Section 11
we describe how to further modify the signatures in order to capture the full generality of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta.

We start by changing the given sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta[\texttau]
to a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\prime}
that is equivalent to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
but, instead of allowing arbitrary positive Boolean combinations, we allow only disjunctions of conjunctions.
This new sentence is equivalent to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
(
Section 11
).
This “simplification” allows us to do a two-step approach, by first allowing only conjunctions (see
Subsection 11.1
) and then also allowing disjunctions of conjunctions (see
Subsection 11.2
).
In both steps, we perform a trick to express these more general sentences as
a finite combination of sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}
and we build out-signatures and in-signatures based on this “reduction”.
In
Section 11
we define the analogue of split version of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
when allowing conjunctions or even disjunctions of conjunctions, and the corresponding characteristics of extended compasses of flatness pairs.
To prove
Subsection 8.1
for sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta[\texttau],
we again use the algorithm
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\tt Find\_Equiv\_FlatPairs}
and, in the end of
Section 11
, we describe how to prove its correctness for sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta[\texttau].

9
Dealing with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\Theta}_{1}

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary.
In this section we aim to present the proof of
Subsection 8.1
for a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\bar{\textTheta}_{1}[\texttau].

We fix
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}
to be a second-order variable.
Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau]
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu\in{\sf EM}[\texttau]
be the target sentences of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\bf tw}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]
be the modulator sentence of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

By definition,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\texttheta

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma,

(5)

where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma=(\textsigma\wedge\textmu)^{({\sf c})}
or
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma=\textsigma\wedge\textmu.

In other words,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
asks whether, given a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A},

there exists a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A})
such that

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf star}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A},X)\models\textbeta

and

•

depending whether
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma=(\textsigma\wedge\textmu)^{({\sf c})}
or
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma=\textsigma\wedge\textmu
:

–

either for every connected component
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus X,
it holds that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}[C]\models\textsigma\wedge\textmu,
or,

–

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf rm}_{\sf X}(\mathfrak{A},X)\models\textsigma\wedge\textmu.

Before proceeding to the proof of
Subsection 8.1
for a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\bar{\textTheta}_{1}[\texttau],
we gather all formulas used in the definition of a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\bar{\textTheta}_{1}[\texttau]
in
Table 3
.
