\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
-privileged sequence gives rise to a
sequence
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Z_{i}
’s of parts of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I^{(d-r+1)}
that
are being “chopped off” by the removal of the modulator sets in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal X}

and therefore all arguments in
Claim 1
, that deal with the satisfaction of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\sf out}_{q},
as encoded by the out-signature, have to be proved again, in
Claim 4
, for their “recursive” analogue in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}.

For the in-signature, we still deal with the satisfaction of a Gaifman
FOL
-sentence in the privileged component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}}
with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal X}
and therefore the proof of
Claim 5
is almost identical to the one of
Claim 2
.
For
Claim 6
,
the proof is again, as for
Claim 3
, a direct implication of the Assumption 5 of
Subsection 8.1
.

11
Dealing with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
(the full story)

In this section we describe the proof of
Subsection 8.1
for all sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta.

Our approach first considers a restricted version of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta,
the logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\textTheta}
defined in
Subsection 11.1
where the only positive Boolean combination of sentences that we allow in each recursive level of a question expressed in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
is the
conjunction
of a finite number of sentences.
Then, in
Subsection 11.2
we show how to insert also disjunctions to our arsenal of
positive Boolean combinations, in order to achieve the generality of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta.

This approach is based on the fact that every formula that is a positive Boolean combination of some set of formulas
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textPhi
has an equivalent formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi^{\prime}
that is a disjunction of conjunctions of formulas in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textPhi.

More formally,
given a vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
and a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal L}\subseteq\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau],

we define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf CONJ}({\cal L})
(resp.
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf DISJ}({\cal L})
) as the set of all sentences, in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau],
that are conjunctions (resp. disjunctions) of sentences of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal L},
and
we observe the following:

Observation 47
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal L}\subseteq\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau],
for some vocabulary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau.

For every formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi\in{\bf PB}({\cal L})
there is a formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi^{\prime}\in{\bf DISJ}({\bf CONJ}({\cal L}))
such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\rm Mod}(\textphi)={\rm Mod}(\textphi^{\prime}).

Moreover,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|\textphi^{\prime}|={\cal O}_{|\textphi|}(1).

For every formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi\in{\bf CONJ}({\cal L})
(resp.
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi\in{\bf DISJ}({\cal L})
), we define the
conjunction-width
(resp.
disjunction-width
) of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi
to be the minimum integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell\in\mathbb{N}
such that there exist formulas
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi_{1},\ldots,\textphi_{\ell}\in{\cal L}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi=\textphi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\textphi_{\ell}
(resp.
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi=\textphi_{1}\vee\ldots\vee\textphi_{\ell}
).

Rooted trees.

Before we continue, let us give some definitions concerning rooted trees.
Given a tree
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T,
we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}L(T)
the set of its leaves, i.e., the set of vertices of degree one in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T.

Given a rooted tree
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r)
and a vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in V(T),
we define the
height
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v
to be the number of edges in a minimum path in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T
starting from a leaf of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v.

The
height
of a rooted tree
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r)
is the maximum height among all its vertices, and note that this maximum is always achieved by the root.
Given a rooted tree
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r)
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h,
we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}L_{i},
for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[h],
the set of nodes of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i.

We denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal T}_{h,m}
the set of all rooted trees of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|L(T)|=m.

11.1
Conjunctions

In this subsection we aim to sketch the proof of
Subsection 8.1
for all sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\textTheta},
a restriction of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
that deals only with conjunctions.
Our strategy is to “reduce” each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\hat{\textTheta}[\texttau]
to a combination
of sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}[\texttau]
and extend the definitions and the ideas presented in
Section 10
so to capture also
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\textTheta}[\texttau].

Every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\hat{\textTheta}
will be associated with a rooted tree expressing its recursive definition, where the root will correspond to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
and every conjunction to a bifurcation of the tree.
Under the presence of a “big enough” pseudogrid in our given structure, we will use the aforementioned tree to define an equivalent version of the problem, where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
is “focused” towards the privileged connected component occurring each time in the leaves of this tree.
Under this scope, every root-to-leaf path of the tree will correspond to a sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}.

Performing this modification, we have to keep track of the bifurcations of the tree and ask the modulators that correspond to each such a bifurcation to be the same sets in all paths that contain this “bifurcated” node.
These equalities will have to be respected when searching for an equivalent “solution-certificate” that comes with the application of the irrelevant vertex technique.
For this reason, we have to (further) modify the definition of signatures and characteristics given in
Subsection 10.3
so as to “add one dimension” to them (corresponding to the shift from the “path-like” structure of sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}
to the “tree-like” structure of sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\textTheta}
), while respecting the equalities obtained from above.

Let us start by defining
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\textTheta}.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary.
We define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\textTheta}[\texttau]:=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\hat{\textTheta}_{i}[\texttau],
where

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\hat{\textTheta}_{0}[\texttau]

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\{\textsigma\wedge\textmu\mid\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau]\mbox{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\textmu\in{\sf EM}[\texttau]\}\mbox{\penalty\ and}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\mbox{for $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\geq 1,$ }\hat{\textTheta}_{i}[\texttau]

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\{\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma\mid\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\bf tw}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]\mbox{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\textgamma\in{\bf CONJ}({\hat{\textTheta}_{i-1}[\texttau]}^{({\sf c})})\}.

Observe that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\textTheta}[\texttau]\subseteq\textTheta[\texttau].

Conjunctive terms of sentences.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\hat{\textTheta}_{i}[\texttau],

there exist a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{{\bf tw}}[\texttau]
and a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma\in{\bf CONJ}({\hat{\textTheta}_{i-1}[\texttau]}^{({\sf c})})
such that

\texttheta=\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma.

Equivalently, there exist

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{1},\ldots,\texttheta_{\ell}\in{\hat{\textTheta}_{i-1}[\texttau]}^{({\sf c})},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
is the conjunction-width of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma,
such that

\texttheta=\textbeta\triangleright(\texttheta_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\texttheta_{\ell}).

We call the sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{1},\ldots,\texttheta_{\ell}
the
conjunctive terms of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
.
Observe that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf CONJ}(\hat{\textTheta}_{0})=\hat{\textTheta}_{0}.

To see this, notice that the conjunction of a finite number of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma_{i}\wedge\textmu_{i}
can be expressed in a single sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma^{\star}\wedge\textmu^{\star},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma^{\star}
is the conjunction of all
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma_{i}
’s and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu^{\star}
is the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu_{\cal F}|_{\sf gf},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal F}=\{K_{{\bf hn}(\texttheta)}\}.

Therefore, every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\hat{\textTheta}_{1}
has exactly one conjunctive term.

We now recursively define a function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf conj}:\hat{\textTheta}[\texttau]\to\mathbb{N}
as follows:

•

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\hat{\textTheta}_{0}[\texttau]\cup\hat{\textTheta}_{1}[\texttau],
we set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf conj}(\texttheta):=1.

•

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\hat{\textTheta}_{i}[\texttau],i\geq 2,
we set

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf conj}(\texttheta):=\sum_{i\in[\ell]}{\sf conj}(\texttheta_{i}),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{1},\ldots,\texttheta_{\ell}
are the conjunctive terms of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

Following the above definition, we stress that each sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\hat{\textTheta}
has
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf conj}(\texttheta)
target sentences.

Tree-representations of sentences.

We now describe an alternative way to view sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\textTheta}
that demonstrates the recursive structure of such a sentence.
Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\hat{\textTheta},
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h={\sf height}(\texttheta),

and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}m={\sf conj}(\texttheta).

This means that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\hat{\textTheta}_{h}.

Observe that each conjunctive term of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
is a sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\textTheta}_{h-1}.

Then, each such a conjunctive term has its own conjunctive terms.
Following this recursive argument, we define a rooted tree
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r)\in{\cal T}_{h,m}
and a function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho:V(T)\to\hat{\textTheta}

with the following properties:

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho(r)=\texttheta
and

•

for every parent node
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in V(T),
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho(v)=\texttheta,
then the children nodes of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v
are mapped, via
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho,
to the conjunctive terms of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

We stress that, if for a parent node
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in V(T),
the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho(v)
has only one conjunctive term, then its only child node is mapped, via
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho,
to this single conjunctive term.
Therefore, every root-to-leaf path in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T
has length
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h

and every leaf of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T
is mapped, via
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho,
to a target sentence of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,
which implies that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|L(T)|=m.

We call
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r,\textrho)
the
tree-representation of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
.

Linear projections of sentences.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\hat{\textTheta},
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h={\sf height}(\texttheta),
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}m={\sf conj}(\texttheta).

Let also
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r,\textrho)
be the tree-representation of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}b
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}d
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}e
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}g
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}g
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}b
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}b
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}d
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}e

Figure 22
:
A rooted tree and its root-to-leaf paths.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P
be a root-to-leaf path in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T.

By definition, the vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P
are mapped, via
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho,
to a sequence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S_{P}:=(\texttheta_{h},\ldots,\texttheta_{0})

of sentences, where, for each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[0,h-1],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i}
is a conjunctive term of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i+1}.

Also by definition,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{h}=\texttheta
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{0}
is a sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}_{0}^{({\sf c})}.

Notice that, as every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i}\in\hat{\textTheta}_{i},

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[h],
there exists a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta_{i}\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\bf tw}[[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}_{i}\}]

and an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\texttheta_{i}=\textbeta_{i}\triangleright(\texttheta_{i,1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\texttheta_{i,\ell_{i}}),

where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i,1},\ldots,\texttheta_{i,\ell}
are the conjunctive terms of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i},
one of them being equal to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i-1}.

Also, since every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i},i\in[0,h-1],
is a conjunctive term of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i+1},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i}
is also a
sentence that belongs in

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\hat{\textTheta}_{i}[\texttau]}^{({\sf c})}.

This allows us, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[0,h-1],
to consider a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\circ/\bullet
-flag corresponding to whether
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i}\in\hat{\textTheta}_{i}[\texttau]
or
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i}\in{\hat{\textTheta}_{i}[\texttau]}^{({\sf c})}\setminus\hat{\textTheta}_{i}[\texttau].

Therefore, we associate the sequence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S_{P}=(\texttheta_{h},\ldots,\texttheta_{0})
with a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\circ/\bullet
-scenario
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h,

where for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[0,h-1],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w_{i}=\circ
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i}\in{\hat{\textTheta}_{i}[\texttau]}^{({\sf c})}\setminus\hat{\textTheta}_{i}[\texttau]
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w_{i}=\bullet
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i}\in\hat{\textTheta}_{i}[\texttau].

We set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{P}
to be the sentence defined as follows:

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{P}_{0}:=\texttheta_{0},

•

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[h],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{P}_{i}:=\textbeta_{i}\triangleright\texttheta^{P}_{i-1},
and

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{P}:=\texttheta^{P}_{h}.

We call
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{P}
the
linear projection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P
.
It is now easy to see the following:

Observation 48
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\hat{\textTheta}
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r,\textrho)
be its tree-representation.
For every root-to-leaf path
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T,
the linear projection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{P}
f
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P
is a sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}_{h},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h={\sf height}(\texttheta).

Focused linear projections.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\hat{\textTheta},
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h={\sf height}(\texttheta),
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}m={\sf conj}(\texttheta),
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r,\textrho)
be the tree-representation of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

For each root-to-leaf path
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T,
we consider the linear projection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{P}
f
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P.

Due to
Subsection 11.1
, every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{P}
is
a sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}_{h}.

To ease the readability, we consider an arbitrary ordering of the root-to-leaf paths of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T,
say
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{1},\ldots,P_{m},
and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],
we set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{(i)}:=\texttheta^{P_{i}}.

The fact that, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{(i)}
is a sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}_{h},
allows us to mimic the set of definitions in
Subsection 10.2
.
To do so,
first let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q,l\in\mathbb{N},
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
be a collection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
constant symbols not contained in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau,
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{{\sf R}\}\cup{\bf Q}
be a set of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}2q+1
unary relation symbols not contained in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau.

Also, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],
let

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal X}_{i}=\{{\sf X}_{i,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{i,h}\}
be a set of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h
unary relation symbols not contained in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau,

and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w_{i}
be the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\circ/\bullet
-scenario of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{(i)}.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],

we let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma_{i}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu_{i}
be the target sentences of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{(i)}

and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textzeta_{i}
be the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
-apex-projected sentence of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma_{i}.

Also, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],
we
define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{q}^{(i)\sf out}
to be the sentence as in
Subsection 10.2
.
Finally, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],
we define
the formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}^{(i)}({\sf X}_{i,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{i,h}),
which is a formula with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}_{i,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{i,h}
as free variables and that is obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{(i)}
as follows:

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}^{(i)}({\sf X}_{i,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{i,h})=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\framebox{$\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\texttheta^{(i)\sf out}_{{q}}}({\sf X}_{i,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{i,h})$}}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\wedge

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0}\framebox{$\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\exists{\sf C}\ \texteta_{w\text{-}{\sf pr}_{\{{\sf X}_{i,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{i,h}\},{\sf C}}}\ \wedge{\breve{\textzeta}_{i{\sf R}}|_{{\sf ap}_{\bf c}}}|_{{\sf ind}_{\sf C}}$}}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\wedge

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\framebox{$\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\exists{\sf C}\ \texteta_{w\text{-}{\sf pr}_{\{{\sf X}_{i,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{i,h}\},{\sf C}}}\ \wedge\textmu_{i}|_{{\sf ind}_{\sf C}}$}}.

We stress that the only difference between the definition of a split sentence given in
Equation 23
in
Subsection 10.2
and the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}^{(i)}
given above is the following:
in the definition of a split sentence in
Equation 23
,
the second-order variables
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}_{i,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{i,h}
are quantified by an existential quantifier inside the sentence,
while, in the the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}^{(i)}
given here,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}_{i,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{i,h}
are considered as free variables of the formula.
We call
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}^{(i)}({\sf X}_{i,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{i,h}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],
a
focused linear projection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
.

Equality-checking formulas.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\hat{\textTheta},
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h={\sf height}(\texttheta),
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}m={\sf conj}(\texttheta),
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r,\textrho)
be the tree-representation of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

We now define a binary relation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf Eq}
over
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}[m]\times[h].

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i,j\in[m]
with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\neq j.

We consider the root-to-leaf paths
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{i}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{j}
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}z
be the minimum height of a node in the intersection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{i}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{j}.

It is easy to see that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}z\in[h].
We include in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf Eq}
the two pairs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(i,z)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(j,z).

In this way, we define the binary relation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf Eq}\subseteq([m]\times[h])^{2}.

We call
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf Eq}
the
equality relation corresponding to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r,\textrho)
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}.

Given a binary relation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Q\subseteq([a]\times[b])^{2},
we define the formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textxi_{Q}
with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a\cdot b
free second-order variables
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}_{1,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{a,b}
to be the following:

\textxi_{Q}=\bigwedge_{\{(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}\in Q}{\sf X}_{i,j}={\sf X}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}.

Observe that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textxi_{Q}\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\{{\sf X}_{1,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{a,b}\}].

In the case that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Q={\sf Eq},
we call the formula
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textxi_{\sf Eq}
the
equality-checking formula of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r,\textrho)
.

Tree-projected versions of sentences.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\hat{\textTheta},
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h={\sf height}(\texttheta),
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}m={\sf conj}(\texttheta),
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r,\textrho)
be the tree-representation of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{(i)},i\in[m],
be the linear projections of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

Also, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q,l\in\mathbb{N},
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf c}
be a collection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
constant symbols not contained in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau,
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{{\sf R}\}\cup{\bf Q}
be a set of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}2q+1
unary relation symbols not contained in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau.

Also, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],
let

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal X}_{i}=\{{\sf X}_{i,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{i,h}\}
be a set of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h
unary symbols not contained in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau,

and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w_{i}
be the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\circ/\bullet
-scenario of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{(i)}.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
be an enhanced version of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

We consider the focused linear projections
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}^{(i)},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
and
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textxi_{\sf Eq}
be the equality-checking formula of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r,\textrho).

Then, we define the sentence

\hat{\texttheta}:=\exists{\bf X}\ \textxi_{\sf Eq}({\bf X})\wedge\bigwedge_{i\in[m]}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}^{(i)}({\sf X}_{i,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{i,h}),

where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf X}=({\sf X}_{1,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{m,h}).

Observe that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\texttheta}\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf R}\}\cup{\bf Q}\cup{\bf c}].

We call
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\texttheta}
the
tree-projected version of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
.
By the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\texttheta}
and using
Subsection 10.2
, it is easy to prove the following:

Lemma 49
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
be an enhanced version of a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\hat{\textTheta}
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l\in\mathbb{N}.

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{{q}}
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q
-pseudogrid in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{q}={\sf height}(\texttheta)\cdot({\bf tw}(\texttheta)+1)^{2}+1,
and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
is an apex-tuple of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l,

then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},R,{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\iff(\mathfrak{A},R,{\bf W}_{{q}},{\bf a})\models\hat{\texttheta}.

In what follows, we describe how to extend the signatures and characteristics defined in
Subsection 10.3
to capture sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\textTheta}.

ut-signature.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],

we set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau_{i}^{\prime}:=\texttau\cup{\bf Q}\cup\{{\sf R}\}\cup\{{\sf X}_{i,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{i,h}\}\cup{\bf c}
and let

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf SIG}_{\sf out}}=\{({\bf H}_{1,1},\ldots,{\bf H}_{m,h},\bar{\textphi}_{1},\ldots,\bar{\textphi}_{m})

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\mid

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\exists\ell_{1,1},\ldots,\ell_{m,h}\in[0,{\bf tw}(\texttheta)]\mbox{\penalty\ such that}

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i,j\in[m]\times[h],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf H}_{i,j}\in{\cal H}^{(\ell_{i,j})}
and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\ \ \ \mbox{for every $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],$\penalty\ }\bar{\textphi}_{i}\in{{\sf rep}^{(\sum_{j=1}^{h}\ell_{i,j})}_{\texttau_{i}^{\prime}}(\texttheta^{\sf out}_{i,q})}\}.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{K}=(\mathfrak{A}[V(K^{\bf a})],{\bf a},{\bf I},{\bf W}_{{q}})
be the extended compass of a flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus V({\bf a})
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}2w+j,
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R\subseteq V(K^{\bf a}),
let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{p}),
where for each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}d_{i}\in[r,w],
and let, for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal L}_{i}=\{L_{i,1},\ldots,L_{i,h}\}\in(2^{[l]})^{h}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal Z}_{i}=\{Z_{i,1},\ldots,Z_{i,h}\}
be a collection of subsets of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I^{(d_{i}-r+1)}.

We define

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf out}\text{-}{\sf sig}}(\mathfrak{K},R,{\bf d},{\cal L}_{1},\ldots,{\cal L}_{m},{\cal Z}_{1},\ldots,{\cal Z}_{m})
to be the following set:

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\{({\bf H}_{1,1},\ldots,{\bf H}_{m,h},\bar{\textphi}_{1},\ldots,\bar{\textphi}_{m})\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf SIG}_{\sf out}}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\mid

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i,j\in[m]\times[h],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\exists\ F_{i,j}\in{\cal F}^{V_{L_{i,j}}({\bf a})}_{|V(H_{i,j})|-|\partial_{\mathfrak{K}}(Z_{i,j})|}\mbox{\penalty\ such that}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \textxi_{\sf Eq}(F_{1,1},\ldots,F_{m,h})\mbox{ is true and}

if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal V}_{i,j}=(\partial_{\mathfrak{K}}(Z_{i,j}),V_{L_{i,j}}({\bf a}),V(F_{i,j})\setminus V_{L_{i,j}}({\bf a}))

and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf H}_{i,j}=(H_{i,j},{\cal U}_{i,j}),

then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal V}_{i,j}
is a nice 3-partition

of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K^{\bf a}[\partial_{\mathfrak{K}}(Z_{i,j})\cup V_{L_{i,j}}({\bf a})]\cup F_{i,j}
and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K^{\bf a}[\partial_{\mathfrak{K}}(Z_{i,j})\cup V_{L_{i,j}}({\bf a})]\cup F_{i,j}
is strongly

isomorphic to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H_{i,j}
with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}({\cal V}_{i,j},{\cal U}_{i,j}),

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\exists\ \mbox{an ordering $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf b}_{i}$ of $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bigcup_{j\in[h]}\big(\partial_{\mathfrak{K}}(Z_{i,j})\cup V(F_{i,j})\big)$ and }

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i,j\in[m]\times[h],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\exists\ \tilde{X}_{i,j}\subseteq Z_{i,j}\cup V(F_{i,j})\mbox{\penalty\ such that}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \textxi_{\sf Eq}(\tilde{X}_{1,1},\ldots,\tilde{X}_{m,h})\mbox{ is true,}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \partial_{\mathfrak{K}}(Z_{i,j})\cup V(F_{i,j})\subseteq\tilde{X}_{i,j}\mbox{\penalty\ and}

if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal X}_{i}=\{\tilde{X}_{i,1},\ldots,\tilde{X}_{i,h}\}
and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R_{i}^{\prime}=\bigcup_{j\in[h]}R\cap(Z_{i,j}\setminus\partial_{\mathfrak{K}}(Z_{i,j})),
then

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \big(\mathfrak{A}^{(d_{i},{\cal Z}_{i},{\cal L}_{i},{\cal F}_{i})},R_{i}^{\prime},{\bf W}_{q},\varnothing^{l},{\cal X}_{i},{\bf b}_{i}\big)\models\bar{\textphi}_{i}\}.

In-signature.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],

we let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma_{i}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu_{i}
be the target sentences of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{(i)}

and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textzeta_{i}
be the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
-apex-projected sentence of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma_{i}.

Also, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}_{i\sf R}\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}\cup\{{\sf R}\}]

be the sentence obtained for a Gaifman sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}_{i}
that is equivalent to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textzeta_{i},
after restricting its “scattered” variables of its basic local sentences to be contained in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf R},
as done in
Subsection 6.2
.
For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],

let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}_{i,1},\ldots,\breve{\textzeta}_{i,p_{i}}\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau^{\langle\bf c\rangle}\cup\{{\sf R}\}],

be the corresponding basic local sentences of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}_{i\sf R}

with the corresponding integers
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell_{i,1},\ldots,\ell_{i,p_{i}}

and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r_{i,1},\ldots,r_{i,p_{i}}
(all depending on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{\textzeta}
).
We define

{\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0}{\sf SIG}_{\sf in}}=2^{[\ell_{1,1}]}\times\cdots\times 2^{[\ell_{m,p_{m}}]}\times[w].

We define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0}{\sf in\mbox{-}sig}}(\mathfrak{K},R,{\bf d},{\cal L}_{1},\ldots,{\cal L}_{m},{\cal Z}_{1},\ldots,{\cal Z}_{m})
to be the following set:

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\{(Y_{1,1},\ldots,Y_{m,p_{m}},t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0}{\sf SIG}_{\sf in}}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\mid

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ t_{i}\leq d_{i}\mbox{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\exists\ C_{i}\in{\sf pr}(K^{\bf a}[I^{(d_{i})}],{\bf W}_{q},\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}{\cal Z}_{i})\mbox{\penalty\ and}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \exists\ (\tilde{X}_{i,1},\ldots,\tilde{X}_{i,p_{i}})\mbox{\penalty\ such that\penalty\ }\forall j\in[p_{i}]\

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{X}_{i,j}=\{x_{i}^{j}\mid i\in Y_{j}\},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{X}_{i,j}\subseteq(I^{(t_{i}-\hat{r}+1)}\setminus\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}{\cal Z}_{i})\cap R,\text{ and}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{if\penalty\ }{\bf a}_{i}^{\prime}={\bf a}\setminus V_{\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}{\cal L}_{i}}({\bf a}),\text{ then}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{X}_{h}\text{\penalty\ is\penalty\ }(|Y_{h}|,r_{h})\text{-scattered}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{in\penalty\ }{\sf ap}_{{\bf c}}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a}_{i}^{\prime})[I^{(t_{i})}\setminus\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}{\cal Z}_{i}]\text{ and }

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ {\sf ap}_{{\bf c}}(\mathfrak{A},{\bf a}_{i}^{\prime})[C_{i}\cup V({\bf a}_{i}^{\prime})]\models\bigwedge_{x\in\tilde{X}_{i,j}}\textpsi_{i,j}(x)\}.

We finally define

{\sf CHAR}=([r,w])^{m}\times((2^{[l]})^{h})^{m}\times 2^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf SIG}_{\sf out}}}\times 2^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0}{\sf SIG}_{\sf in}}}

and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\texttheta\text{-}{\sf char}(\mathfrak{K},R)

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\{({\bf d},{\cal L}_{1},\ldots,{\cal L}_{m},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf sig}_{\sf out}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0}{\sf sig}_{\sf in}})\in{\sf CHAR}\mid

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m]

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\exists\ Z_{i,1},\ldots,Z_{i,h}\subseteq I^{(d_{i}-r+1)}\mbox{\penalty\ such that}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\textxi_{\sf Eq}(Z_{1,1},\ldots,Z_{m,h})\mbox{ is true and}

if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal Z}_{i}=\{Z_{i,1},\ldots,Z_{i,h}\},
then

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf out}\text{-}{\sf sig}}(\mathfrak{K},R,{\bf d},{\cal L}_{1},\ldots,{\cal L}_{m},{\cal Z}_{1},\ldots,{\cal Z}_{m})={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf sig}_{\sf out}},\mbox{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0}{\sf in\mbox{-}sig}}(\mathfrak{K},R,{\bf d},{\cal L}_{1},\ldots,{\cal L}_{m},{\cal Z}_{1},\ldots,{\cal Z}_{m})={\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0}{\sf sig}_{\sf in}}\}.

Having stated the above definitions, we now sketch how to prove
Subsection 8.1
for a sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\textTheta}[\texttau].

Our argumentation is very similar to the case of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}[\texttau].

We run the algorithm
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\tt Find\_Equiv\_FlatPairs}
presented in
Subsection 9.5
,
which finds a collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\cal W}^{\prime}=\{(\tilde{W}_{1},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{1}),\ldots,(\tilde{W}_{p},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{p})\}

of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}p=q\cdot(\hat{\ell}+3)
flatness pairs that are
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
-equivalent. i.e., their
extended compasses have the same characteristic, as defined in the previous paragraph.
The algorithm

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\tt Find\_Equiv\_FlatPairs}

also outputs
the set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y:=V({\sf compass}_{\breve{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}}(\breve{W}^{\prime})),
where

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\breve{W}^{\prime},\breve{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{W}
-tilt
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{W}
is the central
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j^{\prime}
-subwall of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{W}_{1},

and
a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\bullet}
-tilt

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})

of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R}),

where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\bullet}
is be the central
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}g
-subwall of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{W}_{1}.

We now provide a sketch of proof for the correctness of the above algorithm, i.e., we prove that

(\mathfrak{A},R,{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\iff(\mathfrak{A}\setminus V({\sf compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime})),R\setminus Y,{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

We consider the tree-projected version
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\texttheta}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

Due to
Subsection 11.1
, if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q
-pseudogrid of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{q}={\sf height}(\texttheta)\cdot({\bf tw}(\texttheta)+1)^{2}+1,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},R,{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\iff(\mathfrak{A},R,{\bf W}_{q},{\bf a})\models\hat{\texttheta}.

For each linear projection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{(i)},i\in[m]
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,

we treat the focused linear projection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}^{(i)}({\sf X}_{i,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{i,h})
separately.
Intuitively,
for each sequence of disjoint sets
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{i,1},\ldots,X_{i,h}
assigned to the
free variables
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf X}_{i,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{i,h}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}^{(i)},

we find a “buffer”
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}d_{i}\in[r,w]
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}_{1},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{1})
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bigcup_{j}\in[h]X_{i,j}
does not intersect
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I_{1}^{(d_{i})}\setminus I_{1}^{(d_{i}-r+1)}.

We set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal X}_{\rm in}^{(i)}
to be the collection of the sets
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{i,j}\cap I_{1}^{(d_{i}-r+1)},j\in[h]
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal X}_{\rm out}^{(i)}=\{X_{i,1}^{\rm out},\ldots,X_{i,h}^{\rm out}\},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{i,j}^{\rm out}=X_{i,j}\cap I_{1}^{(d)}=\emptyset,j\in[h].

For each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],
there exists some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k_{i}\in[p]
such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}_{k_{i}},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{i})\in\tilde{\cal W}^{\prime}\setminus\{(\tilde{W}_{1},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{1})\}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V({\sf compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{k_{i}}})
is disjoint from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bigcup_{j}\in[h]X_{i,j}.

We stress that for each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[m],
there is a possibly different
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k_{i}\in[p]
such that the above holds.
Our purpose is to “replace”
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal X}_{\sf in}^{(i)}
with an other collection

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal X}^{(i)^{\prime}}=\{X_{i,1}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{i,h}^{\prime}\}
of sets that are inside
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}I_{k_{i}}^{(d_{i}-r+1)},
that is an “inner” part of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}_{k_{i}},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{i}).

Notice that we can treat each focused linear projection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}_{q}^{(i)}({\sf X}_{i,1},\ldots,{\sf X}_{i,h})

and thus every sequence of sets
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{i,1},\ldots,X_{i,h}
separately, as
the equality-checking formula in the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\texttheta}
is also incorporated inside the definition of characteristic and therefore, when
replacing
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal X}_{\rm in}^{(i)}
with an other collection

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal X}^{(i)^{\prime}},
the satisfaction of equality-checking formula from
all
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal X}^{(i)^{\prime}},i\in[m]

will prove that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\texttheta}
is satisfied by interpreting
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf X}
as

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{1,1},\ldots,X_{m,h}
if and only if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\texttheta}
is satisfied by interpreting
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf X}
as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{1,1}^{\rm out}\cup X_{1,1}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{m,h}^{\rm out}\cup X_{m,h}^{\prime}.

We avoid to present a detailed proof for all the above, as it can be reproduced
by local adjustments in the proof in
Appendix B
.

11.2
Disjunctions of conjunctions

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary.
In this subsection we aim to provide the additional ideas that we need in order to complete the proof of
Subsection 5.4
, that is, dealing with the logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta[\texttau].

Recall that we defined
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\textTheta_{i},
where

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\textTheta_{0}[\texttau]

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\{\textsigma\wedge\textmu\mid\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau]\mbox{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\textmu\in{\sf EM}[\texttau]\}\mbox{\penalty\ and}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\mbox{for $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\geq 1,$ }\textTheta_{i}[\texttau]

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\{\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma\mid\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\bf tw}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]\mbox{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\textgamma\in{\bf PB}(\textTheta_{i-1}[\texttau]^{({\sf c})})\}.

Following
Section 11
,
for each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\geq 1,
in the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{i}[\texttau]
we can replace the term

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma\in{\bf PB}(\textTheta_{i-1}[\texttau]^{({\sf c})})
by

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma\in{\bf DISJ}({\bf CONJ}(\textTheta_{i-1}[\texttau]^{({\sf c})})).

Having dealt with conjunctions in the previous subsection, we now aim to
resolve the case of disjunctions.
We first start by giving some additional definitions.

Disjunctive terms of sentences.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta_{i}[\texttau].

Notice that there exist a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{{\bf tw}}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}_{i}\}]
and a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma\in{\bf DISJ}({\bf CONJ}({\hat{\textTheta}_{i-1}[\texttau]}^{({\sf c})}))
such that

\texttheta=\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma.

Equivalently, there exist

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi_{1},\ldots,\textpsi_{\ell}\in{\bf CONJ}(\textTheta_{i-1}[\texttau]^{({\sf c})}),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
is the disjunction-width of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma,
such that

\texttheta=\textbeta\triangleright(\textpsi_{1}\vee\ldots\vee\textpsi_{\ell}).

We call the sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi_{1},\ldots,\textpsi_{\ell}
the
disjunctive terms of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
.
We stress that, since
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf DISJ}({\bf CONJ}(\textTheta_{0}))={\bf DISJ}(\textTheta_{0})
(this follows by the fact that
all target sentences are conjunctions), every sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta_{1}
can have several disjunctive terms.

Boolean terms of sentences.

As each disjunctive term
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi_{i}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
is a sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf CONJ}(\textTheta_{i-1}[\texttau]^{({\sf c})}),

for each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[\ell]
there exist sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{i,1},\ldots,\texttheta_{i,k_{i}}\in\textTheta_{i-1}[\texttau]^{({\sf c})},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k_{i}
is the conjunction-width of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi_{i},

such that

\textpsi_{i}=\texttheta_{i,1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\texttheta_{i,k_{i}}.

Therefore, we can write

\texttheta=\textbeta\triangleright\Big((\texttheta_{1,1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\texttheta_{1,k_{1}})\vee\ldots\vee(\texttheta_{\ell,1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\texttheta_{\ell,k_{\ell}})\Big).

We call the sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{1,1},\ldots,\texttheta_{1,k_{1}},\ldots,\texttheta_{\ell,1},\ldots,\texttheta_{\ell,k_{\ell}}
the
Boolean terms of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
.

We now recursively define a function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf width}:\textTheta[\texttau]\to\mathbb{N}
as follows:

•

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in{\textTheta}_{0}[\texttau],
we set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf width}(\texttheta):=1.

•

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\geq 1
and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in{\textTheta}_{i}[\texttau],

we set

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf width}(\texttheta):=\sum_{i\in[\ell]}\sum_{j\in[k_{i}]}{\sf width}(\texttheta_{i,j}),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{1,1},\ldots,\texttheta_{1,k_{1}},\ldots,\texttheta_{\ell,1},\ldots,\texttheta_{\ell,k_{\ell}}
are the Boolean terms of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

Following the above definition, we stress that each sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta
has
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf width}(\texttheta)
target sentences.

The ultimate goal of the rest of this subsection is to define a notion of a
conjunctive scenario
of a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta,
expressed in terms of a tree-representation as in the previous subsection,
and to prove that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
is satisfied by a structure if and only if this structure satisfies at least one of the conjunctive scenarios of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

Representations of sentences in trees.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
be a vocabulary and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h:={\sf height}(\texttheta)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}m:={\sf width}(\texttheta).

We define the
representation of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
as the triple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r,\textrho),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r)\in{\cal T}_{2h,m}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho:V(T)\to\textTheta,
such that

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho(r)=\texttheta,

•

for every parent node
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in L_{2i},i\in[h],
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho(v)=\texttheta,
then the children nodes of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v
are mapped, via
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho,
to the disjunctive terms of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,
and

•

for every parent node
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in L_{2i-1},i\in[h],
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho(v)=\textpsi,
then the children nodes of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v
are mapped, via
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho,
to the conjunctive terms of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpsi.

We stress that, if for a parent node
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}L_{2i},i\in[h]
(resp. in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}L_{2i-1},i\in[h]
), the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho(v)
has only one disjunctive (resp. conjunctive) term, then its only child node is mapped, via
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho,
to this single disjunctive (resp. conjunctive) term.
We can observe that, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[h],
every vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in L_{2i}
is mapped, via
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho,
to a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta_{i}
and its grandchildren are nodes in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}L_{2(i-1)}
that are mapped, via
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho,
to the Boolean terms of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,
which in turn are sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{i-1}.

The leaves of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r)
are mapped to the target sentences of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
(thus, belonging to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{0}
).
Every root-to-leaf path of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T
has length
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}2h.

Conjunctive scenarios of sentences.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r,\textrho)
be the representation of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

We now aim to define a collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal T}^{\prime}
of subtrees of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T.

A subtree
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T^{\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T
belongs in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal T}^{\prime}

if and only if

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\in V(T^{\prime}),
all root-to-leaf paths of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T^{\prime}
have length
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}2h-1,
and, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[h],
for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in L_{2i}(T^{\prime}),
there exists
a child
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}u_{v}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}u_{v}\in V(T^{\prime}),
and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in L_{2i-1}(T^{\prime}),
every child
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}u_{v}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v
belongs to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(T^{\prime}).

​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​​

Figure 23
:
A rooted tree and the collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal T}^{\prime}.

We denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal S}=\{(L_{1}(T^{\prime}),L_{3}(T^{\prime}),\ldots,L_{2h-1}(T^{\prime}))\mid T^{\prime}\in{\cal T}^{\prime}\}.

Notice that for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S\in{\cal S},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S=(V_{1},\ldots,V_{h}),
the
graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T_{S}:=T[\bigcup_{i\in[h]}N_{(T,r)}[V_{i}]]
is a tree of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}2h
that is an induced subgraph of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T.

By construction, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[h]
and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in L_{2i}(T_{S}),

there is exactly one
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}u_{v}\in N_{(T,r)}(v)
that is contained in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(T_{S})

and
all the vertices in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}N_{(T,r)}(u_{v})
are contained in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(T_{S}).

Following this, note that if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho(v)
is a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta_{i}^{({\sf c})},
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho(u_{v})
is a disjunctive term of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
(and the choice of a unique
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}u_{v}
for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v
corresponds to the choice of a unique such a disjunctive term for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho(v)
)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{\textrho(w)\mid w\in N_{(T,r)}(u_{v})\}
is the set of all conjunctive terms of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho(u_{v}),
that are sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{i-1}^{({\sf c})}.

For this reason, we call every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S\in{\cal S}
a
conjunctive scenario
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

Sentences corresponding to conjunctive scenarios.

Let now
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(T,r,\textrho)
be a representation of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

Note that for each even
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[2h]
and every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in L_{i}(T),

there exists a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\bf tw}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}]

such that

\textrho(v)=\textbeta\triangleright(\bigvee_{u\in{N_{(T,r)}(v)}}\textrho(u)),

while, for every odd
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[2h]
and every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in L_{i}(T),

\textrho(v)=\bigwedge_{u\in{N_{(T,r)}(v)}}\textrho(u).

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S
be a conjunctive scenario of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,

and consider the subtree
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T_{S}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T.

By the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T_{S},
for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[h]
and every vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in L_{2i}(T_{S}),
there is a unique vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}u_{v}\in N_{(T,r)}(v)\cap V(T_{S}).

We define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T^{\star}_{S}
to be the tree obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T_{S}
after contracting, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[h]
and every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in L_{2i}(T_{S}),
the edge
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{v,u_{v}\}.

Observe that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T^{\star}_{S}
is a tree of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}h
and, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[0,h],
the vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}L_{i}(T_{S}^{\star})
are the vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}L_{2i}(T_{S}).

We define a function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho_{S}^{\star}:V(T^{\star}_{S})\to\textTheta

where

•

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in L_{0}(T_{S}^{\star}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho_{S}^{\star}(v)=\textrho(v),
and

•

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[h]
and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in L_{i}(T_{S}^{\star}),

if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho(v)=\textbeta\triangleright(\bigvee_{u\in{N_{(T_{S},r)}(v)}}\textrho(u)),
for some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\bf tw}[\texttau\cup\{{\sf X}\}],
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho_{S}^{\star}(v)=\textbeta\triangleright(\bigwedge_{w\in{N_{(T_{S}^{\star},r)}(v)}}\textrho^{\star}_{S}(w)).

Observe that for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[0,h]
and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in L_{i}(T_{S}^{\star}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho^{\star}_{S}(v)
is a sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\textTheta}_{i}.

We call
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho_{S}^{\star}(r)
the
sentence corresponding to the scenario
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S
and we denote it by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{S}.

Since, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S\in{\cal S},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{S}
is a sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\textTheta}_{h},

we can consider its tree-representation and its tree-projected version
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\texttheta}_{S},
as defined in
Subsection 11.1
.

The next lemma says that, given a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta,
its
enhanced version
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
is equivalent to satisfying
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{\texttheta}_{S}
for at least one conjunctive scenario
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

Its proof can be easily derived using
Subsection 10.2
.

Lemma 50
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
be an enhanced version of a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l\in\mathbb{N}.

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
-structure,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R\subseteq V(\mathfrak{A}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{{q}}
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q
-pseudogrid in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{\mathfrak{A}},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{q}={\sf height}(\texttheta)\cdot({\bf tw}(\texttheta)+1),
and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
is an apex-tuple of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l,

then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{A},R,{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\iff(\mathfrak{A},R,{\bf W}_{{q}},{\bf a})\models\bigvee_{S\in{\cal S}}\hat{\texttheta}_{S}.

In what follows we sketch the proof of
Subsection 8.1
, for a general
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal S}=\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{s}\}
be the set of all conjunctive scenarios of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta.

First of all, we define

\texttheta\text{-}{\sf char}(\mathfrak{K},R)=\Big(\texttheta_{S_{1}}\text{-}{\sf char}(\mathfrak{K},R),\ldots,\texttheta_{S_{s}}\text{-}{\sf char}(\mathfrak{K},R)\Big).

(24)

Then, we follow the same arguments as described at the end of
Subsection 11.1
.
In our current case, when considering a collection of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
-equivalent extended compasses, following the definition of the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta
-characteristic given above, these extended compasses are

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{S}
-equivalent for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S\in{\cal S}.

Therefore, when we find a part of a wall and declare it “irrelevant”,
this part is “irrelevant” in any possible conjunctive scenario of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,
and the proof is complete.

12
Constructibility issues

In this section we argue that our algorithm can be effectively constructed (see
Subsection 12.1
) and present some consequences of our technique in the context of constructing the obstruction set of minor-closed graph classes (see
Subsection 12.2
).

12.1
Constructing our (meta-)algorithm

All subroutines of our algorithm for deciding models of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
-sentences can be effectively constructed, given a bound on the Hadwiger number of the corresponding sentence.
In order to do so, basic ingredients are (i) the quadratic algorithm for minor containment of
[
91
]
(ii) the linear algorithms from
[
130
,
129
]
(that are, in turn, based on the algorithm of
[
93
]
) for finding a flat wall whose compass has bounded treewidth, and (iii) the computation of all the out- and in-signatures that is done in linear time, using the treewidth bound and Courcelle’s theorem, applied on sentences whose size effectively bounded by the constants of Gaifman’s theorem. As the dependencies in Gaifman’s theorem as well as in the algorithm of Courcelle’s theorem can be effectively (however non-elementarily) bounded (see e.g.,
[
61
,
42
,
23
,
77
,
84
,
64
]
), it follows that our algorithm for deciding models of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
-sentences is also effectively constructible. We summarize this discussion in the following theorem.

Theorem 51
.

There is a Turing machine that
receives as input a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\textTheta
and an upper bound on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf hw}({\rm Mod}(\texttheta))
and returns as output the quadratic algorithm of
Subsection 5.4
.

The above theorem permits us to state
Subsection 5.4
(and
Subsection 5.4
as well) so that the running time is bounded by

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f(|\texttheta|)\cdot n^{2}
(resp.
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f(|\texttheta|,{\bf hw})\cdot n^{2}
) for some
constructive
function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f.

In this paper we did not focus on optimizing this function or even giving explicit upper bounds for it. We believe that for certain instantiations (or parameterizations) of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta,
providing reasonable upper or lower bounds for the function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f
is an interesting research direction.

12.2
Constructibility horizon of Robertson-Seymour’s theorem

Recall that the
(minor) obstrucion set
of a graph class
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
is the set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\cal G})
of all minor-mininal graphs that are not contained in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}.

When
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
is minor-closed, the set

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\cal G})

completely
characterizes
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G},
as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}={\sf excl}({\sf obs}({\cal G})).

By definition, no two elements of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\cal G})
are comparable with respect to the minor relation, therefore, by Robertson-Seymour’s theorem
[
126
]
,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\cal G})
is always a finite set. Unfortunately, while we “know” the finiteness of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\cal G}),
there is no general way to construct this set, given some (finite) description of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}

[
49
]
(see also
[
62
,
101
]
).
This means that we may resort to a case study of proving bounds
7
7

7

Notice that if we have a bound on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\cal G})
one may use the “finite description” of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
in order to identify all obstructions of

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\cal G}),
by exhaustive search.
on the size of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\cal G})

for particular instantiations of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
(see
[
2
,
106
,
130
,
1
,
27
,
34
]
).
As an attempt to enlarge the constructibility horizon of Robertson-Seymour’s theorem, researchers have
considered several mechanisms to build minor-closed graphs classes from simpler ones. An interesting problem is whether it is possible to

construct
the obstruction of the new class given the obstructions of the simpler ones.
To detect the widest possible set of operations between graphs classes that maintains this constructibility is an interesting challenge.
We proceed with some definitions.

Constructive operations.

Given an integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 1,
a
graph class operation of arity
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
is
any function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{f}:(2^{{\cal G}_{\sf all}})^{r}\rightarrow 2^{{\cal G}_{\sf all}}.

Such an operation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{f}
is
minor-invariant
if whenever
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}_{1},\ldots,{\cal G}_{r}
are minor-closed graph classes, then so is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{f}({\cal G}_{1},\ldots,{\cal G}_{r}).

We say that a minor-invariant graph class operation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{f}
is
explicitly constructive
if there is a computable function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f:\mathbb{N}^{r}\to\mathbb{N}
such that if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}_{1},\ldots,{\cal G}_{r}
are minor-closed graph classes, then

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf hw}({\cal G})\leq f(\max\{{\bf hw}({\cal G}_{i})\mid i\in[r]\}).

It is easy to verify that the intersection operation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\cap
is explicitly constructive.
The case of the union operation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\cup
is more difficult and has been studied by Adler, Grohe, and Kreutzer
[
2
]
– see also
[
106
]
where the notion of
intertwines
has been introduced.
It has also been proved by Bulian and Dawar
[
24
]
that the operation​
c
​ defined as

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}^{\sf c}=\{G\mid\forall C\in{\sf cc}(G),G\in{\cal G}\}
is also explicitly constructive.
The same was proven recently in
[
44
]
for the operation
b

defined as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}^{\sf b}=\{G\mid\forall C\in{\sf bc}(G),G\in{\cal G}\},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf bc}(G)
is the set of all blocks of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G.
We enlarge this set of operations by defining the
graph class operation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,
as follows

{\cal B}\mbox{\footnotesize$\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,$}{\cal G}=\{G\mid\exists X\subseteq V(G),{\sf torso}(G,X)\in{\cal B}\ \wedge\ G\setminus X\in{\cal G}\}.

It is easy to prove that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,
is minor-invariant.
8
8

8

Here we should stress that, alternatively, one might define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal B}\mbox{\footnotesize$\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,$}{\cal G}
by replacing
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf torso}
by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf torso}^{+}
as defined in
footnote 2
. The main statement of this subsection copies for this definition as well.

Notice that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,
is strongly related with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\triangleright
and the above definition closely imitates the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\textTheta}_{1}.

Our results imply the following.

Theorem 52
.

The operation

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,
is explicitly constructive when restricted to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal B}
’s where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\cal B})
contains some planar graph.

Proof (sketch).

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\in{\sf obs}({\cal B}\mbox{\footnotesize$\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,$}{\cal G}),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in V(G),
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}=G\setminus v.

Let also
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma\in\bar{\textTheta}_{1},

where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta
expresses the fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf torso}(G,X)\in{\cal B}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textgamma
expresses the fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X\in{\cal G}.
Notice that the fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\cal B})
contains some planar graph, implies that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf torso}(G,X)

has bounded treewidth, therefore there is indeed some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\sf tw}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\rm Mod}(\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma)={\cal B}\mbox{\footnotesize$\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,$}{\cal G}.

Since
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\in{\sf obs}({\cal B}\mbox{\footnotesize$\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,$}{\cal G}),
we get
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}\in{\cal B}\mbox{\footnotesize$\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,$}{\cal G},
therefore
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf hw}(G)\leq{\bf hw}(G^{\prime})+1\leq{\bf hw}({\cal B}\mbox{\footnotesize$\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,$}{\cal G})+1\leq{\bf hw}({\cal B})+{\bf hw}({\cal G})+1.

We set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c:={\bf hw}({\cal B})+{\bf hw}({\cal G})+1.

We apply the algorithm of the proof of
Subsection 5.4
using
Subsection 8.1
instead of
Subsection 8.1

for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,V(G),{\bf a}),
no matter the choice of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a},
and the output is
either a report that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf tw}(G)\leq\hyperref@@ii[@interference]{f_{\ref*{@interference}}}(c)\cdot r

or a non-empty vertex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Z\in V(G)
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\models\texttheta\iff G\setminus Z\models\texttheta.
Here it is important to note that, in the second case, no annotation vertices appear because
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma
has no
FOL
-target sentences.
As
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\in{\sf obs}({\cal B}\mbox{\footnotesize$\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,$}{\cal G}),
this second outcome of the proof of
Subsection 5.4

is excluded, and therefore we obtain that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf tw}(G)
is bounded by some explicit function of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c.

After bounding the treewidth of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\in{\sf obs}({\cal B}\mbox{\footnotesize$\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,$}{\cal G}),
it is possible to bound its size as well, just by using the
fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\triangleright\textgamma
is a
CMSOL
-sentence.
For this, one may use the classic technique of Lagergren
[
106
]
(see also
[
105
,
107
]
as well as the more recent application in
[
130
]
) that combines the fact that

CMSOL
-sentences have finite index with
the use of lean-decompositions (see
[
68
,
88
,
89
,
67
]
for further developments of this technique.
∎

As discussed above, the operation

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,
can be seen as a way to create minor-closed classes by “composing together” simpler ones.
The only previously known result about the constructibility of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,
follows from
[
2
,
130
]
(see also
[
50
]
) for the case where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|X|\leq 1,
that is, when
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\cal B})=\{K_{2}\}.

Subsection 12.2
extends this for every obstruction set containing some planar graph.
It is an interesting question whether
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,
remains explicitly constructive if we drop the planarity condition on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\cal B}).
It is certainly desirable to give a reasonable estimation of an upper bound on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf hw}({\cal B}\mbox{\footnotesize$\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,$}{\cal G})
as a function
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf hw}({\cal B})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf hw}({\cal G}).

We believe that this is aim is not “out of reach” as, at least, one of the “sources of non-elementarity” in the dependencies of our main result, namely Gaifman’s theorem, is missing in the proof of
Subsection 12.2
.

13
From
FOL
to
FOL​+DP
: the compound logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Theta^{{\sf DP}}

In the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Theta_{0}
,
the base case of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Theta
, we consider compound sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\sigma\wedge\mu
, where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\sigma\in{\sf FOL}
and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mu
expresses minor-exclusion.
However,
one can consider extensions of
FOL
in the compound sentences.
A possible candidate is First-order logic with disjoint-paths predicates defined in
[
132
]
(see the paragraph below for a formal definition).
This way we can define a more general logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Theta^{{\sf DP}}
and prove an algorithmic meta-theorem that encompasses also the results in
[
73
,
74
]
.
To ease reading, in this subsection we deal only with graphs and not with general structures. However, our results can be straightforwardly be extended to general structures.

13.1
The disjoint-paths logic

We define the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}2k
-ary predicate
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf dp}_{k}({\sf x}_{1},{\sf y}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{k},{\sf y}_{k})
, which evaluates true in a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
if and only if there
are paths
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
of length at least two between (the interpretations of)
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf x}_{i}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf y}_{i}
for all
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[k]
such that for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i,j\in[k]
,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\neq j
,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(P_{i})\cap V(P_{j})=\emptyset.

We let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}\!\!+\!{\sf DP}
be the logic obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}
after allowing

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf dp}_{k}({\sf x}_{1},{\sf y}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{k},{\sf y}_{k}),k\geq 1
as atomic predicates.

The compound logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Theta^{{\sf DP}}
.

We define an extension
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Theta^{{\sf DP}}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
by considering, as the base
case, instead of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{0},
the logic

\textTheta_{0}^{{\sf DP}}=\{\textsigma\wedge\textmu\mid\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}\!+\!{\sf DP}\mbox{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\textmu\in{\sf EM}[\{{\sf E}\}]\}.

We sketch how to prove the following extension of
Subsection 1.2
.

Theorem 53
.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in\Theta^{{\sf DP}},

there exists an algorithm that, given a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
, outputs whether
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\models\texttheta

in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{|\texttheta|}(n^{2})
.

As we define the alternative
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\Theta}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Theta
,
we can also define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\Theta}^{{\sf DP}}
by taking
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\textTheta}_{0}^{{\sf DP}}={\sf FOL}\!\!+\!\!{\sf DP}
as the base case, i.e.,
by discarding the minor-exclusion from the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\textTheta}_{0}^{{\sf DP}}.

Notice that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\Theta}^{{\sf DP}}
contains
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}\!\!+\!{\sf DP}
and can be seen as a natural extension of it.
As a corollary of
Subsection 13.1
, we get the following analogue of
Subsection 1.2
.

Theorem 54
.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\texttheta}\in\tilde{\Theta}^{{\sf DP}},
there exists an algorithm that, given a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
, outputs whether
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\models\texttheta

in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{|\texttheta|,{\bf hw}(G)}(n^{2})
.

Subsection 13.1
contains all results and applications of
[
73
,
74
]
as a (very) special case.
For a visualization of the current meta-algorithmic landscape, see
Figure 24
.

of a graph

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k

13.2
Bypassing Gaifman’s Theorem

We now explain how to use use the recent results of
[
73
,
74
]

in order to modify the proofs of
Subsection 1.2
and
Subsection 1.2
, towards proving the more general versions
Subsection 13.1
and
Subsection 13.1
. For this, we essentially show how
we may bypass the computation of the in-signature (based on the application of Gaifman’s theorem)
by using instead the main result of
[
73
,
74
]
.

As a first step in the proof of
Subsection 13.1
,
we modify the definition of the enhanced version of a sentence in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Theta
(see
Section 6
)
to the corresponding notion for sentences in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Theta^{{\sf DP}}
as follows:
we consider the enhanced version
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
of every formula in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}[\texttau{+}{\sf DP}]
as defined in
[
74
, Section 4]

and we define the
enhanced version

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta

to be a sentence obtained
from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\theta
after replacing each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}[\texttau{+}{\sf DP}]
-target sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\sigma
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\theta
with the enhanced version

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
.
This way, it is easy to prove that both
Subsection 6.2
and
Subsection 6.2
hold in this setting.

The only missing ingredient for the proof of
Subsection 13.1
is an analogue of
Subsection 8.1
for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Theta^{{\sf DP}}
.
As described in
Subsection 8.1
,
to achieve this we inspect the three basic elements of our problem
(i.e., satisfaction of the modulator sentence in the modulator sets, satisfaction of the two target sentences in the “remaining part”) and
we argue how to reduce the annotated set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R
and characterize some non-annotated vertices as “irrelevant”.
As explained in
Subsection 8.1
, for the “irrelevancy” for minor-exclusion we can use
Subsection 7.2
, while the remaining two parts we design the algorithm
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\tt Find\_Equiv\_FlatPairs}
(see
Subsection 9.5
).
The proof of correctness of this algorithm is based on three Claims (see
Subsection 9.6
for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{\Theta}_{1}
).
While
Claim 1
and
Claim 3
work for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Theta^{{\sf DP}}
, this is not the case for
Claim 2
.
The reason is that
dealing with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}\!\!+\!{\sf DP}
-target sentences, we can no longer use Gaifman’s theorem and therefore we have to employ different techniques.
In fact, we use the following result from
[
74
]
that intuitively says that,
given graph that contains a “large enough” flat wall,
we can finds an area inside the flat wall that is
annotation-irrelevant
and
inside this area, the removal of any subset of vertices does not change the satisfiability of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
.
To state it we also need to define the notion of
outer-compatibility
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,G^{\prime}
be two graphs, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R,R^{\prime}
be subsets of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(G)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(G^{\prime})
, respectively,
and let a partial function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textxi:V(G)\to V(G^{\prime})
.
We say that the pairs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,R)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G^{\prime},R^{\prime})
are
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textxi
-compatible
if for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[h]
and every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in V(G)
,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in X_{i}\iff\textxi(v)\in X_{i}^{\prime}
.
Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,R)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G^{\prime},R^{\prime})
be two annotated graphs and let a partial function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textxi:V(G)\to V(G^{\prime})
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,R)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G^{\prime},R^{\prime})
are
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textxi
-compatible.
We denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,R)\oplus_{\textxi}(G^{\prime},R^{\prime})
the colored graph obtained from the disjoint union of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,R)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G^{\prime},R^{\prime})
after identifying vertices
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in V(G)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}u\in V(G)
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textxi(v)=u
.
Let two annotated graphs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,R),(G^{\prime},R^{\prime})
, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
be an apex-tuple of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
, and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
be a flatness pair of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus V({\bf a})
.
Given that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}=(X,Y,P,C,\textGamma,\textsigma,\textpi)
,
we call a partial function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textxi:V(X\cap Y)\cup V({\bf a})\to V(G^{\prime})
such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,R)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G^{\prime},R^{\prime})
are
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textxi
-compatible
an
outer-compatibility function of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,R)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G^{\prime},R^{\prime})
.

Given an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
of some graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
and an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r^{\prime}\leq r
,
we say that a subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-internal
if it is a subwall of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{(r-r^{\prime})}
.

Proposition 55

(​​
[
74
]
)
.

There are two functions
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f_{9}:\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f_{10}:\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}

and an algorithm that, given

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t,l,g\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}n
-vertex graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{G}
,

•

an apex-tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{G}
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}l
,

•

a regular flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{G}\setminus V({\bf a})
of height at least
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@individuated]{f_{\ref*{@individuated}}}(t,l,g)
whose compass has treewidth at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf tw}
,
and

•

a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R\subseteq V(G)
,

outputs, in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{t,l,g,{\sf tw}}(n)
, a
flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus V({\bf a})

that is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-tilt of some subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
of height at least
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[fun_inte]{f_{\ref*{fun_inte}}}(t,l)+g

such that

•

for every flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}^{\prime\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime\prime})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus V({\bf a})

that is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime\prime}
