-tilt of some subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
of height at least
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[fun_inte]{f_{\ref*{fun_inte}}}(t,l)+g

and for every flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}^{\prime\prime\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime\prime\prime})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus V({\bf a})

that is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime\prime\prime}
-tilt of some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[fun_inte]{f_{\ref*{fun_inte}}}(t,l)
-internal subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime\prime\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}g
,
for every annotated graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(F,R^{\star})
,
every outer-compatibility function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textxi
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,{R})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(F,R^{\star})
,
every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau{+}{\sf DP}]
of quantifier rank at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t
,
and every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y\subseteq V({\sf Compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime\prime\prime}))
, it holds that

((F,R^{\star})\oplus_{\textxi}(G,R),{\bf a})\models\textsigma_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\iff((F,R^{\star})\oplus_{\textxi}(G\setminus Y,R\setminus V({\sf Compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime\prime}))),{\bf a})\models\textsigma_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

Modifying the definition of characteristics.

To use
Subsection 13.2
to prove
Claim 2
for formulas in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Theta^{\sf DP}
(i.e., to show that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,R,{\bf a}^{\prime})[C]\models\textsigma_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\iff(G,R\setminus Y,{\bf a}^{\prime})[C^{\prime}]\models\textsigma_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}
), we also have to slightly modify the definition of
the characteristic in the end of
Subsection 9.4
and the algorithm
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\tt Find\_Equiv\_FlatPairs}
, as we proceed to describe.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q=({\bf tw}(\texttheta)+1)^{2}+1
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
.
We set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j={\sf odd}(\max\{q/2,j^{\prime}\})
.
We set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f(x,y,z,0)=z
and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\geq 1
we set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f(x,y,z,q)=\hyperref@@ii[@individuated]{f_{\ref*{@individuated}}}(x,y,f(x,y,z,i-1))
.
Then, we set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w=f(t,l,g,q)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r=\hyperref@@ii[@individuated]{f_{\ref*{@individuated}}}(t,l,g)
.
We set

{\sf CHAR}=[2,q]\times 2^{[l]}\times 2^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf SIG}_{\sf out}}}.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
be a graph, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l})
be an apex-tuple of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
be a flatness pair of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus V({\bf a})
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}2w+j
, and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K:={\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K^{\bf a}:=G[V({\bf a})\cup V(K)].

Also, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{{q}}
be the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q
-pseudogrid defined by the horizontal and vertical paths of the central
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q
-subwall of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W.

We call
enhanced extended compass
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
a tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{K},U_{1},\ldots,U_{q},R)
, where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{K}=(\mathfrak{A}[V(K^{\bf a})],{\bf a},{\bf W}_{{q}})

and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{U_{1},\ldots,U_{q},R\}\subseteq 2^{V(\mathfrak{K})}
.
We stress that in
Subsection 9.2
, in the definition of the extended compass of a flatness pair, we include to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{K}
the tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf I}
, while this is not anymore necessary in the definitions needed here.

Given a tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(i,L)\in[2,q]\times 2^{[l]}
and a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Z\subseteq U_{i}
,
we define the
out-signature
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{K},R,U_{i},L,Z)
,
denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf out}\text{-}{\sf sig}}(\mathfrak{K},R,U_{i},L,Z)
, as in
Subsection 9.3
, by replacing
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{A}^{(d,Z,L,F)}
by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{(U_{i},Z,L,F)}
, where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{(U_{i},Z,L,F)}
is the graph obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G[U_{i}\cup V_{L}({\bf a})]
after adding a set of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|V(F)|
vertices and all edges corresponding to the additional edges of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}F
and every edge between a vertex in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(F\setminus V_{L}({\bf a}))
and a vertex in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}U_{i}\setminus Z
.

Given an enhanced extended compass
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\mathfrak{K},U_{1},\ldots,U_{q},R)
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
,
we define its
characteristic
to be

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\texttheta\text{-}{\sf char}(\mathfrak{K},U_{1},\ldots,U_{q},R)

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle=

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\{(i,L,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf sig}_{\sf out}})\in{\sf CHAR}\mid\exists\ Z\subseteq U_{i}\mbox{\penalty\ such that}

(25)

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf out}\text{-}{\sf sig}}(\mathfrak{K},R,U_{i},L,Z)={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf sig}_{\sf out}}\}.

After defining the characteristic, we proceed to describe the modifications in the proof of
Subsection 8.1
.

Modifying the algorithm
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\tt Find\_Equiv\_FlatPairs}
.

The algorithm
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\tt Find\_Equiv\_FlatPairs}
presented in
Subsection 9.5
,
in Step 2 computes a collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{{\cal W}}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}2^{|{\sf CHAR}|}\cdot q
flatness pairs of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{G}\setminus V({\bf a})
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}2w+j.

We then add an extra step:
For each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}_{i},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{i})\in\tilde{{\cal W}}
,
we recursively apply the algorithm of
Subsection 13.2

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q
-many times,
where, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[2,q]
,
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W_{i-1}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{i}^{\prime})
is the output of the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(i-1)
-th recursive application,
the input of the algorithm in the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i
-th application is a tilt of an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f(k)
-internal subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{i-1}^{\prime\prime\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W_{i-1}^{\prime}
.
This way,
for each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[|\tilde{{\cal W}}|]
,
we compute flatness pairs

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W_{i,1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{R}_{i,1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(W_{i,q}^{\prime},\mathfrak{R}_{i,q}^{\prime})
and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W_{i,1}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathfrak{R}_{i,1}^{\prime\prime\prime}),\ldots,(W_{i,q}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathfrak{R}_{i,q}^{\prime\prime\prime})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus V({\bf a})

such that if we set

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W_{i,0},\mathfrak{R}_{i,0}):=(\tilde{W}_{i},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{i})

and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j\in[0,q]
, we set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}U_{i,j}^{\prime}:=V({\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i,j}^{\prime}}(W_{i,j}^{\prime}))
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}U_{i,j}^{\prime\prime\prime}:=V({\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i,j}^{\prime\prime\prime}}(W_{i,j}^{\prime\prime\prime}))
,
then

•

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j\in[q]
,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}U_{i,j}^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq U_{i,j}^{\prime}\subseteq U_{i,j-1}^{\prime\prime\prime}
, and

•

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j\in[q]
, every annotated graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(F,R^{\star})
,
every outer-compatibility function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textxi_{i,j}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,R)[U_{i,j-1}]
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(F,R^{\star})
,
every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau{+}{\sf DP}]
of quantifier rank at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t
,
and for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S\subseteq U_{i,q}^{\prime\prime\prime}
, if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G_{i,j-1},R_{i,j-1}):=(G,R)[U_{i,j-1}^{\prime}]
, then

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle((F,R^{\star})\oplus_{\textxi_{i,j}}(G_{i,j-1},R_{i,j-1}),{\bf a})\models\textsigma_{{\sf R},{\bf c}},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\iff

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle((F,R^{\star})\oplus_{\textxi_{i,j}}(G_{i,j-1}\setminus S,R_{i,j-1}\setminus U_{i,q}^{\prime}),{\bf a})\models\textsigma_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(i,j)\in[|\tilde{\cal W}|]\times[q]
, we set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V_{i,j}=U_{i,j}^{\prime}\setminus U_{i,j}^{\prime\prime\prime}
.
Observe that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V_{i,1},\ldots,V_{i,q}
are pairwise disjoint subsets of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V({\sf Compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{i}}(\tilde{W}_{i}))
.
Then, in Step 3, after
defining the enhanced extended compass
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{K}_{i},U_{i,1}^{\prime\prime\prime},\ldots,U_{i,q}^{\prime\prime\prime},R_{i}
for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[|\tilde{{\cal W}}_{i}|]
,
we compute
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\text{-}{\sf char}(\mathfrak{K}_{i},U_{i,1}^{\prime\prime\prime},\ldots,U_{i,q}^{\prime\prime\prime},R_{i})
using Courcelle’s theorem.
We output the set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y:=U_{1,q}^{\prime}
and the flatness pair

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W_{1,q}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathfrak{R}_{1,q}^{\prime\prime\prime}).

Proof of correctness of the modified
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\tt Find\_Equiv\_FlatPairs}
.

What remains is to show that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,R,{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\iff(G\setminus U_{1,q}^{\prime\prime\prime},R\setminus U_{1,q}^{\prime},{\bf a})\models\texttheta_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

We describe how to modify the proof of
Subsection 9.6
to work in our case.

We consider the simple case where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\theta=\textbeta\triangleright(\textsigma\wedge\textmu)^{({\sf c})}
,
for some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbeta\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\bf tw}[\{{\sf E},{\sf X}\}]
,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}\!+\!{\sf DP}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mu\in{\sf EM}
.
Suppose that
there is a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(G)
such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,X)\models\beta
and for every connected component
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X
,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G[C]\models\textmu
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,R,{\bf a})[C]\models\textsigma_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\breve{C}
be the privileged connected component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
with respect to a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q
-pseudowall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}^{(1)}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
.
Since
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
intersects at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q-1
internal bags of any
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}_{1},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{1})
-canonical partition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus V({\bf a}),

we know that there is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j_{0}\in[q]
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\cap V_{1,j_{0}}=\emptyset
.
We set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{\sf in}:=X\cap U_{1,j_{0}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{\sf out}:=X\setminus X_{\sf in}
.
Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Z=U_{1,j_{0}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\setminus\breve{C}
.

Also, recall that
for every annotated graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(F,R^{\star})
and
every outer-compatibility function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textxi_{1,j_{0}}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G_{1,j_{0}-1},R_{1,j_{0}-1})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(F,R^{\star})
,
it holds that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle((F,R^{\star})\oplus_{\textxi}(G_{1,j_{0}-1},R_{1,j_{0}-1}),{\bf a})\models\textsigma_{{\sf R},{\bf c}},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\iff

(26)

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle((F,R^{\star})\oplus_{\textxi_{1,j_{0}}}(G_{1,j_{0}-1}\setminus Z,R_{1,j_{0}-1}\setminus U_{1,q}^{\prime}),{\bf a})\models\textsigma_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

The fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\text{-}{\sf char}(\mathfrak{K}_{1},U_{1,1}^{\prime\prime\prime},\ldots,U_{1,q}^{\prime\prime\prime},R_{1})=\texttheta\text{-}{\sf char}(\mathfrak{K}_{2},U_{2,1}^{\prime\prime\prime},\ldots,U_{2,q}^{\prime\prime\prime},R_{2})
implies that there is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Z^{\prime}\subseteq U_{2,j_{0}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf out}\text{-}{\sf sig}}(\mathfrak{K}_{1},R_{1},U_{1,j_{0}}^{\prime\prime\prime},L,Z)={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sf out}\text{-}{\sf sig}}(\mathfrak{K}_{2},R_{2},U_{2,j_{0}}^{\prime\prime\prime},L,Z^{\prime})

and by
Claim 1
there is a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X^{\prime}
satisfying
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta^{\sf out}_{q}
.
Also,
for every annotated graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(F,R^{\star})
and
every outer-compatibility function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textxi_{2,j_{0}}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G_{2,j_{0}-1},R_{2,j_{0}-1})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(F,R^{\star})
,
it holds that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle((F,R^{\star})\oplus_{\textxi_{2,j_{0}}}(G_{2,j_{0}-1},R_{2,j_{0}-1}),{\bf a})\models\textsigma_{{\sf R},{\bf c}},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle\iff

(27)

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\displaystyle((F,R^{\star})\oplus_{\textxi_{2,j_{0}}}(G_{2,j_{0}-1}\setminus Z^{\prime},R_{2,j_{0}-1}\setminus U_{2,q}^{\prime}),{\bf a})\models\textsigma_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

We set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C^{\prime}
be the privileged connected component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}^{(1)}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{\rm out}\cup X^{\prime}
.
Note that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C\cup Z=C^{\prime}\cup Z^{\prime}
(as both these sets are equal to the privileged
connected component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf W}_{q}^{(1)}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X_{\rm out}
).

We also set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(F_{1},R_{F_{1}}):=(G,R)[C\setminus U_{1,j_{0}-1}^{\prime}]
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(F_{2},R_{F_{2}}):=(G,R)[C^{\prime}\setminus U_{2,j_{0}-1}^{\prime}]
.
Notice there is an outer-compatibility function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textxi_{1,j_{0}}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(F_{1},R_{F_{1}})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G_{1,j_{0}-1},R_{1,j_{0}-1})

such that

(F_{1},R_{F_{1}})\oplus_{\textxi_{1,j_{0}}}(G_{1,j_{0}-1},R_{1,j_{0}-1})=(G,R)[C\cup Z]

and there is an outer-compatibility function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textxi_{2,j_{0}}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(F_{2},R_{F_{2}})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G_{2,j_{0}-1},R_{2,j_{0}-1})

such that

(F_{2},R_{F_{2}})\oplus_{\textxi_{2,j_{0}}}(G_{2,j_{0}-1},R_{2,j_{0}-1})=(G,R)[C^{\prime}\cup Z^{\prime}].

Therefore, using the fact that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C\cup Z=C^{\prime}\cup Z^{\prime}
and (
26
) and (
27
) we derive that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,R,{\bf a})[C]\models\textsigma_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}\iff(G,R\setminus U_{1,q}^{\prime},{\bf a})[C^{\prime}]\models\textsigma_{{\sf R},{\bf c}}.

14
Limitations, extensions, and further directions

To conclude the article, in
Subsection 14.1
we justify the necessity of the ingredients of our logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
. Next, in
Subsection 14.2
, we discuss two additional extensions of our results. The one is based on the notion of
irrelevant-friendliness
and the other suggests an alternative meta-algorithmic trade off based on the
scattered disjoint-paths predicate
. Finally, in
Subsection 14.3

we present several directions and open problems for further research.

14.1
Natural limitations

We now wish to comment on why the three basic
ingredients of the definition of our logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
are necessary for the statement and the
proof of a meta-algorithmic result such as
Subsection 5.4
.

The first ingredient of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
is that the modulator sentences belong in

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mbox{\sf CMSOL}^{\sf tw}[\{{\sf E},{\sf X}\}]
which is defined so
that the treewidth of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf torso}(G,X)
is bounded. While it is known that bounding the treewidth is
necessary for
CMSOL
-model-checking
[
100
,
36
]
, one may ask why it is not enough to just bound the treewidth of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G[X].

To see why this unavoidable, consider a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}
be the graph obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
by subdividing
each edge once. Then, asking whether
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
is Hamiltonian, which is a well-known
NP
-complete problem
[
66
]
,
is equivalent to asking whether
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}
has a vertex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S^{\prime}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}[S^{\prime}]

is a cycle and such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}\setminus S^{\prime}
is an edgeless graph, that is, a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{2}
-minor-free graph. Notice that, while
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf tw}(G^{\prime}[S^{\prime}])=2,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf torso}(G^{\prime},S^{\prime})=G

has unbounded treewidth.

The second ingredient of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
is minor-exclusion, that is materialized by the conjunction with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu
in the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{0}.
Notice first that expressing whether a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
contains a clique on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
vertices can be done by a
FOL
-sentence, while the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
-
Clique
problem is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf W}[1]
-hard
[
38
]
. Therefore, the minor-exclusion condition cannot be dropped. Moreover, even if we consider a
fixed
target
FOL
-sentence, it was proved in
[
55
]
that there exists a
FOL
-sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
such that
checking whether a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
has a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S\subseteq V(G)
with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|S|=k

such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus S\models\textsigma
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf W}[1]
-hard problem, when parameterized by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k.
This implies that, even for this restricted problem where the
FOL
-sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
is fixed, an algorithm in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f(k)\cdot n^{{\cal O}(1)}
cannot be expected.

The third ingredient of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
is the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}
demand, that is materialized by the conjunction with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
in the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{0}.
This is also necessary,
as otherwise we may choose some property
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
not definable in
FOL
, such as
Hamiltonicity, which is
CMSOL
-definable and
NP
-complete
on
planar graphs
[
66
]
. Without the restriction that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
needs to be
FOL
-definable, a void modulator and a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu
expressing planarity would be able to model this
NP
-complete problem. Nevertheless, we may consider extensions of
FOL
in the target sentence, as it is done in
Section 13
and as suggested in the next subsection.

14.2
Extensions

Irrelevant-friendliness.

In the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{0}
we include compound sentences
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\wedge\textmu

where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu
expresses minor-exclusion.
Our proof is modulated so to allow that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma

may express a wider set of sentences, not definable in
FOL
, which we proceed to discuss.
We call these sentences
irrelevant-friendly
, a technical concept whose formal definition is the following:

Definition 56

(Irrelevant-friendliness)
.

For a real number
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\alpha\geq 1,
we say that a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi\in{\rm\mbox{\sf CMSOL}}[\{E\}]
(evaluated on graphs) is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textalpha
-irrelevant-friendly

if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\rm Mod}(\textphi)
is hereditary
10
10

10

A graph class
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
is
hereditary
if every induced subgraph of a graph in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
belongs in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}.
and there
exists a function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f_{11}:\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}(n^{\textalpha})
with the following specifications:

Input:
two integers
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k,a\in\mathbb{N},
an odd integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3},
a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A\subseteq V(G)
with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|A|\leq a,
and
a flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}({W},{\mathfrak{R}})

of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus A
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q\geq\hyperref@@ii[@reintroduced]{f_{\ref*{@reintroduced}}}(k,a,\ell).

Output:
A subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
such that if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K
is the compass of some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-tilt of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\subseteq V(G),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf bid}(X\setminus{A},W,\mathfrak{R})\leq k,
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus X\models\textphi\Rightarrow G\setminus(X\setminus V(K))\models\textphi.

(The definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf bid}(X\setminus{A},W,\mathfrak{R})
can be found in
Subsection 7.2
.) The above property is the abstraction of
what is required for a sentence in order to make our proof applicable. In fact, minor-exclusion is already an irrelevant-friendly property and
an important part of the proof is based on this fact.

For each irrelevant-friendly sentence

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\{{\sf E}\}],
we define an extension
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta^{\textphi}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
by considering, as the base
case, instead of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{0},
the logic

\textTheta_{0}^{\textphi}=\{\textsigma\wedge\textmu\wedge\textphi|_{\sf gf}\mid\textsigma\in{\sf FOL}[\texttau]\mbox{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\textmu\in{\sf EM}[\texttau]\},

that is, we add
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi
in the conjunction defining
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{0}.

Theorem 57
.

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\alpha\geq 1
is a real number,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttau
is some vocabulary,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[\{{\sf E}\}]
is some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textalpha
-irrelevant-friendly sentence on graphs, and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\texttheta\in{\textTheta}^{\textphi}[\texttau],
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\rm Mod}(\texttheta)
can be decided in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}(n^{\textalpha+1}).

The proof of
Subsection 14.2

is a direct consequence of the algorithm of
Subsection 5.4

and
Subsection 14.2
.
Indeed, the algorithm first finds an apex set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A
and a flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus A
as in the second step of proof of the algorithm of
Subsection 5.4
as presented in
Subsection 8.2
.
Before proceeding
to the third step,
the algorithm uses the algorithm in
Subsection 14.2
in order to detect
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime},
and the compass
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K^{\prime}
of some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-tilt
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W.
According to the specifications of the algorithm in
Subsection 14.2
, the whole vertex set of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K^{\prime}
is already irrelevant with respect to the sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi
and, because
of the hypothesis that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\rm Mod}(\textphi)
is hereditary, we can now
proceed to the third step,
apply the algorithm of
Subsection 8.1

inside the compass
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K^{\prime},
and detect there the irrelevant vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
as well as the vertices to be removed from the annotated set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}R.

The consequences of
Subsection 14.2

are open to investigate, as it may permit to handle target sentences that are not necessarily
FOL
-definable.
As an example, we mention the result
of Fiorini, Hardy, Reed, and Vetta in
[
51
]
who designed an algorithm of time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{k}(n)
that checks whether
a graph can be made planar and bipartite by removing
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
vertices. Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi\in\mbox{\sf CMSOL}[{\sf E}]
be the property of being bipartite. For planar graphs, using a simple parity argument, one can show that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi
is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}1
-irrelevant-friendly.
Based on this and
Subsection 14.2
,
one may demand
any

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf FOL}
-property, apart from planarity and bipartiteness (which is not
FOL
-definable)
and solve the corresponding problem in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{k}(n^{2})
by
Subsection 14.2
. This easy corollary
of
Subsection 14.2
can be further extended, as bipartiteness is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}1
-irrelevant-friendly for other minor-closed graph classes as well. We prefer not to enter into details here, as we mention this problem only as an example of the potential of
Subsection 14.2
. For other examples of irrelevant-friendly properties, one may consider, for instance, the exclusion of odd-minors
[
85
]
or of topological minors
[
59
]
.

From
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\textTheta}^{\sf DP}
to its “scattered” extension
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\textTheta}^{\sf SDP}
.

In
Section 13
we defined the logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta^{\sf DP}
(and its counterpart
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\textTheta}^{\sf DP}
)
by using as target logic
FOL​+DP
, that is
FOL
enhanced by disjoint-path predicates

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf dp}_{k}(\cdot)
where

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf dp}_{k}({\sf x}_{1},{\sf y}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{k},{\sf y}_{k})
evaluates true in a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
if and only if there pairwise disjoint paths
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
of length at least two between (the interpretations of)
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf x}_{i}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf y}_{i}
for all
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[k].
In the same section we proved extensions of
Subsection 1.2
and
Subsection 1.2
for these more general logics by using the recent results of
[
73
]
. In
[
73
]
, a generalization of
FOL​+DP
was defined, namely
FOL​+SDP
, by considering (for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}s\in\mathbb{N}
) the predicate

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}s
-dp
{}_{k}({\sf x}_{1},{\sf y}_{1},\ldots,{\sf x}_{k},{\sf y}_{k})
, where we now demand that the disjoint paths in question are pairwise
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}s
-scattered, i.e., there are no two vertices of two distinct
paths that are within distance at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}s
.
It is proved in
[
73
,
74
]
that model-checking for sentences in
FOL​+SDP
can be done in quadratic time for graphs of bounded Euler genus.
Similarly to the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\textTheta}^{\sf DP},

we may define the logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\textTheta}^{\sf SDP}
by using
FOL​+SDP
as the target logic. By using the same arguments as in
Section 13
for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\textTheta}^{\sf SDP},

it is possible to prove an analogue of
Subsection 13.1

where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\textTheta}^{\sf DP}
is replaced by the more expressive
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\textTheta}^{\sf SDP}
, while
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf hw}(G)
is replaced by the more restrictive
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf eg}(G)
, that is the Euler genus of the graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
. The only essential change in the proof is that now, instead of
Subsection 13.2
, we apply its “
FOL​+SDP
-counterpart” given by
[
74
, Lemma 12]
.

14.3
Further research

The minor-exclusion framework.

The graph-structural horizon in both
Subsection 5.4
and
Subsection 5.4
is delimited by minor-exclusion.
In the case of
Subsection 5.4
, this restriction is applied to the target
property defined by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textmu
in the logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta,
while in
Subsection 5.4
this is the promise combinatorial restriction that yields efficient model-checking for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\textTheta}.
This restriction is
hard-wired in our proof in the way it combines the Flat Wall theorem
with Gaifman’s theorem. Recently, several efficient algorithms appeared
for modification problems targeting or assuming topological minor-freeness (see
[
124
,
59
,
86
,
4
]
and the meta-algorithmic result in
[
132
]
). For such classes, to achieve efficient model-checking
for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta,
or some fragment of it, is
an interesting open challenge.

Quadratic time.

The proof of
Subsection 5.4
can be seen as a possible “meta-algorithmization” of the

irrelevant vertex technique
introduced by Robertson and Seymour
[
125
]
, going further than the two known recent attempts in this direction
[
73
,
53
]
.
The main routine of the algorithm
transforms the input of the problem
to a simpler graph by detecting territories in it that can be safely discarded, therefore producing a simpler instance. This routine is applied repetitively
until the graph has “small” treewidth, so that the problem can
be solved in linear time by using Courcelle’s theorem. This approach
gives an algorithm running in quadratic time. Any improvement of this running time
should rely on techniques escaping the above scheme of gradual simplification. The only results in this direction are the cases of making a graph planar by deleting at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k
vertices (resp. edges) in
[
87
]
(resp.
[
92
]
) that run in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}_{k}(n).

Other modification operations.

In
Section 3
we gave a wide variety of problems that can be modeled by the logic
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta,
either directly or indirectly, via reductions. All these modifications concern problems involving edge or vertex removals. Is it possible to extend
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
so that it can also deal with other (local) operations such as edge contractions, edge additions, or others? This was done in
[
53
]

for the case of removing vertices to achieve planarity and a
FOL
-definable property. Moreover, especially for
the part of
Subsection 3.2
concerning the grammar
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal M},

it is possible to make the following enhancement in order to include the contraction operation:
include the
production rule
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf M}\rightarrow({\sf s}{{\sf M})}
and add in the definition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{M}
that if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\sf w}=({\sf s}{\sf w}^{\prime}),
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}_{\tilde{\sf w}}=\{G\mid\exists e\in V(G),G\,/\penalty\,e\in{\cal G}_{\tilde{\sf w}^{\prime}}\}.
11
11

11

We use
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\,/\penalty\,e
for the result of contracting the edge
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}e
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G.
By using the more general result in
Subsection 13.1
, the proof of the second part of
Subsection 3.2
can be easily adapted, using the expressive potential of the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf dp}_{k}
predicates, so to work with this enhanced version of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal M}
by using one extra annotation set in order to mark the edges under contraction as “specially colored” vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}.
We believe that using analogous enhancements it is possible to deal with other type of (not necessarily local) modification operations (see
[
54
,
73
]
for some previous steps in this direction).

Further than connectivity closure.

One of the key operations defining
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta

is the connectivity extension operation, that is, given a sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi,
to consider the (conjunctive) sentence
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi^{({\sf c})}.
We incorporated this operation to our logic in order to express elimination distance modifications (such as those of tree-depth
[
25
]
and bridge-depth
[
21
]
) where, at each step, we remove some tree-like structure and then we apply the
current target sentence
to the connected components of the remaining graph.
In
[
44
]
, the notion of
block elimination distance
has been
introduced, where the target property is applied to the biconnected components of the remaining graph (instead of the connected components). We are confident that our results can be adapted so to include the
biconnectivity extension – or even the 3-conectivity extension, as defined by Tutte’s decomposition
[
136
]
. However, we prefer to avoid this here as it would add undesirable burden
to the statement
of our results (and to the proofs as well).
Another direction is to consider different versions of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi^{({\sf c})}.
One of them might be a
disjunctive
version, namely
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi^{\vee({\sf c})},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\models\textphi^{\vee({\sf c})}
if

at least one
of the connected components of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G

is a model of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi.
Another one is a
selective
version, namely
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi^{\exists({\sf c})},
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\models\textphi^{\wedge({\sf c})}
if there is some subset of the connected components of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
whose union
is a model of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi.
Our proof fails if we wish to incorporate any of these two variants of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi^{({\sf c})}
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta.
However, it can be easily adapted so to incorporate
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textphi^{\vee({\sf c})}
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\textTheta}.

Descriptive complexity and the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
-hierarchy.

Recall that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\textTheta_{i},

where each level of the sentence set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{i}
is defined by adding an extra modulator sentence, followed by some positive
Boolean combination of the connectivity closure
of the lower level. We extended our result from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{1}
to every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{i}

because
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
is quite versatile and makes it easier to express more complex hierarchical modification problems, as we did in
Subsection 3.2
.
However, it is an open problem whether this hierarchy is proper with respect to the descriptive complexity of the problems that it defines in each of its levels.
In simple cases where the modulator sentence asks for a set of bounded size,
and under the absence of positive Boolean combinations, it is
possible to express any
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
-definable problem using
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{1}.

For instance, elimination ordering to some

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{0}
-definable class can be straightforwardly expressed in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta,
however with a more technical proof one can also express it in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta_{1}
(see
[
56
]
).
Is this collapse maintained
when we consider the full expressive power of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
? We conjecture a negative answer to this question
for both
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta
and its extension
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textTheta^{\sf DP}
.

Constructibility further than bounding treewidth.

Subsection 12.2
extends the constructibility horizon of Robertson-Seymour’s theorem
[
126
]
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal B}\mbox{\footnotesize$\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\,\blacktriangleright\,$}{\cal G}

when both
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal B}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal G}
are minor-closed and, moreover,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf obs}({\cal B})
contains some planar graph (see
Subsection 12.2
). Does this constructibility result still hold when we drop this latter planarity restriction? We are not in position to conjecture positively or negatively on this.

Acknowledgements.
We wish to thank
Stavros G. Kolliopoulos
and

Christophe Paul
for their valuable remarks on earlier versions of this paper.

Appendix A
Flat walls framework

Here we present the framework on flat walls that was introduced in
[
129
]
.
In
Subsection A.1
we give some additional basic definitions and in
Subsection A.2
we define walls, subwalls, and other notions related to walls.
Next, in
Subsection A.3
, we give the definitions of renditions and paintings, that are used in
Subsection A.4
to define flatness pairs.
In
Subsection A.4
, apart from the definition of flatness pairs, we present notions like
influence
,
regularity
, and
tilts
.
Then,
in
Subsection A.5
, we state
Subsection A.5
that is a critical
ingredient of our algorithm of
Subsection 5.4
in
Subsection 8.2
.
In
Subsection A.6
we present the definition of
homogeneous flatness pairs
and state
Subsection A.6
, that is also important for the proof of
Subsection 8.1
.
Finally, in
Subsection A.7
, we give the definition of a canonical partition of a wall that allow us to define the bidimensionality of a set with respect to a flatness pair in
Subsection 7.2
.

A.1
Basic definitions

Given a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
we define the
detail
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf detail}(G),
to be the maximum among
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|E(G)|
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|V(G)|.

Given a finite collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal F}
of graphs, we set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell_{\cal F}=\max\{{\sf detail}(H)\mid H\in{\cal F}\}.

Dissolutions and subdivisions.

Given a vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in V(G)
of degree two with neighbors
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}u
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w,
we define the
dissolution
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v

to be the operation of deleting
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v
and, if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}u
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
are not adjacent, adding the edge
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{u,w\}.

Given two graphs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H,G,
we say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H
is a
dissolution
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G

if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H
can be obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
after dissolving vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G.

Given an edge
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}e=\{u,v\}\in E(G),
we define the
subdivision
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}e

to be the operation of deleting
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}e,
adding a new vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}w
and making it adjacent to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}u
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v.

Given two graphs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H,G,
we say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H
is a
subdivision
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G

if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H
can be obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
after subdividing edges of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G.

Contractions and minors.

A graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}
is a
contraction
of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,

if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G^{\prime}
can be obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
by a sequence of edge contractions.
Given two graphs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H,G,
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}H
is a minor of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
then for every vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in V(H)
there is
a set of vertices in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
that are the endpoints of the edges of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
contracted towards creating
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v.

We call this set
model
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G.

A.2
Walls and subwalls

Walls.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}k,r\in\mathbb{N}.
The

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(k\times r)
-grid
is the
graph whose vertex set is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}[k]\times[r]
and two vertices
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(i,j)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
are adjacent if and only if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|i-i^{\prime}|+|j-j^{\prime}|=1.

An
elementary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall
, for some odd integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 3,
is the graph obtained from a

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(2r\times r)
-grid
with vertices
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(x,y)\in[2r]\times[r],

after the removal of the
“vertical” edges
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{(x,y),(x,y+1)\}
for odd
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}x+y,
and then the removal of
all vertices of degree one.
Notice that, as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 3,
an elementary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall is a planar graph
that has a unique (up to topological isomorphism) embedding in the plane
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathbb{R}^{2}

such that all its finite faces are incident to exactly six
edges.
The
perimeter
of an elementary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall is the cycle bounding its infinite face,
while the cycles bounding its finite faces are called
bricks
.
Also, the vertices
in the perimeter of an elementary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall that have degree two are called
pegs
,
while the vertices
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(1,1),(2,r),(2r-1,1),(2r,r)
are called
corners
(notice that the corners are also pegs).

An
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall
is any graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
obtained from an elementary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{W}

after subdividing edges. A graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
is a
wall
if it is an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall for some odd
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 3

and we refer to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
as the
height
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W.
Given a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,

a
wall of

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
is a subgraph of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
that is a wall.
We insist that, for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall, the number
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
is always odd.

We call the vertices of degree three of a wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W

3-branch vertices
.
A cycle of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
is a
brick
(resp. the
perimeter
) of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W

if its 3-branch vertices are the vertices of a brick (resp. the perimeter) of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{W}.

We denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal C}(W)
the set of all cycles of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W.

We use
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}D(W)
in order to denote the perimeter of the wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W.

A brick of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
is
internal
if it is disjoint from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}D(W).

Subwalls.

Given an elementary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{W},
some odd
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in\{1,3,\ldots,2r-1\},
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i^{\prime}=(i+1)/2,

the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i^{\prime}
-th vertical path
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{W}
is the one whose
vertices, in order of appearance, are
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(i,1),(i,2),(i+1,2),(i+1,3),(i,3),(i,4),(i+1,4),(i+1,5),(i,5),\ldots,(i,r-2),(i,r-1),(i+1,r-1),(i+1,r).

Also, given some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j\in[2,r-1]
the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}j
-th horizontal path
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{W}

is the one whose
vertices, in order of appearance, are
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(1,j),(2,j),\ldots,(2r,j).

A
vertical
(resp.
horizontal
) path of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
is one
that is a subdivision of a vertical (resp. horizontal) path of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{W}.

Notice that the perimeter of an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W

is uniquely defined regardless of the choice of the elementary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{W}.

A
subwall
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
is any subgraph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W

that is an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r^{\prime}
-wall, with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r^{\prime}\leq r,
and such the vertical (resp. horizontal) paths of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
are subpaths of the
vertical (resp. horizontal) paths of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W.

Layers.

The
layers
of an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
are recursively defined as follows.
The first layer of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
is its perimeter.
For
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i=2,\ldots,(r-1)/2,
the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i
-th layer of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
is the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(i-1)
-th layer of the subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}

obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
after removing from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
its perimeter and
removing recursively all occurring vertices of degree one.
We refer to the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(r-1)/2
-th layer as the
inner layer
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W.

The
central vertices
of an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
are its two branch vertices that do not belong to any of its layers
and are connected by a path of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
that does not intersect any layers of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
.

Central walls.

Given an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
and an odd
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3}
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q\leq r,

we define the
central
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q
-subwall
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W,
denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{(q)},

to be the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q
-wall obtained from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
after removing
its first
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(r-q)/2
layers and all occurring vertices of degree one.

Tilts.

The
interior
of a wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
is the graph obtained
from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
if we remove from it all edges of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}D(W)
and all vertices
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}D(W)
that have degree two in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W.

Given two walls
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{W}
of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,

we say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{W}
is a
tilt
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{W}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
have identical interiors.

A.3
Paintings and renditions

In this subsection we present the notions of renditions and paintings, originating in the work of Robertson and Seymour
[
125
]
.
The definitions presented here were introduced by Kawarabayashi, Thomas, and Wollan
[
93
]
(see also
[
129
]
).

Paintings.

A
closed
(resp.
open
)
disk
is a set homeomorphic to the set

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}
(resp.
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid x^{2}+y^{2}<1\}
).
Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Delta
be a closed disk.
Given a subset
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Delta,
we
denote its closure by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{X}
and its boundary by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf bd}(X).

A
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Delta
-painting
is a pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textGamma=(U,N)

where

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}N
is a finite set of points of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Delta,

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}N\subseteq U\subseteq\Delta,
and

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}U\setminus N
has finitely many arcwise-connected components, called
cells
, where, for every cell
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\circ

the closure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{c}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c

is a closed disk
and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\circ

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|\tilde{c}|\leq 3,
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{c}:={\sf bd}(c)\cap N.

We use the notation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}U(\textGamma):=U,

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}N(\textGamma):=N
and denote the set of cells of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textGamma

by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C(\textGamma).

For convenience, we may assume that each cell of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textGamma
is an open disk of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Delta.

Notice that, given a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Delta
-painting
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textGamma,

the pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(N(\textGamma),\{\tilde{c}\mid c\in C(\textGamma)\})
is a hypergraph whose hyperedges have cardinality at most three and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textGamma
can be seen as a plane embedding of this hypergraph in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Delta.

Renditions.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
be a graph and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Omega
be a cyclic permutation of a subset of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(G)
that we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\Omega).
By an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Omega
-rendition
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
we mean a triple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\textGamma,\textsigma,\textpi),
where

(a)

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textGamma
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Delta
-painting for some closed disk
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Delta,

(b)

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpi:N(\textGamma)\to V(G)
is an injection, and

(c)

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma
assigns to each cell
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c\in C(\textGamma)
a subgraph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma(c)
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
such that

(1)

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G=\bigcup_{c\in C(\textGamma)}\textsigma(c),

(2)

for distinct
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c,c^{\prime}\in C(\textGamma),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma(c)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma(c^{\prime})
are edge-disjoint,

(3)

for every cell
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c\in C(\textGamma),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpi(\tilde{c})\subseteq V(\textsigma(c)),

(4)

for every cell
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c\in C(\textGamma),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(\textsigma(c))\cap\bigcup_{c^{\prime}\in C(\textGamma)\setminus\{c\}}V(\textsigma(c^{\prime}))\subseteq\textpi(\tilde{c}),
and

(5)

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpi(N(\textGamma)\cap{\sf bd}(\Delta))=V(\Omega),
such that the points
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}N(\textGamma)\cap{\sf bd}(\Delta)
appear in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf bd}(\Delta)
in the same ordering
as their images, via
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpi,
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Omega.

A.4
Flatness pairs

In this subsection we define the notion of a flat wall, originating in the work of Robertson and Seymour
[
125
]
and later used in
[
93
]
.
Here, we define flat walls as in
[
129
]
.

Flat walls.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
be a graph and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
be an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
for some odd integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 3.

We say that a pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(P,C)\subseteq D(W)\times D(W)
is a
choice
of pegs and corners for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
is the subdivision of an elementary
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{W}

where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P
and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
are the pegs and the corners of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{W},
respectively (clearly,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C\subseteq P
).
To get more intuition, notice that a wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
can occur in several ways from the elementary wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{W},

depending on the way the vertices in the perimeter of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\bar{W}
are subdivided.
Each of them gives a different selection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(P,C)
of pegs and corners of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W.

We say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
is a
flat
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall

of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
if there is a separation
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(X,Y)
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
and a choice
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(P,C)

of pegs and corners for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
such that:

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(W)\subseteq Y,

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P\subseteq X\cap Y\subseteq V(D(W)),
and

•

if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Omega
is the cyclic ordering of the vertices
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X\cap Y
as they appear in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}D(W),

then there exists an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Omega
-rendition
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\textGamma,\textsigma,\textpi)
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G[Y].

We say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
is a
flat wall

of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
if it is a flat
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r
-wall for some odd integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 3.

Flatness pairs.

Given the above, we say that the choice of the 7-tuple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}=(X,Y,P,C,\textGamma,\textsigma,\textpi)

certifies that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
is a flat wall of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
.
We call the pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
a
flatness pair
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
and define
the
height
of the pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
to be the height of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W.

We use the term
cell of

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
in order to refer to the cells of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textGamma.

We call the graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G[Y]
the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
-compass
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,

denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W).

It is easy to see that there is a connected component of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
that contains the wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
as a subgraph.
We can assume that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(W)
is connected, updating
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\mathfrak{R}}
by removing from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y
the vertices of all the connected components of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)

except of the one that contains
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
and including them in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}X
(
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textGamma,\textsigma,\textpi
can also be easily modified according to the removal of the aforementioned vertices from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}Y
).
We define the
flaps
of the wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
as

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf flaps}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W):=\{\textsigma(c)\mid c\in C(\textGamma)\}.

Given a flap
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}F\in{\sf flaps}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W),
we define its
base

as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\partial F:=V(F)\cap\textpi(N(\textGamma)).

A cell
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{{\mathfrak{R}}}
is
untidy
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpi(\tilde{c})
contains a vertex

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}x
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{W}
such that two of the edges of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{W}
that are incident to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}x
are edges of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma(c).
Notice that if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
is untidy then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|\tilde{c}|=3.

A cell
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\mathfrak{R}}
is
tidy
if it is not untidy.
The notion of tidy/untidy cell as well as the notions that we present in the rest of this subsection have been introduced in
[
129
]
.

Cell classification.

Given a cycle
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W),
we say that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
-normal
if it is
not
a subgraph of a flap
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}F\in{\sf flaps}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W).

Given an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
-normal cycle
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W),

we call a cell
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
-perimetric
if

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma(c)
contains some edge of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C.

Since every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
-perimetric cell
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
contains some edge of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|\partial\textsigma(c)|\leq 3,
we observe the following.

Observation 58
.

For every pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(C,C^{\prime})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\mathfrak{R}}
-normal cycles of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(W)
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(C)\cap V(C^{\prime})=\emptyset,
there is no cell of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\mathfrak{R}}
that is both
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
-perimetric and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C^{\prime}
-perimetric.

Notice that if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
-perimetric, then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpi(\tilde{c})
contains two points
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}p,q\in N(\textGamma)

such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpi(p)
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpi(q)
are vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
where one,
say
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{c}^{\rm in},
of the two
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\textpi(p),\textpi(q))
-subpaths of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
is a subgraph of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma(c)
and the other,
denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{c}^{\rm out},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\textpi(p),\textpi(q))
-subpath contains at most one internal vertex of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma(c),

which should be the (unique) vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}z
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\partial\textsigma(c)\setminus\{\textpi(p),\textpi(q)\}.

We pick a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(p,q)
-arc
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A_{c}
in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hat{c}:={c}\cup\tilde{c}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textpi^{-1}(z)\in A_{c}
if and only if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}P_{c}^{\rm in}
contains
the vertex
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}z
as an internal vertex.

We consider the circle
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{C}=\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\{A_{c}\mid\mbox{$\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c$ is a $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C$-perimetric cell of $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}$}\}

and we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Delta_{C}
the closed disk bounded by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{C}
that is contained in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Delta.

A cell
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
is called
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
-internal
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c\subseteq\Delta_{C}

and is called
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
-external
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Delta_{C}\cap c=\emptyset.

Notice that the cells of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
are partitioned into
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
-internal,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
-perimetric, and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
-external cells.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
be a tidy
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
-perimetric cell of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|\tilde{c}|=3.
Notice that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c\setminus A_{c}
has two arcwise-connected components and one of them is an open disk
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}D_{c}
that is a subset of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Delta_{C}.

If the closure
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\overline{D}_{c}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}D_{c}
contains only two points of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{c}
then we call the cell
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
-marginal
.
See
Figure 25
for a figure illustrating the above notions.
We refer the reader to
[
129
]
for more figures.

Figure 25
:
This picture is taken from
[
129
]
.
It depicts a flat wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
in a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
the painting of a rendition
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
certifying its flatness, a subwall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W,
of height three, which is

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
-normal, and the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
-flaps of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W,
that correspond to either
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-perimetric (depicted in grey) or
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-internal cells (depicted in green).
The circle
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{W^{\prime}}
is the fat orange cycle. The
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-marginal cells are depicted in light grey and the untidy cells are those with dashed boundary.

Influence.

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
-normal cycle
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
we define the set

{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}}(C)=\{\textsigma(c)\mid\mbox{$\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c$ is a cell of $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}$ that is not $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C$-external}\}.

A wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
-normal
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}D(W^{\prime})
is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
-normal.
Notice that every wall of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
(and hence every subwall of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
) is an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
-normal wall of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W).
We denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal S}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
the set of all
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
-normal walls of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W).
Given a wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}\in{\cal S}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
and a cell
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R},

we say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-perimetric/internal/external/marginal
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}D(W^{\prime})
-perimetric/internal/external/marginal, respectively.
We also use
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{W^{\prime}},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Delta_{W^{\prime}},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W^{\prime})
as shortcuts
for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{D(W^{\prime})},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Delta_{D(W^{\prime})},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}}(D(W^{\prime})),
respectively.

Regular flatness pairs.

We call a flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G

regular

if none of its cells is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
-external,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
-marginal, or untidy.

Tilts of flatness pairs.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
be two flatness pairs of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G

and let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}\in{\cal S}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W).

We assume that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\mathfrak{R}}=(X,Y,P,C,\textGamma,\textsigma,\textpi)

and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},P^{\prime},C^{\prime},\textGamma^{\prime},\textsigma^{\prime},\textpi^{\prime}).

We say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-tilt

of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
if

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}
does not have
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{W}^{\prime}
-external cells,

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{W}^{\prime}
is a tilt of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime},

•

the set of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{W}^{\prime}
-internal cells of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}
is the same as the set of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-internal
cells of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\mathfrak{R}}
and their images via
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textsigma^{\prime}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\textsigma}
are also the same,

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime})
is a subgraph of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}{\sf influence}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(W^{\prime}),
and

•

if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}c
is a cell in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C(\textGamma^{\prime})\setminus C(\textGamma),
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|\tilde{c}|\leq 2.

The next observation follows from the third item above and the fact that the cells corresponding to flaps
containing a central vertex of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
are all internal (recall that the height of a wall is always at least three).

Observation 59
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,{\mathfrak{R}})
be a flatness pair of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}\in{\cal S}_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(W).

For every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-tilt
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{{\mathfrak{R}}}^{\prime})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,{\mathfrak{R}}),
the central vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
belong to the vertex set of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\tilde{{\mathfrak{R}}}^{\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime}).

Also, given a regular flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,{\mathfrak{R}})
of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
and a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}\in{\cal S}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W),

for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-tilt
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{{\mathfrak{R}}}^{\prime})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R}),
by definition, none of its cells is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{W}^{\prime}
-external,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{W}^{\prime}
-marginal, or untidy – thus,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{{\mathfrak{R}}}^{\prime})
is regular.
Therefore, regularity of a flatness pair is a property that its tilts “inherit”.

Observation 60
.

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
is a regular flatness pair of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
then for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}\in{\cal S}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W),
every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-tilt of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
is also regular.

We next present one of the two main results of
[
129
]
(see
[
129
, Theorem 5]
).

Proposition 61
.

There exists an algorithm that given a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
a flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}({W},{\mathfrak{R}})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
and a wall
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}\in{\cal S}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W),
outputs a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W^{\prime}
-tilt of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}({W},{\mathfrak{R}})
in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal O}(n+m).

We conclude this subsection with the Flat Wall theorem and, in particular, the version proved by Chuzhoy
[
30
]
, restated in our framework (see
[
129
, Proposition 7]
).

Proposition 62
.

There exist two functions
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f_{12}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f_{13}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N},
where the images of
f

A.4

are odd numbers, such that if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3}
is an odd integer,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
is a graph that does not contain
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{t}
as a minor, and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
is an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@donnescamente]{f_{\ref*{@donnescamente}}}(t)\cdot r
-wall of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,

then there is a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A\subseteq V(G)
with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|A|\leq\hyperref@@ii[@carlovingios]{f_{\ref*{@carlovingios}}}(t)

and a flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus A
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r.

Moreover,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@donnescamente]{f_{\ref*{@donnescamente}}}(t)={\cal O}(t^{2})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@carlovingios]{f_{\ref*{@carlovingios}}}(t)=t-5.

A.5
Flat walls with compasses of bounded treewidth

The following result was proved in
[
129
, Theorem 8]
.
It is a version of the Flat Wall theorem, originally proved in
[
125
]
.
The proof in
[
129
, Theorem 8]
is strongly based on
the proof of an improved version of the Flat Wall theorem given by of Kawarabayashi, Thomas, and Wollan
[
93
]
(see also
[
30
,
70
]
).

Proposition 63
.

There is a function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f_{14}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
and
an algorithm that receives as input a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,
an odd integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r\geq 3,
and a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},
and outputs, in time
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}2^{{\cal O}_{t}(r^{2})}\cdot n,
one of the following:

•

a report that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}K_{t}
is a minor of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G,

•

a tree decomposition of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
of width at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@interference]{f_{\ref*{@interference}}}(t)\cdot r,
or

•

a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A\subseteq V(G),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|A|\leq\hyperref@@ii[@carlovingios]{f_{\ref*{@carlovingios}}}(t),
a regular flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus A
of height
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}r,

and a tree decomposition of the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\mathfrak{R}
-compass of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}W
of width at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@interference]{f_{\ref*{@interference}}}(t)\cdot r.
(Here
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@carlovingios]{f_{\ref*{@carlovingios}}}(t)
is the function of
Subsection A.4
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[@interference]{f_{\ref*{@interference}}}(t)=2^{{\cal O}(t^{2}\log t)}.
)

A.6
Homogeneous walls

We first present some definitions on boundaried graphs and folios that will be used to define the notion of homogeneous walls.
Following this, we present some results concerning homogeneous walls that are key ingredients in our proofs.

Boundaried graphs.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t\in\mathbb{N}.

A
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t
-boundaried graph
is a triple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{G}=(G,B,\textrho)
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
is a graph,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}B\subseteq V(G),

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|B|=t,
and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho:B\to[t]
is a bijection.
We call
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}B
the
boundary
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf G}
and the vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}B

the boundary vertices
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf G}.

For
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}B^{\prime}\subseteq B,
we define the bijection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho[B^{\prime}]:B^{\prime}\to[|B^{\prime}|]
such that for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}v\in B^{\prime},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho[B^{\prime}](v)=|\{u\in B^{\prime}\mid\textrho(u)\leq\textrho(v)\}|.

Also, for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}S\subseteq V(G),
we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf G}\setminus S
the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t
-boundaried graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G\setminus S,B\setminus S,\textrho[B\setminus S]).

We say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{G}_{1}=(G_{1},B_{1},\textrho_{1})
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{G}_{2}=(G_{2},B_{2},\textrho_{2})

are
isomorphic
if there is an isomorphism from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{1}
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G_{2}

that extends the bijection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho_{2}^{-1}\circ\textrho_{1}.

The triple
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(G,B,\textrho)
is a
boundaried graph
if it is a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t
-boundaried graph for some
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t\in\mathbb{N}.

As in
[
125
]
(see also
[
7
]
), we define the
detail
of a boundaried graph

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{G}=(G,B,\textrho)
as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf detail}(\textbf{G}):=\max\{|E(G)|,|V(G)\setminus B|\}.

We denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal B}^{(t)}
the set of all (pairwise non-isomorphic)
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t
-boundaried graphs and by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal B}_{\ell}^{(t)}
the set of all (pairwise non-isomorphic)
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t
-boundaried graphs with detail at most
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell.

We also set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal B}=\bigcup_{t\in\mathbb{N}}{\cal B}^{(t)}.

Topological minors of boundaried graphs.

We say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(M,T)
is a
tm
-pair
if
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}M
is a graph,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T\subseteq V(M),
and all vertices in

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(M)\setminus T
have degree two. We denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf diss}(M,T)
the graph obtained
from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}M
by dissolving all vertices in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(M)\setminus T.

A
tm
-pair
of a graph
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
is a
tm
-pair

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(M,T)
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}M
is a subgraph of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G.

We call the vertices in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T

branch
vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(M,T).

We need to deal with topological minors for the notion of homogeneity defined below, on which the statement of
[
7
, Theorem 5.2]
relies.

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{M}=(M,B,\textrho)\in{\cal B}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}T\subseteq V(M)
with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}B\subseteq T,
we call
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\textbf{M},T)
a
btm
-pair

and we define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf diss}(\textbf{M},T)=({\sf diss}(M,T),B,\textrho).
Note that we do not permit dissolution of boundary vertices, as we consider all of them to be branch vertices. If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{G}=(G,B,\textrho)
is a boundaried graph and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(M,T)
is a
tm
-pair of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G

where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}B\subseteq T,
then we say that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\textbf{M},T),
where
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{M}=(M,B,\textrho),
is a
btm
-pair
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{G}=(G,B,\textrho).

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{G}_{1},{\bf G}_{2}
be two boundaried graphs.
We say that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{G}_{1}
is a
topological minor

of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{G}_{2},
denoted by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{G}_{1}\preceq_{\sf tm}\textbf{G}_{2},
if

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{G}_{2}
has a
btm
-pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\textbf{M},T)

such that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf diss}(\textbf{M},T)
is isomorphic to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{G}_{1}.

Folios.

Given a
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{G}\in{\cal B}
and a positive integer
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell,
we define the
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
-folio
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\bf G}

as

{\ell}\mbox{\sf-folio}(\textbf{G})=\{\textbf{G}^{\prime}\in{\cal B}\mid\textbf{G}^{\prime}\preceq_{\sf tm}\textbf{G}\mbox{\penalty\ and $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{G}^{\prime}$ has detail at most $\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell$}\}.

The number of distinct
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell
-folios of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t
-boundaried graphs is indicated in the following result, proved first in
[
7
]
and used also in
[
8
]
.

Proposition 64
.

There exists a function
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}f_{15}:\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t,\ell\in\mathbb{N},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|\{\ell\mbox{\sf-folio}({\bf G})\mid{\bf G}\in{\cal B}_{\ell}^{(t)}\}|\leq\hyperref@@ii[label_dogmatizador]{f_{\ref*{label_dogmatizador}}}(t,\ell).
Moreover,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\hyperref@@ii[label_dogmatizador]{f_{\ref*{label_dogmatizador}}}(t,\ell)=2^{2^{{\cal O}((t+\ell)\cdot\log(t+\ell))}}.

Augmented flaps.

Let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G
be a graph,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}A
be a subset of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}V(G)
of size
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}a,
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,{\mathfrak{R}})
be a flatness pair of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus A.

For each flap
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}F\in{\sf flaps}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
we consider a labeling
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell_{F}:\partial F\rightarrow\{1,2,3\}
such that
the set of labels assigned by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell_{F}
to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\partial F
is one of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{1\},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{1,2\},

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{1,2,3\}.

Also, let
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{a}\in[a].

For every set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\tilde{A}}\in\binom{A}{\tilde{a}},
we consider a bijection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho_{\tilde{A}}:\tilde{A}\to[\tilde{a}].

The labelings in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal L}=\{\ell_{F}\mid F\in{\sf flaps}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)\}
and the labelings in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\{\textrho_{\tilde{A}}\mid\tilde{A}\in\binom{A}{\tilde{a}}\}

will be useful for defining a set of boundaried graphs that we will call augmented flaps. We first need some more definitions.

Given a flap
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}F\in{\sf flaps}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W),
we define an ordering

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Omega(F)=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{q}),
with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}q\leq 3,
of the vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\partial{F}

so that

•

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{q})
is a counter-clockwise cyclic ordering of the vertices of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\partial F
as they appear in the corresponding cell of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C(\textGamma).
Notice that this cyclic ordering is significant only when
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}|\partial F|=3,

in the sense that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
remains invariant under shifting, i.e.,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
is the same as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(x_{2},x_{3},x_{1})
but not under inversion, i.e.,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
is not the same as
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(x_{3},x_{2},x_{1}),
and

•

for
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}i\in[q],

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\ell_{F}(x_{i})=i.

Notice that the second condition is necessary for completing the definition of the ordering
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\Omega(F),

and this is the reason why we set up the labelings in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal L}.

For each set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{A}\in\binom{A}{\tilde{a}}
and each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}F\in{\sf flaps}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
with
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}t_{F}:=|\partial F|,

we fix
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textrho_{F}:\partial F\to[\tilde{a}+1,\tilde{a}+t_{F}]
such that

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\textrho^{-1}_{F}(\tilde{a}+1),\ldots,\textrho^{-1}_{F}(\tilde{a}+t_{F}))=\Omega(F).

Also, we define the boundaried graph

\textbf{F}^{\tilde{A}}:=(G[\tilde{A}\cup F],\tilde{A}\cup\partial F,\textrho_{\tilde{A}}\cup\textrho_{F})

and we denote by
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}F^{\tilde{A}}
the underlying graph of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{F}^{\tilde{A}}.
We call
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textbf{F}^{\tilde{A}}
an
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{A}
-augmented flap
of the flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus A

in
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G.

Palettes and homogeneity.

For each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\mathfrak{R}}
-normal cycle
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}C
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
and each set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{A}\in 2^{A},
we define
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{A},\ell)\mbox{\sf-palette}(C)=\{\ell\mbox{\sf-folio}({\bf F}^{\tilde{A}})\mid F\in{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}}(C)\}.

Given a set
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{A}\in 2^{A},
we say that the flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus A
is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textlambda
-homogeneous with respect to
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\tilde{A}
if every
internal
brick of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{W}
has the
same

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(\tilde{A},\textlambda)
-palette
(seen as a cycle of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\sf compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
).
Also, given a collection
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}{\cal S}\subseteq 2^{A},
we say that the flatness pair
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}(W,\mathfrak{R})
of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}G\setminus A
is
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.1,0.34}\textlambda
-homogeneous
with respect to
